## Preface.

The article concerning heavenly medicine is rich and precious, well-grounded in Holy Scripture, and extensive, encompassing also the conversion of the sinner. It is most lovely, useful, and comforting to all poor sinners who desire to be rid of their sin, even though few concern themselves with it. It shows, therefore, how the merciful, kind God deals with his elect, whom he receives into grace, whom he transfers out of Satan’s kingdom into his heavenly kingdom; how he converts them, treats the soul-sick with medicine <note>The original German uses the verb *arzeneyet*, a now-archaic term meaning to apply medical treatment or to act as a physician.</note>, heals them, and makes them healthy—all through Jesus Christ <note>original: *Jesum Christum*</note> his Son, our Lord. Thus, four main points will primarily be dealt with here in a summary <note>original: *Summa*, a Latin term for a compendium or comprehensive summary of a subject.</note>, so that what follows may be more easily understood.

<summary>This preface introduces the "Heavenly Medicine" as a biblical doctrine of conversion and spiritual healing. It outlines God's process of redeeming the elect through Christ, framing the upcoming text around four key summary points.</summary>
<keywords>Preface, Heavenly Medicine, conversion of the sinner, the elect, Satan's kingdom, Jesus Christ, spiritual healing, Caspar Schwenkfeld, Summary, Pennsylvania German theology</keywords>

## Preface.

First: All these sick ones are natural, "old-Adamic" humans <note>The term "old-Adamic" refers to the theological concept of the "Old Adam," representing human nature in its fallen, sinful state inherited from the biblical first man, Adam.</note>, none excepted, who descend from the blood and flesh of *Adam* and *Eve*.

Second: Spiritual sickness is nothing other than the evil, sinful nature which brings eternal death, under which all people are confined, so that *God* <note>original: *GOtt*</note> may have mercy on all. *Romans 11:32.*

Third: Jesus Christ <note>original: *Jesus Christus*</note> with His grace is the only physician of the sick soul—indeed, of the entire sinful human being who was corrupted through Adam’s disobedience. God, the kind and merciful Father, has appointed Him to us poor, sick people as the Restorer, Physician, and Savior, after having given Him all power and a name that is above every name, so that all who believe in Him shall not die the eternal death, but shall instead become healed, joyful, and healthy.

Fourth: The medicine <note>original: *Arzeney*</note>, with which this Physician heals our spiritual sickness and all wounded hearts and injuries and makes them healthy, is—

In every recipe for healing and medicine from our heavenly Physician, or for the salvation of the sinner, the death, martyrdom, and above all the blood-shedding of Christ must be used <note>The author uses the metaphor of a pharmaceutical recipe, where Christ's sacrifice is the primary ingredient for spiritual restoration.</note>. Since, however, a proper medical practice and physician also require a pharmacist and a pharmacy, the Holy Spirit shall be our pharmacist in these spiritual medicines; He is the one who prepares and distributes the medicine for our souls in cooperation with the Physician. 

The pharmacy, where one is accustomed to fetching medicine, shall here be the grace of **God** <note>original: *Gottes*</note> on the heavenly throne. There, we are to collect this spiritual medicine by lifting up our hearts; we must come to the Physician himself, receive the medicine from him through the Holy Spirit, and carry it home—that is, into our hearts—so that it may bring about our health.

Since every physician has his medical practice <term>Practica: Referring to the practical application of medical knowledge or a specific clinical method.</term>, the practice of this heavenly Physician is the conversion of the sinner, the forgiveness of sins, the receiving of the Holy Spirit, justification <note>Gerechtmachung: A theological term for being made righteous in the eyes of God.</note>, and sanctification; which sanctification the Holy Spirit through Paul always tells us to pursue. And just as physical doctors provide everyone they heal with a rule for preserving health—prescribing how one should maintain a good diet in eating and drinking and other matters—so our Physician Jesus Christ has established for us in the Gospel and through His apostles a "rule of health": that we should sin no more, but live godly lives and keep God’s commandments.

# Preface.

Thus we see now, in summary <note>original: "in einer Summa"</note>, this entire divine dealing <note>Handel: Here meaning a transaction or sacred business between God and humanity.</note> concerning the heavenly medicine; concerning our Physician Jesus Christ and His patients, whom He makes well, along with other related matters which shall be treated more extensively hereafter through several chapters. May the almighty, merciful God grant to us poor, sick sinners that we also hold faithfully to the Physician Christ, and become partakers of His healing unto eternal life.

### Concerning the
# Heavenly Medicine.

## The First Chapter.

### That Jesus Christ is the true heavenly Physician, and poor, sinful man is the patient, the wounded, or the sick.

When our dear Lord and Master, Jesus Christ, called Matthew Levi the tax collector <note>original: "Zöllner," referring to a publican or tax gatherer for the Roman Empire, often despised in biblical times</note> to be his disciple, apostle, and evangelist, the latter prepared a great feast for the Lord in his house, and many tax collectors and sinners sat at the table with him and his disciples. When the scribes and Pharisees saw this, they grumbled and said to his disciples: "Why does your Master eat with the tax collectors and sinners?" When Jesus heard this, he said to them: "Those who are healthy do not need the physician, but those who are sick; but go and learn what this means: I have pleasure in mercy <note>original: "Barmherzigkeit"</note> and not in sacrifice. I have come to call sinners to repentance <note>original: "Buße"</note>, and not the righteous." Luke 5:31–32; Matthew 9:13.

Among other useful points which the Lord Jesus teaches and presents to us in this holy Gospel <note>original: "Evangelio"</note>, there is firstly depicted and shown the corruption, the innate sickness, and the poverty of human nature, alongside the grace of Almighty God, the restoration, and the gracious office <note>Amt: Here meaning the specific duty or role assigned to Christ by God</note> of His Son Jesus Christ, which He performs for mankind, so that after the

<summary>This page introduces the first chapter, establishing the biblical foundation for Christ as the 'Heavenly Physician' through the story of the calling of Matthew the tax collector. It frames human sinfulness as an innate sickness that requires divine medical intervention.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Physician, Matthew Levi, tax collectors, sinners, mercy, repentance, original sin, spiritual sickness, Gospel of Luke, Gospel of Matthew</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 1, completing the description of human corruption and introducing the central theme of Christ as the divinely appointed physician for the soul's sickness.</meta>

human being before God in soul, body, and all his powers, through Adam’s Fall <note>Referring to the biblical narrative of the first sin in the Garden of Eden</note> and through disobedience and sin, has become completely corrupted, sick, frail, and a poor miserable sinner, in dire need of all mercy, grace, and help; [and] that the Lord Jesus, by contrast, is the true Savior <note>original: "Heyland"</note>, the only healer, the physician of humanity, helper, and redeemer.

That the almighty heavenly Father, after endowing him with all authority and the name that is above all names, set him before all people as Physician and Savior, and commanded him to make them completely healthy; that is, He has given him to us as a Bringer of Salvation <note>original: "Seeligmacher," literally one who makes others blessed or saved</note>. Therefore, all such sick persons should seek no other physician, but hasten to Jesus Christ and hold fast to his help and counsel alone. For such authority and office <note>Amt: Here meaning the specific divine duty or role assigned to Christ by God</note> of healing <note>original: "Artzneyens," referring to the practice or administration of medicine</note> is given to and belongs to no one else but him; anyone else who presumes to heal souls only kills them, ruins them utterly, and leads them into Hell.

Above all things, this should be diligently learned, well-considered, rightly recognized, understood, and internalized <note>original: "eingebildet," in this 16th/17th-century context meaning to imprint upon the mind or heart</note> within the Christian faith, so that we may truly know what we are by nature, how Christ the Son of God benefits us, and why he came in the flesh; we must know what his grace, power, effect, and office can achieve in us, and how and through what he was presented and given to us by God his Father as the true heavenly physician of our souls for all their flaws, damages, and infirmities. He illustrates this with such fine and lovely parables here and throughout the whole Gospel <note>original: "Evangelio"</note> that even the heart of a troubled, poor sinner who considers it and believes the Lord's word will overflow with joy; for the word of the Lord is true, and what he promises, he surely keeps. Psalm 33:4.

Therefore, by the grace of God, for the praise of Christ our Lord and for the benefit of all sick, broken consciences and all poor sinners, I have resolved to write something comforting about this Physician and his heavenly medicine (as well as about the sickness), so that we may be exhorted to better recognize the deadly hereditary disease <note>original: "Erbsucht," a term framing Original Sin as an inherited medical condition</note> which is born in us from Adam, and to seek and desire such a heavenly Physician all the more. May we also, through faith, more diligently accept his help, medicine, and healing, and yield ourselves quite obediently to his treatment <note>original: "Kur," meaning a medical cure or prescribed course of treatment</note>.

# Chapter 2.

### Concerning the Visitation of the Sick, and how Christ has proved Himself to be an eternal Physician of Souls through many kinds of benefits and miraculous signs.

---

In the same way that a faithful personal physician attends to sick or wounded people, comforts them, exhorts them, employs all diligence to help them, eats and drinks with them, and devises all manner of things that might serve or be useful for their recovery, delight, and for the easing of the spirit; he does not even shrink from bearing the sick person's foul smell and uncleanness, so that they might only be helped—as no physician can be entirely without such things: So also has our faithful Physician of Souls, the heavenly spiritual physician Jesus Christ, done; he has faithfully taken upon himself the corruption of the sick, wounded human being and our misery with all diligence. He did not shrink from nor was he ashamed of the company, love, and fellowship of poor sinners—though they be penitent sinners—for whose sake he left heaven, his honor, and glory, and came into mud and filth, indeed, into agony, suffering, and death; so that he even took human nature upon himself to heal the sick, weak human being.

This serves as comfort and good assurance for our timid <note>original: "blöden," meaning weak or faint-hearted rather than unintelligent</note>, weak conscience, so that we should expect his office, help, and spiritual medicine without ceasing. The kind Lord Jesus Christ illustrated, explained, and proved this early in the beginning with many visible miracles performed externally upon people: when he made those who were physically ill healthy, the deaf to hear, the blind to see, the lame to walk straight, the lepers to be clean, the possessed to be free, and the dead to live, as the Evangelist Matthew writes in Chapter 4, 24. He healed all manner of plagues and sickness among the people, thereby indicating that after the completion of his suffering and after the merciful opening of the heavenly apothecary of grace <note>A common Baroque metaphor comparing the Church or Christ’s merit to a pharmacy where spiritual cures are dispensed</note>, he also intended to practice, work, and graciously apply such things spiritually to the sick inner man through the Holy Spirit.

For in truth, all flesh—indeed, all people—are fashioned no differently inwardly before God than were the sick, the frail, and the moonstruck <note>original: "Mondsüchtigen," referring to those afflicted by "lunacy" or epilepsy, a term used in contemporary translations of Matthew 4:24</note> outwardly, whom the Lord there at the...

<summary>This page compares Christ to a faithful physician who endures the unpleasantness of a patient to bring healing, emphasizing that His physical miracles were signs of the spiritual healing He offers through the 'apothecary of grace.'</summary>

<keywords>Physician of Souls, miracles of Christ, spiritual healing, Matthew 4:24, apothecary of grace, mercy, human nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument from the previous page, detailing the extent of human corruption following the Fall of Man and establishing Christ as the sole authorized "Physician" capable of healing the soul’s "hereditary disease."</meta>

...humanity before God in soul, body, and all its powers has been entirely corrupted, sickened, and weakened through Adam’s fall, by means of disobedience and sin, becoming poor, miserable sinners in need of all mercy, grace, and help; and that, in contrast, the Lord Jesus is the true Savior <note>original: "Heyland"</note>, the only healer, and the Physician, Helper, and Redeemer of humanity.

Because the almighty heavenly Father has endowed Him with all power and the name that is above all names, He has appointed Him as the Physician and Savior for all people and charged Him with making them completely healthy—that is, He has given Him to us as a Savior <note>original: "Seeligmacher," literally one who makes blessed or saves</note>. Therefore, all such sick people should seek no other physician, but rather hasten to Jesus Christ and rely alone on His help and counsel. For such power and the office of dispensing medicine is neither given to nor belongs to anyone else but Him; whoever else presumes to heal souls only kills them, ruins them further to the core, and leads them into hell.

This should be diligently learned above all things in the Christian faith, well-considered, rightly recognized, understood, and internalized <note>original: "eingebildet," here meaning to be deeply impressed upon the mind or spirit</note>, so that we may truly know what we are by nature, how Christ the Son of God benefits us, and why He came into the flesh. We must understand what His grace, power, effect, and office can achieve in us, and how and why He was presented and given to us by God His Father as the true heavenly Physician of our souls for all their defects, damages, and infirmities. He illustrates this with such fine, lovely parables here and throughout the entire Gospel, so that even a troubled, poor sinner who ponders it and believes the Lord's word will have their heart overflow with joy; for the word of the Lord is true, and what He promises, He certainly keeps. Psalm 33:4.

Therefore, by the grace of God, for the praise of Christ our Lord and for the benefit of all sick, broken consciences and all poor sinners, I have undertaken to write something comforting about this Physician and His heavenly medicine (as well as about the sickness). This is done so that we may be exhorted to recognize more closely the deadly hereditary disease <note>original: "Erbsucht," a term for Original Sin, framed here as a chronic or inherited ailment</note> born into us in Adam, and to seek and desire this heavenly Physician more—that we may more diligently perceive His help, medicine, and healing through faith, and surrender ourselves quite obediently under His care <note>original: "Kur," meaning a medical treatment or cure</note>.

# Chapter 2.

### Concerning the Visitation of the Sick, and how Christ has proven Himself an eternal Physician of Souls through various benefits and miraculous signs.

---

In the same way, however, as a faithful physician of the body <note>original: "Leibarzt"</note> attends to sick or wounded people, comforts them, exhorts them, uses all diligence to help them, eats and drinks with them, and devises all sorts of things that might serve or be useful for their recovery, delight, and the easing of their spirits; he also does not shy away from bearing the patient's foul smell and uncleanness, so that they might only be helped; for no physician can be entirely without such things: So also has our faithful Physician of Souls, the heavenly spiritual Physician Jesus Christ, done, in that He has faithfully and with all diligence taken upon Himself the corruption of sick, wounded humanity and our misery. He did not shy away from nor was He ashamed of the company, love, and fellowship of poor sinners—provided they were repentant sinners—for whose sake He left heaven, His honor, and His glory, and came into the mud and filth, yes, into agony, suffering, and death; so that, to heal sick and weak humanity, He even took human nature upon Himself.

This serves for the comfort and good assurance of our weak and timid <note>original: "blöden," which in this period meant faint-hearted or infirm rather than "stupid"</note> conscience, so that we should constantly expect His office, help, and spiritual medicine. The kind Lord Jesus Christ prefigured, explained, and proved this early on with many visible miracles performed outwardly upon people: when He made those who were physically ill healthy, the deaf hearing, the blind seeing, the lame straight, the lepers clean, the possessed free, and the dead alive, as the Evangelist Matthew writes in Chapter 4, verse 24. He healed all kinds of plagues and diseases among the people to show that, after the completion of His suffering and after the merciful opening of the heavenly pharmacy of grace <note>original: "Apotheke der Gnaden," a common Baroque metaphor depicting Christ as a divine apothecary providing remedies for sin</note>, He also intended to spiritually exercise, work, and graciously apply all these things to the sick inner person through the Holy Spirit.

For in truth, all flesh—indeed, all people—are inwardly fashioned before God no differently than the sick, the infirm, and the lunatics <note>original: "Mondsüchtigen," literally "moon-sick," historically referring to those with epilepsy or mental distress</note> were outwardly, whom the Lord there at the...

<meta>This page continues the theological argument from the previous page, where Christ was introduced as the Physician of Souls. It moves from the physical healing of the body to the spiritual healing of the soul, using the parable of the Good Samaritan and Christ’s own interactions with sinners as primary evidence of His "medical" mission.</meta>

...healed, improved, and cured in the body: namely, those who were dead in sins, leprous, possessed by the evil spirit, blind, deaf to God’s inspiration <note>original: "Einsprechen GOttes"—referring to the internal call or word of God entering the heart</note>, mute and stinking in the eyes of God; that is, frail, damned, miserable, and full of sin. Such internal damage and corruption of the flesh is first revealed and shown to us by the Lord Christ through the sick, so that He also, as previously heard, wished to treat man spiritually, heal him internally, and make him healthy, just as He did before with external physical illness.

The eternal Word of God, Jesus Christ, became man and died for this reason: that He might awaken man, who was dead before God, from eternal death, free him from the rule of the devil, and cleanse him from the leprosy of sin. He wished to enlighten the eyes of his heart, open the ears of his understanding, endow him with divine righteousness, wisdom, and truth, gladden his conscience, acquire the heavenly kingdom, and make man capable, alert, and ready for all good works for the praise of God. What else does the parable in Luke, concerning the one who fell among murderers and was wounded, signify? <note>Luke 10:30–37, the Parable of the Good Samaritan</note> Nothing else than that the poor sick person should find total spiritual comfort in the Lord Christ—the true Physician of Souls, the new and skillful Master who brings a new medicine for all sin from heaven—regarding everything that the man there experienced physically from the Samaritan, and should always expect His healing with true faith.

In all of this, He has also proven His faithfulness, love, and mercy in many ways through gracious and humble fellowship with poor sick sinners and simple, poor people; for He kept company with them, taught them, walked with them, ate and drank, as we have just heard at the beginning of the Gospel. Therefore, the Son of God had to risk His entire reputation <note>original: "all sein gut Gerücht in die Schanz schlagen"—an idiomatic expression meaning to gamble or put one's good name at stake</note> and, as the faithful Physician, bear the sinners' bad taste, stench, and horror—yes, their shame, disgrace, and sin—so that He was held and condemned by the Pharisees and Jews as their companion and as a sinner, a Samaritan, a companion of the devil, and indeed a wicked, sinful man. John 8:48. (O, the great love of Christ Jesus toward us poor sinners!) Oh, that we might finally be found truly grateful to Him and His heavenly Father for this with all our hearts.

# Chapter 3.

### Concerning the Office of this Physician, and how the illness of the sick whom he is to heal must first be rightly recognized.

As the Gospel reports: After the Pharisees <note>a strict Jewish sect known for their rigorous adherence to traditional laws</note> and scribes, the high priests and leaders of the people saw that the Lord kept company with such despised people, namely with tax collectors <note>original: "Zöllnern"—in this era, local officials who collected taxes for Rome and were often hated as traitors or thieves</note>, and even with notorious women and open sinners, and that he allowed himself to be invited and made himself common with them, they murmured hostilely and took offense at it. They belittled the Lord for this and said: Behold, how this man is a glutton and a wine-drinker, a companion of tax collectors and sinners, Matthew 11:19. It was as if they wanted to say: If he were such a holy man as people praise him to be, a true prophet—indeed, if he were the true Messiah <note>the promised Savior of Israel</note> for whom he wishes to be regarded—he would certainly behave more honorably; he would not have so much fellowship with such lowly people.

The Lord answered them quite wisely and modestly when he said: A physician is not for the healthy and strong, for they do not need him, but only for the weak and sick. By this, he first wanted to indicate that he is a physician who has come solely for the sake of the sick, and should only associate with the sick, having to do with humbled, needy people. To further explain what kind of physician he is (namely the **Physician of Souls**, the eternal and heavenly physician of all people), he says further: I have come to call sinners (these are the sick and patients of this physician) to repentance <note>original: "Buße"—a turning away from sin and a change of heart toward God</note> and not the righteous.

With this, he gives the Pharisees enough to understand how the very thing for which they reproached and belittled him—having fellowship with sinners, joining them for their conversion, and letting himself be invited by them—is his true office and proper work of grace <note>original: "Gnadenwerk"—an act performed out of divine mercy rather than obligation</note>. Understand, however, such sinners who accept the physician, seek health from him, and indeed desire from their hearts to become pious through him; who henceforth love righteousness, put away all luxury, and constantly strive after a chaste, godly, healthy, new life.

These, then, are the true "Gospel sick," who are called by Christ out of the world, made healthy by him, and belong in the assembly of the Church of Christ, for whose sake also this physician has come from heaven into the world: For for this reason, he is as

<summary>This chapter explains that Christ’s primary mission is as a "Physician of Souls" who specifically seeks out those who recognize their spiritual "sickness" (sin). It refutes the criticism of the Pharisees by arguing that a doctor's place is among the ill, not the healthy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 3 regarding Christ’s "medical" office. the Son of God became human so that He might call sinful people to repentance <note>original: "Buße"—referring to a transformative change of heart and mind</note>, convert them, make them godly, and allow Himself to be invited by them (even into their very hearts). For this reason, He, as the Word of God, became flesh, so that the flesh which was sick, wounded, and corrupted in Adam <note>the biblical first man, representing fallen humanity and the origin of sin</note>—indeed, the entirely decayed and impure soul, heart, and conscience—might be restored, treated with medicine, renewed, and made healthy through Him. A Savior will come to Zion, and to those who turn from their sins (says the Lord), and I will make such a covenant with them. Isaiah 59:20–21.

Alongside this, the Lord Christ points out to the Pharisees <note>a Jewish sect known for strict, often external, adherence to religious law</note> their proud heart, which they themselves do not recognize, when He says: I have not come for the sake of the righteous—that is, for the sake of those who presumptuously exalt themselves in their own righteousness, judging and condemning others while wishing to be seen and highly esteemed by men. We have a very fine example of this in the Pharisee mentioned in Luke 18:11–14. For the sake of such people, I have not come (says the Lord), for they do not require medicine—that is, they do not need My help and kindness. Why? They are their own physicians; they know of other pharmacies <note>original: "Apotheken"—referring here to self-prescribed spiritual remedies or moral justifications</note>; even if some illness were to be noticed in them, they have never, in their own eyes, become poor, small, miserable, or sick. Instead, I have come as a physician for the benefit of those who humble themselves, who feel their sickness, who know the corrupted nature of their flesh, recognize their condemnation and misery, and have regret and sorrow over their sins; those who also heartily desire health, grace, and godliness from God.

These, then, along with Zacchaeus <note>a wealthy tax collector who repented after meeting Jesus; see Luke 19</note>, are the true children of Abraham. They are the children to whom the Kingdom of God is promised, and to whom help, comfort, and salvation shall happen. As the Lord Christ says: When the "holy" Jews (as they regarded themselves, though they were full of all malice) also murmured because He went to stay with Zacchaeus as if with an open sinner, He answered them and said: The Son of Man is come to seek and to save that which was lost. This is as much as saying that the heavenly physician Jesus Christ has come to visit, to treat, and to make healthy the poor sinner who was sick, corrupted, and lost before God—indeed, entirely alienated from the life of God, dead, and under the power of the evil spirit and eternal death.

We read of something similar in the Gospel described by John, where the Lord made a man born blind to see, saying: I am come into this world for judgment, so that those who do not see might become seeing, and those who see might become blind. Those who do not see are all the humble people who recognize their sin (from which the blindness of the heart comes) and take refuge with true faith in Christ, the Eye-Physician <note>original: "Augenarzt"—a metaphor for Christ as the one who restores spiritual sight</note> and the enlightener of all hearts, by whom they are also enlightened. ...of whom the Lord speaks here, are the haughty, blind Pharisees <note>a religious group often criticized in the New Testament for focusing on outward ritual over inner transformation</note>, who want to see and understand everything: yet they neither see nor know themselves and their own evil, wicked, envious hearts; therefore the sickness of their souls, which is their sin, must remain upon their necks.

Our heavenly physician Jesus Christ intended the same in the aforementioned Gospel <note>original: "Evangelio"—the biblical accounts of Jesus’s life</note>, where He says: Those who are healthy do not need a physician, but those who are sick; and I have come to call sinners to repentance <note>original: "Buße"—a change of mind or heart</note>, and not the righteous; as if He meant to say: A physician from heaven has come into the world for a miraculous judgment, so that through Him the sick might become healthy, and the healthy might become sick; that the poor in God become rich, and the rich become poor; that sinners become righteous, and the righteous become sinners—that is, be eternally lost. This is indeed very strange, and seems impossible, even too lofty, for the old, wanton flesh and carnal mind; but whoever turns to the Lord, to whom He gives His Spirit—the Spirit of humility <note>original: "Demuth"</note>, of the love of God, and the recognition of his own illness—he will be able to grasp it, and will receive this physician with heartfelt joy, as he also learns to recognize his innate sickness through the Spirit of His grace.

# Chapter 4.

### Concerning the Faithfulness, Love, and Mercy of the Heavenly Physician, to awaken Comfort, Faith, and Hope in Him for all the Sick.

Now I wish to further boast of, praise, and—according to my poor ability—further write about the faithfulness and love of our most gracious physician Jesus Christ, to see if, among so many hundreds of thousands of sick people, someone might seriously think of Him, entrust themselves entirely to His cure or physician’s care, trust Him with their illness, or rather seek true health through Him, hope in Him, and find it in Him; for before He would let the sick person, wounded unto death, perish and die the eternal death, He, the Physician Himself, was wounded and beaten for man, and became so sick that from the sole of His foot to the top of His head, there was nothing healthy in Him; indeed, not only did He become so sick, but He also died, and died a painful, shameful death, namely on the gallows of the cross <note>original: "Galgen des Kreuzes"—using the term "gallows" emphasizes the social stigma and criminal nature of the execution in the eyes of the law</note>, so that the sick, wretched person would be healed through His wounds, blood, and pain; so that he might become healthy through the Physician’s sickness, and living through His bitter death, and come to honor through His shame: becoming holy, healthy in God, and blessed.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 4, elaborating on the character of Christ as the "Heavenly Physician." In this the love of God was manifested among us, that God sent his only begotten Son into the world, so that we might live through him, etc. And we have seen and testify that the Father sent the Son as the Savior—that is, as the helper, physician, and healer <note>original: "Gesundmacher"</note> of the world, 1 John 4:9, 14 and 1 John 2:1, 2. My little children, I write these things to you so that you do not sin (which is the sickness) and if anyone sins, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ, who is righteous, and he himself is the atonement (yes, the true medicine <note>original: "Arzeney"</note>) for our sins. Not only for ours, however, but also for those of the whole world; all of which praises and makes known the faithfulness, love, and mercy of the heavenly physician Jesus Christ to all sick people—that is, all poor sinners—for their comfort.

Saint Paul bows his knees before the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, and prays for the Ephesians, that he might give them, according to the riches of his glory, to be strengthened with power through his Spirit in the inner man, and that Christ may dwell in their hearts through faith. Finally, that they may recognize the inexpressible love of Christ, which surpasses all knowledge, so that they may be filled with the fullness of God; when he says that the love of Christ surpasses all knowledge, he wants to indicate how Christ has loved us so heartily and fervently, and still loves us, such that no person, without the special grace and revelation of God, may either fathom or recognize it.

Concerning this, our physician and Lord Jesus Christ himself says in the Gospel <note>original: "Evangelio"—the biblical accounts of Jesus’s life</note>, when he speaks to his disciples, saying: Just as my Father has loved me, so have I also loved you. No one has greater love than this, that he lays down his life for his friends. Which the Lord Jesus also did, as he speaks elsewhere: The Son of Man did not come that he might be served, but that he might serve, and give his life as a ransom for many, Matthew 20:28. And again: I am the good shepherd; a good shepherd lays down his life for the sheep, John 10:12 <note>This citation refers to the passage where Jesus contrasts the shepherd with the hireling</note> and verse 15. I lay down my life for the sheep.

Just as Christ is the only good shepherd who lays down his life for the sheep, with whom they find pasture and have full abundance—just as he is also the merciful, faithful High Priest and Bishop of our souls, who himself was first tempted in all things yet without sin, so that he could have compassion for our weakness and help those who are tempted; so also is he the only faithful physician, who distributes and mildly <note>In the sense of "generously" or "graciously"</note> gives up his soul, body, flesh, blood, and everything he has and is for the healing <note>original: "Gesundmachung"</note> of many sick, poor sinners, so that they, released from their sin and sickness, may have salvation, peace, bliss, and joy in him.

# Chapter 5.

### How the heavenly Physician was made sick, wounded, and killed for the sick themselves, so that poor humanity would become alive, refreshed, and healthy through him.

Certainly, no other physician on earth performs such a precious exchange, no matter what his name may be; none is so faithful that he would himself become sick and wounded for his patient, let alone die for him. Yet this is exactly what Christ Jesus, our lovely and pious Physician, has most willingly endured. Indeed, he has done this for the benefit of wicked, unfaithful humanity, in that he has gifted them eternal health and, in exchange, has carried their painful sickness, sin, and misery. In short, this heavenly Physician has taken all the damage of the sick person entirely upon himself, as it is written of him in the Book of Psalms, where he speaks of his suffering: "I must pay for what I did not steal; and the insults of those who insult you (O God the Father) fall on me; and they give me gall to eat, and in my great thirst, vinegar to drink." Psalm 69:22 <note>The author uses Psalm 69 as a "Messianic" prophecy, interpreting the speaker's suffering as a foreshadowing of Christ's crucifixion.</note>.

The man Adam robbed God of his honor in that he wished to be equal to Him; through this, he and all his descendants fell into a heavy, deadly sickness. For this, Christ had to suffer and take all such things upon himself. Following this, it says further in the Psalm: "The wretched (that is, the poor sinners who rightly recognize their sickness) shall see this (namely the satisfaction <note>original: "Genugthuung" — a theological term meaning the payment of a debt or atonement</note> and healing medicine <note>original: "Arzeney"</note> of Christ's suffering for their sin), and those who rejoice and seek the Lord (this heavenly Physician), their hearts shall live," says the Prophet; that is, they shall become comforted, joyful, and healthy. Psalm 69:33.

Isaiah prophesied most comfortingly of all this long ago, when he said: "Surely he bore our sickness and took our pains upon himself; yet we considered him to be the one struck and martyred by God." That is to say, we poor sick people could not recognize the great faithfulness of this heavenly Physician; we did not think that he wanted to help us, but rather that he himself was to blame for his suffering—just as all those still do today who are found to be ungrateful for the suffering of Christ.

But he was wounded for the sake of our misdeeds and crushed for the sake of our sins (that is, for the sake of our sickness he became sick; for the sake of our sin he was on the Cross...

See, kind-hearted person, this is our God, Lord, and Master Jesus Christ, through whose knowledge alone we must become pious, healthy, blessed, and righteous. Oh, how noble and worthy is the true knowledge of this heavenly physician Jesus Christ, which is also the only art in which all people should study their whole lives to become learned, rich, and complete; for his knowledge, as he himself says, brings eternal life, through which we also become partakers of the divine nature.

# Chapter 6.

### The Holy Spirit's comforting invitation through Paul, for all who are sick, to the heavenly physician Jesus Christ.

However, so that we may also boast all the more joyfully of the love of God the Father toward us poor sinners (in the giving of his own Son, the heavenly physician Jesus Christ, for the sick), let us consider here what Paul says to the Romans, speaking thus: Romans 5:6, etc. For when we were still weak, according to the time, Christ died for us ungodly people. Now, someone will hardly die for the sake of the law <note>The German "Recht" here refers to a "righteous person," following the traditional translation of Romans 5:7.</note>; for the sake of something good, someone might perhaps dare to die. Therefore, God praises his love toward us, in that Christ died for us while we were still sinners. How much more, then, shall we now be saved from wrath through him, having been made righteous through his blood. For if we were reconciled to God through the death of his Son while we were still enemies, how much more shall we be saved by his life, now that we are reconciled. But not only that, we also boast in God through our Lord Jesus Christ, through whom we have now received reconciliation.

This is indeed a most comforting passage for us poor sick people, an invitation from the Holy Spirit through the ministry of Paul, in which the entire office of the heavenly physician, Christ, is contained, praised, and revealed. These words should rightly be read and explained to all Christian-believing, dying people on their deathbeds to exercise their faith in Christ, and thereby point them toward Christ; for what could the exceeding...

<meta>This page continues the exposition of Romans 5, expanding on the metaphor of Christ as the "Heavenly Physician." It transitions from describing the legal state of the sinner to the profound emotional and spiritual comfort found in Christ's willingness to suffer for his enemies.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

How could we ever more highly praise, explain, and make known the Love and Mercy of GOD toward us poor sinners than through the harsh, bitter suffering of His Son for us? What could better uplift and comfort our faith and our weak consciences in Christ than when we consider that Christ, the beloved Son of GOD, our Physician and Lord, sacrificed Himself for unjust and godless people—for His own and His Father's enemies—and willingly gave Himself over to a bitter death? He has become our ransom and our salvation. Dear reader, where else can one find a physician who treats his enemy for free <note>original: "umsonst arzeneye." The author uses "physician" as a verb here, meaning to provide medical care or apply remedies.</note>, let alone one who would put his own health at risk or take on a boil or a wound for his patient’s sake? As for being willing to die, we shall remain silent on that. Where in the history of the entire world has it ever been heard that someone died for the sake of the unjust? Otherwise, a good friend might perhaps die for another. Many also die for the sake of the common good, and some, as we read in the histories, have been allowed to die for the sake of justice and what is good.

But Christ Jesus, the faithful, loving, and pious Lord, died for the sake of the unjust person; for He died for us while we were still unjust, while we were still weak, while we were still sinners, while we were still godless—yes, while we were still His enemies. How much more will He now (after the completion of all His torment and after He has shed His blood for us), now that He has also risen again, and rules and lives, heal us and make us blessed through that very life of His? For if the death of Christ achieved so much that we are reconciled to GOD through it, how much more can His divine, heavenly life achieve, in which the God-man Jesus Christ rules and reigns in and with GOD? He does not only intercede for us against the wrath of GOD; He does not only heal our wounds, but He gives and grants us the Holy Spirit, the Spirit of adoption <term>Geist der Kindschaft: A biblical term (Romans 8:15) referring to the status of being a child of God, rather than a slave or stranger.</term> and of the love of GOD. In this Spirit, we may boast of, rejoice in, and accept as our own property and comfort the Kingdom of Heaven and all its heavenly goods through Him.

This is what Saint Paul, in the place mentioned before, wished to proclaim to us poor, sick patients and needy people for our everlasting comfort. Why then would we not take comfort in all that is good, in every recovery and healing (regardless of every kind of deadly ulcer <note>original: "tödtlicher Geschwür." Used here metaphorically to represent the corruption of sin.</note>, sin, and sickness) from this heavenly Physician? Why would we not gladly have such a precious Physician around us, love Him, and follow His counsel? Indeed, who would not sing the loveliest song of praise with the pious David, if they have only begun to recognize this Physician even a little: "Oh, give thanks to the Lord, for He is friendly, and His mercy endures forever. Let those give thanks who are redeemed by the Lord, whom He has redeemed from distress; those who wandered in the desert, in unpaved—"

## The 7th Chapter.

### How our heavenly Physician, Christ, posts letters of testimonial and also invites all the sick to Himself.

To the eternal praise of our heavenly Physician, Jesus Christ, I wish to recount here another small passage from Isaiah; these shall be the letters of testimonial and witness <note>original: "Kundschaft- und Zeugniß-Briefe." In the early modern period, traveling physicians would post printed notices or testimonials in public squares to prove their credentials and successful cures.</note> which He—as other unknown physicians also do—publicly announces and posts throughout the whole world, where He speaks thus: "The Spirit of the Lord is with me, therefore the Lord has anointed me; He has sent me to preach good tidings to the wretched (that is, the poor in spirit), to bind up the broken hearts (that is our Physician's own office), to proclaim a release to the captives (namely, from the law of death and sin), and an opening of the prison to those who are bound (by Satan); to proclaim an acceptable year, where pure health, peace, and joy shall reign—the true, grace-filled Jubilee <term>Jubilee: derived from the biblical "Year of Jubilee" (Leviticus 25), a year of total restoration, release from debt, and freedom.</term> of the Lord—and a day of vengeance of our God over all enemies of this Physician and of true health or godliness." 

Now the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ speaks further: "God has sent me to comfort all who mourn, to provide for the mourners in Zion (where this King and Physician is accepted), that they be given an ornament for ashes, and the oil of joy for mourning, and beautiful garments for a troubled spirit (or for their heavy heart); that they be called trees of righteousness, plants of the Lord for His praise." Isaiah 61:1–3.

See, there you have the comforting passports <note>original: "Passporte." Here meaning official documents or certificates of safe-conduct that prove one's authority and skill.</note> of our heavenly Physician Jesus Christ’s art, ability, and medicines—His public notice and testimony—just as it was prophesied long ago in the Prophet by the Holy Spirit and described for our benefit. The Lord Himself opened this, read it, and by divine command applied it to Himself at the beginning of His ministry in the school at Nazareth...

# The 7th Chapter.

### How our heavenly Physician Christ posts His testimonials, and also invites all the sick to Himself.

To the eternal praise of our heavenly Physician Jesus Christ, I wish to recount here another small passage from Isaiah, and these shall be the letters of testimonial and witness <term>testimonial (Kundschafts- und Zeugniß-Briefe): in the early modern period, these were official documents or posters displayed by traveling craftsmen and doctors to prove their qualifications and successful cures</term>, which He, just as other unknown physicians do, publicly proclaims and posts throughout the whole world, where He speaks thus: The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because the Lord has anointed me, He has sent me to preach good tidings to the wretched (that is, the poor in spirit), to bind up the broken hearts (that is our Physician's own office), to proclaim liberty to the captives (namely from the law of death and sin), and an opening to those who are bound (by Satan), to proclaim an acceptable year, in which pure health, peace, and joy shall reign—the true, grace-filled Jubilee <note>original: "Jubeljahr"; a reference to the biblical year of Jubilee where debts were forgiven and slaves freed</note> of the Lord—and a day of vengeance of our God upon all enemies of this Physician and of true health or godliness. Now the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ speaks further: God has sent me to comfort all who mourn, to provide for those who mourn in Zion (where one accepts this King and Physician), so that they may be given ornament for ashes, and oil of joy for mourning, and beautiful garments for a sorrowful spirit (or for their heavy heart); that they might be called trees of righteousness, plants of the Lord for His praise. Isaiah 61:1-3.

## The 8th Chapter.

### How the sickness of the soul is such a great and heavy evil, and why the grace-filled, comforting healing of the Physician Jesus Christ is much more to be extolled, praised, and loved.

That the sickness of the soul is a destructive and heavy evil, where no one can offer counsel or help except for the Physician Jesus Christ our Lord alone, God has also shown us through the Prophet; for thus says the Lord: *Jeremiah 30:12, 13, 17. Your injury is desperately wicked, and your wounds are incurable. No one pleads your cause, that He may bind it up; no one can heal you, etc. But I will make you healthy again and heal your wounds, says the Lord.* <note>The author is quoting the Prophet Jeremiah to emphasize that human spiritual suffering is beyond earthly repair.</note> And *Psalm 147:3: The Lord heals those who are of a broken heart, and binds up their pains; and whoever trusts (or believes) in the Lord shall become healthy.*

Since the size and danger of a physical plague or illness—as well as the skillful restoration to health—contributes not a little to the praise of a good physician when a person is helped: then above all things, the inexpressible, harmful evil of the spiritual sickness of our poor souls must also be well recognized and considered by us. For just as the soul is much nobler than the body, so without doubt the sickness of the soul is far heavier, more dangerous, and more unbearable than all the various plagues of the entire body that could ever be imagined. Therefore, the **Physician of the Soul** <term>Seelenarzt: a common Baroque-era metaphor for Jesus Christ, focusing on His role in curing spiritual and moral failings rather than physical ailments.</term> is, in His station, much more precious, comforting, and more to be praised than all the physical physicians on earth.

<summary>This page defines soul-sickness as a condition more severe than any physical ailment and establishes Jesus Christ as the only Physician capable of healing such spiritual wounds.</summary>
<keywords>soul-sickness, Jesus Christ, Physician of the Soul, Jeremiah, Psalms, spiritual healing, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the role of a physical doctor and the spiritual role of Christ. The previous page established that the sickness of the soul is a "desperately wicked" injury that only the Physician Jesus Christ can heal.</meta>

And since it is evident how proven personal physicians <note>original: "Leib-Aerzte"; doctors of the body</note> appear comfortingly to the sick, natural man (who otherwise knows no help) in times of severe illness or even in the throes of death—how the sick send for them from afar, spare no expense, rejoice in their future arrival, are comforted in their hearts by the same, always ask for their presence, and often draw a hope or a strength of health simply from their arrival, their face, and their words: So, through such a prefiguration <note>original: "Fürbildung"; a person or event that foreshadows something greater</note>, the excellent dignity, divine power, and loveliness of the heavenly Physician of souls can be all the more easily perceived and recognized by all believers. For as much as the sickness of the soul is heavier, deadlier, and more intolerable than the sickness of the body, so much more must those people who feel it rejoice that they know this skillful Physician Jesus Christ, that they can reach Him and bring Him to themselves—especially when they know that He has already stepped before the door, knocks, and waits, if only the sick person will let Him in to help him. <note>original: "Apoc. 3, 20"; Revelation 3:20: "Behold, I stand at the door and knock."</note>

O, what a faithful, lovely Physician is Jesus Christ! Who stretches out His hand daily to rescue us from all kinds of sickness and distress, and to deliver and release us from all evil, as He says in the Proverbs of Solomon under the name of Wisdom: <note>original: "Prov. 1, 24. 25"; Proverbs 1:24-25</note> "I call, and you refuse; I stretch out my hand and no one regards it, and you let go of all my counsel, and do not want my punishment (indeed, my help and medicine)." But we poor patients and foolish people, unfortunately, lack this: we do not truly want to learn to recognize the great damage, the deadly sickness of our souls; otherwise, we would certainly ask without ceasing after the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ and after His knowledge and medicine. We would answer His call, gladly hear of Him, desire His presence from our hearts, and daily perceive in our souls the comfort of His lovely Word and wise counsel, indeed, of His friendly countenance.

# The 9th Chapter.

### What the sickness of the soul actually is, and from where it comes.

But what is the sickness of the soul? It is nothing other—as has already been partly understood—than sin with its powers, its gnawing, anxiety, and sting; the heavy, intolerable evil and corruption of our conscience, of which the Prophet David speaks: "O Lord, be merciful to me, heal my soul, for I have sinned against thee." <note>original: "Psalm 51, 6"; In most English translations, this is Psalm 51:4.</note> And again: There is nothing healthy

<summary>The author compares the desperate need for earthly doctors to the much greater need for Christ as the 'Physician of Souls,' identifying the true 'sickness' of humanity as sin and a corrupted conscience.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Physician of Souls, spiritual health, sin, conscience, Proverbs, Psalm 51, healing</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "heavenly Physician" by defining the specific nature of the soul's sickness. The previous page ended with a quote from Proverbs about rejecting divine counsel and punishment; this page begins by quoting the Psalmist to illustrate the physical and spiritual agony caused by sin.</meta>

...in my flesh because of your threat, and there is no peace in my bones because of my sin, etc. My wounds are stinking and have become rotten. <note>original: "Psalm 38, 6"; In most English Bibles, this corresponds to Psalm 38:3-5.</note>

Here we see that the sickness and wounds of the soul, from which the prophet desires to be healed, are nothing other than sin, along with its powers and all the evil it brings upon the human conscience—together with all the unrest that continually follows it in the soul. This is namely the biting of the conscience, the eternally **gnawing worm**, <note>A common historical metaphor for the persistent pangs of guilt or the torments of hell.</note> terror, trembling, fear of hell and of eternal death, God’s disfavor, wrath, tribulation, and anxiety: Romans 2:9. With these, the soul and conscience of the unjust person are tormented and besieged. It is torment and pain, indeed, eternal destruction from the face of the Lord and from His glorious power: 2 Thessalonians 1:9.

For sin eternally separates the soul from God, who is the highest, most inexpressibly delightful Good. *Your iniquities,* says the Holy Spirit in Isaiah 59:2, *separate you and your God from one another, and your sins hide His face from you, so that you are not heard.* Even as the dead, stinking Lazarus, <note>A reference to John 11, where Lazarus had been dead for four days; the author uses his physical decay to mirror the spiritual state of a sinner.</note> whom the Lord awakened, would appear before the eyes of a delicate king of this world: so much more is the dead-rotten sinner before the eyes of the pure God and the King of Heaven, Christ. Therefore sin—which is the sickness of the soul—separates us, where man is not helped by God, and robs us of His glory. It also grieves the angels, offends one's neighbor, and tears apart the communion of saints. Sin, where it takes the upper hand, grieves the Holy Spirit, drives away the spiritual powers and strength of the inner man, and introduces gross darkness, terror, and eternal death.

But how can a heavier sickness, plague, misery, and misfortune ever be devised? It inflicts deadly harm not only upon the sick person himself but also increasingly upon other people around him. Just as no one can endure the stench of a dead body for long, so little can the angels and children of God (much less God Himself) endure the stench and company of offensive, unrepentant sinners for long, unless they (the children of God) also wish to be led astray and thereby poisoned. Thus the sickness of the soul eats away at everything around it when it takes the upper hand in outward deeds, and the ulcer of the heart breaks out so ruinously, as mentioned in 1 Corinthians 5:11.

In summary, the sickness of the soul is the most harmful, heaviest evil; it is, finally, the eternally gnawing worm of the sinful human heart, which elsewhere in the Psalms...

<summary>This page defines the "sickness of the soul" as sin, describing its symptoms as guilt, fear of death, and spiritual decay. Using the imagery of a rotting corpse, the author explains how sin separates the individual from God and infects the surrounding community.</summary>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the metaphor of the "heavenly Physician" introduced on the previous page. Having established that sin is a rotting wound of the soul, the author now compares the torment of a guilty conscience to the physical violence of a storm and the biological spread of a malignant disease. The catchword "derblich" at the bottom right connects to the word "verderblich" (destructive) at the end of the final paragraph.</meta>

->C 2<-

again, and also elsewhere in Holy Scripture, it is called the bonds of death and the snares of hell, or the agony of death and the fear of hell; yes, even thunder, lightning, fire, and sulfur. For just as everything the thunder strikes smells strongly of sulfur, just as the lightning often ignites an inextinguishable fire thereby: so too is the conscience of the ungodly sinner, which God strikes with the thunderbolt of a judging spirit, which He ignites through the punishment of His after-fire <note>Likely referring to the "consuming fire" of divine judgment or the fires of purgatory/hell in a metaphorical sense.</note> and awakens the stench of sin. There it burns and causes such terrifying anxiety that no man may extinguish it—only that one man, the heavenly Physician, Jesus Christ. O God, help us to recover from such heavy sickness here, and being delivered from such a terrifying evil of fire, become whole and healthy.

It is also well to note that such an **innate sickness** is internal in the heart, soul, and conscience; that our damage does not lie in the flesh alone, but in our entire **distorted nature**; <term>distorted nature</term> <note>original: "verrückten Natur"; refers to the theological concept that human nature was "displaced" or "knocked out of joint" from its original perfection by the Fall of Man.</note> that it pushes out from within and eats away everywhere around it, like a **cancer**, <note>original: "der Krebs"; in this period, the term was used for both spreading tumors and gangrenous ulcers.</note> poisoning flesh, work, speech, and all our powers, even in those places where a man might praise them and hold them to be good. Therefore, this damage should not and must not be healed from the outside in, but—following the example of true masters and surgeons—it must be healed from the inside out, and the root and the ground of the sickness or damage must be attacked. Otherwise, it becomes **festering underneath**, <note>original: "unterköthig"; an archaic medical term for a wound that appears closed on the surface but is still producing pus or "corruption" underneath.</note> breaks open again, the longer it lasts the larger it gets, and the last state becomes worse than the first.

In this way, our Physician Jesus Christ is accustomed to healing and making the person healthy at the deadly wound and ulcer of the heart; there, spiritual health soon begins to break out and proves itself with a new, repentant life in love and godliness. He opens the heart and cleanses it through faith, since from within, out of the hearts of men, proceed evil thoughts, adultery, fornication, murder, theft, greed, false witness, wickedness, deceit, lewdness, an evil eye, blasphemy, pride, and foolishness; all these evil pieces go out from within and make the person unclean, says the Lord in Matthew 15:19 and Mark 7:21.

I have wished to call this to mind because there are many teachers or preachers who do not teach correctly about sin or spiritual sickness, nor do they know its nature, seat, and dwelling place. They do not seek sin internally in nature, but only externally in the flesh, and look upon it only in works. Therefore, they also want to heal the sickness with works. They do not seek sin in the soul and conscience, but only externally and superficially; because of this, they do not highly esteem the medicine of Christ, nor do they present and proclaim it according to its dignity. Instead, they invent another medicine, which occurs through external things, and—though in vain and even **destructive**—they wish to heal people spiritually with it.

<summary>This page argues that sin is a deep-seated, internal "cancer" of the soul that cannot be cured by external good works, but only by the internal healing of Jesus Christ. It warns against preachers who treat sin as a superficial matter of behavior rather than a fundamental corruption of human nature.</summary>

<keywords>conscience, Jesus Christ, Original Sin, internal healing, surgeons, spiritual sickness, repentance, false teachers, corruption of nature</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical allegory of sin, moving from the symptoms of a troubled conscience to the diagnosis of its origin: Original Sin. It transitions from a critique of "false physicians" (likely legalistic preachers) to a biblical explanation of humanity's fallen state.</meta>

Such people do not rightly direct others to Christ, the heavenly Physician, and the pharmacy of His grace; but as mentioned, to the shame and contempt of Christ’s medicine and the Physician Himself, they want to heal the sick themselves and be esteemed as physicians of the soul. They may indeed offer some fine comfort, but in the end, the patient sees that he is ruined, and he laments that he has rejected the true Physician and Master, Jesus Christ. Therefore, let such teachers be exhorted to improve themselves; likewise, let the Christian people be warned against them, so that the honor of the only heavenly Physician, Jesus Christ, may return home pure and whole and be given to Him by everyone. But more shall be said regarding this point hereafter, if God wills.

If you ask, where then does such sickness come from? Answer: The sickness, plague, and corruption of the soul originates from the disobedience and the transgression of God’s commandment. It is a **hereditary sickness** <note>original: "Erbsucht"; a term blending "heredity" and "infirmity," framing Original Sin as a genetic or biological plague inherited from ancestors.</note>, the foul sap and pestilential influence <note>Refers to the medical theory of "miasma" or "influences" that caused disease, here applied to the spiritual infection of sin.</note> born into us from the first man, our father Adam, after he became apostate to the kind God, his Lord and Creator, and instead became obedient to the evil spirit and his seductive words, as is written in Genesis 2 and 3. Thus, this deadly sickness (namely, sin) came into the world through one man, and then further penetrated and took root in all people; this is sufficiently testified and proven in all men by the following punishment—that is, death (which is the wage of sin). The Holy Spirit has most clearly and excellently indicated and interpreted all of this through Paul in Romans 5:12 and 1 Corinthians 15:21. Yet, the poor, sick human being unfortunately does not let himself be satisfied with such an innate evil, but he often increases and heaps up the aforementioned sickness through his evil, disorderly life, through his straying and great iniquity. He creates more and more of this, often persisting in it with contempt for God, so that it becomes all the more difficult for him to be helped.

The prophet Isaiah prophesied truthfully about all of this when he spoke thus: "Woe to the sinful nation, a people laden with iniquity, a seed of evildoers, shameful children who forsake the Lord, blaspheme the Holy Spirit in Israel, and turn away backward, etc. The whole head is sick, the whole heart is faint. From the sole of the foot even unto the head, there is no soundness in them; but wounds, and bruises, and putrifying sores: they have not been closed, neither bound up, neither mollified with ointment." (Isaiah 1:4–6). And Jeremiah 15:18: "Our wounds are so very evil that no one can heal them." Furthermore, in Chapter 17:14: "Heal me, O Lord, and I shall be healed; save me, and I shall be saved." See how the Spirit of God in the prophets traces the entire nature of the sick, corrupted flesh directly from its hereditary root, using the Jewish people as a figure <note>A "figure" or "type" in theology is a historical person or event that serves as a symbol for a universal spiritual truth.</note> and painting it as an example for us all; how He also calls sin a deadly sick-

...ness and evil wounds, which no one can heal but God; it describes its great, unspeakable harm so sharply and terribly, so that through the prophets, a greater longing, hope, love, and desire might be awakened or kindled for the promised Physician <note>Referring to Jesus Christ, framed throughout this text as the "Heavenly Physician."</note> (to whom, as has already been heard in part, all their prophecies point).

But that sin—this sickness of the soul—is such a heavy, unbearable plague, a conspicuous evil, and a deadly disease (to which no earthly illness can be compared in the slightest), is known best by those who have once truly felt such a sickness and have recognized and felt that **gnawing worm** <note>A traditional metaphor for the stinging or "eating" sensation of a guilty conscience.</note> in their conscience. O Lord God, if no counsel, hope, nor comfort were then at hand, and if the Physician did not come to help, it would be the end for mankind; for human nature and frailty could not endure such great anxiety and woe for long.

It is then that the **seven Penitential Psalms** <note>A collection of seven biblical songs (Psalms 6, 32, 38, 51, 102, 130, and 143) historically used to express deep remorse and a plea for God's help.</note> and other Psalms truly begin to be tested and experienced in the heart, when a person, having recognized their soul-sickness, cries out with dear David and says: "O have mercy on me, O Lord God, according to your great mercy, for my iniquities have gone over my head; like an unbearable burden, they have become much too heavy for me. I am otherwise almost crushed and broken. I howl because of the unrest of my heart; my appearance is changed by mourning and has grown old, for I am distressed on every side, and my spirit is anxious within me. My heart is consumed within my body. Fear and trembling have come upon me, and horror has overwhelmed me. O God, help me through your name, for the pains of death surround me, and the **torrents of Belial** <note>original: "Bäche Belials"; a biblical term often signifying the "floods of destruction" or the power of the underworld.</note> terrify me; the bonds of hell encompass me, and the snares of death overcome me." (Ps. 18:5-6).

From this, one can well understand what a heavy and unbearable evil the sin and sickness of the soul must be. For whoever can speak of such things from the heart without hypocrisy—as the Prophet David undoubtedly spoke them—must certainly first feel true anxiety, misery, and distress for himself. He must indeed find himself troubled, weak, and sick enough, praying also for others—yes, for the distress of all Christendom—and complaining before God about the evil, innate nature of the flesh that exists in every man. And that is the right time to call for the Physician, to turn to him quickly, and to surrender to his true healing. Then, the wretched, needy person should quickly turn from his sickness toward the Physician, ask for him, and wait for his help in true faith, so that he may say from the heart: Oh

Lord (Jesus) heal me, for my bones are terrified. God, speak to my soul: I am your salvation <note>original: *Heil*, which carries the dual meaning of "salvation" and "healing" or "health."</note>, because there is no health in my flesh. Ps. 38, 4.

What then does God further promise through the prophet? Such a promise is then a certain testimony and legal record <note>original: *Urkunde*, implying a formal, binding document or deed</note> that all who seek our Physician Jesus Christ from the heart and believe in him shall soon be released from such a terrible evil by him, and become joyful and healthy.

So it stands now, as has been heard, with this heavy plague and sickness—especially among those who truly feel and recognize it—that the sick person may certainly long for the Physician Christ. He would give everything he has if only he were helped, for everything becomes too narrow for him, every space too cramped <note>original: *winklicht*, meaning full of corners or claustrophobic</note>; the whole world often seems to become too small for him. For a bad conscience—namely the **gnawing worm** of the heart—when it is stirred up, presses and gnaws, and is the heaviest burden on earth. If a person does not rid themselves of this through the faithful, merciful Physician Jesus Christ, they must die the eternal death and be buried in hell with it. Only then does one truly learn how useful Christ is to the world, and what a lovely, unspeakable blessing this heavenly Physician provides.

It would also be no wonder if such a person, who feels their spiritual sickness rightly and considers such a great, deadly injury, would never be truly joyful in this world. Indeed, it would be a miracle if such a person could ever be happy without Christ the heavenly Physician and without his medicine <term>Arzeney: an archaic term for a medicinal cure or apothecary’s remedy</term>, as examples can be found of certain people; just as one writer says: "Whether I eat or drink, sleep or wake, etc., the terrible voice always rings in my ears: 'Arise, you dead, come to judgment!'" <note>A famous quote often attributed to St. Jerome regarding the "Trumpet of the Last Judgment."</note> Such unspeakable harm and inner corruption of the soul is only stirred up in some at the moment of death, where they also experience a foretaste of the earnest judgment of God. If the Physician Christ does not come to their aid then, they are eternally lost; for without Christ and his medicine, no one can be saved.

Therefore I have said that for such a spiritually sick, anxious person who feels it, even the large, wide world often becomes too narrow without Christ, when he rightly feels the secret suffering of his wounded soul. # Chapter 10.

### Concerning those who partially experience the soul’s sickness, but do not rightly know, nor wish to know, the Physician and the heavenly medicine.

The feeling of spiritual sickness—that is, the sensing of sin and the **gnawing worm** <note>A common historical metaphor for the persistent, painful pangs of a guilty conscience.</note> of a bad conscience and an impure heart—has in former times driven some into monasteries. It even drove them from one monastery to another, from a lighter order to a heavier one, so that they perhaps took on strict, unbearable rules. They intended to heal their internal sickness without the Physician, Christ, through their own external works, church exercises, and ceremonies. They resolved to do penance for their sin with the severity of human statutes, hoping to escape God’s judgment and hellish pain through self-chosen compulsion, and to still their conscience with the prestige of their religious order <note>original: *Ordens-Ansehen*</note>. But where, meanwhile, does the soul’s health remain? Where remains grace, peace, joy, and salvation? These must be sought, hoped for, and obtained from our Lord and Physician Jesus Christ alone through a true faith, and out of love, and can be found nowhere else.

God also speaks of such sick people through the prophet Hosea, using the figure of the children of Israel, when He says: Ephraim suffers violence and is oppressed (namely in the conscience); he is treated rightly in this, for he has given himself over to human commandments (namely, to heal his sickness through them). I am as a moth to Ephraim, and a plague to the house of Judah <note>original: *Plage*; some translations read "rot" or "a woodworm," referring to a judgment that eats away from the inside because of sin</note>. And when Ephraim felt his sickness and Judah his wounds, Ephraim went to Assyria <note>original: *Asser*</note> and sent to King Jareb <note>original: *Könige zu Jared*; a reference to Hosea 5:13 where Israel seeks political/human alliances instead of divine help</note> (who this is, experience now allows us to recognize); but he could not help you, nor heal your wounds, says the Lord. Hos. 5:11-13.

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of "false cures" for the soul, moving from the general description of spiritual sickness to specific examples of how people seek relief through superstition, legalism, and fear rather than through faith in Christ. It links these behaviors to the biblical story of King Ahaziah.</meta>

These sick people, who seek medicine either from someone other than the true Physician, Christ, or who attempt to help themselves toward health through their own works and self-imposed customs of outward exercise and the like, are just like those who, in physical illness, go to images of the holy pilgrimages or run about, or those who seek health from blessers, fortune-tellers, or sorcerers and despise proven physicians. Although such people may find a measure of imagined comfort in their own opinion, in truth they do not become healthy. This becomes manifest in times of need, in death, and in the act of dying, where they perish, despair, and descend to hell in deadly fear. They are like King Ahaziah, who in his sickness sent messengers to Baal-Zebub <note>A Philistine god; the name literally means "Lord of the Flies." In this context, it represents seeking help from false idols.</note>, etc., to whom it was said by the prophet Elijah through the messengers: "You shall not come down from the bed on which you have gone up, but you shall surely die." 2 Kings 1:4.

Therefore, the repentance or intent of such people (even if they recognize their sickness and would gladly be rid of it) is nonetheless built on no right foundation, insofar as they intend to become healthy, free of sin, and blessed through their own ordinances and monastic rules. Furthermore, their remorse, withdrawal, and acts of penance do not arise from the love of God; they do not happen for the **mortification** <term>The "dying away" of the sinful nature or "old self" in order to be spiritually reborn.</term> of the old man and the growth of the virtuous new man. Nor is it aligned with the single rule and prescription of the Physician Jesus Christ in the freedom of His Spirit; rather, it is more out of self-love and comes only from fear of hell and from the anguish of the pain they have earned with their sins. It stems from the fact that they do not recognize the only Physician, Helper, and Savior, Jesus Christ, with His gift of grace.

For this reason, the penitence and intent of the aforementioned people is not right, because it does not "taste" of the holy Gospel of Jesus Christ <note>A common Baroque-era metaphor suggesting that true faith should be experienced as something sweet or nourishing to the soul’s palate.</note>, because it is not directed toward the true healing of the heavenly Physician and according to His wise counsel. Even if such people, through the deceit of the evil spirit (who also gives himself out to be a physician of souls), may persuade themselves of their spiritual health for a time so that they know no better. They have made a covenant with death and an agreement with hell; when the flood passes through, it will not reach them, etc., as the prophet Isaiah says: Chapter 28:15. But because they make for themselves a false refuge, a self-chosen medicine, and a deceptive screen, it can ultimately have no permanence for them. To be sure, the enemy can well heal a sickness of coarse, outward vice in appearance; however, in its place, he secretly shoots ten poisonous arrows into the heart. Yet these erring people have no remorse or conscience regarding this, wherever the heavenly Physician is not recognized nor His medicine rightly used.

<summary>This page warns that seeking spiritual healing through pilgrimages, superstition, or rigid monastic rules is a false cure driven by fear of hell rather than love for God. It compares these misguided efforts to King Ahaziah’s fatal mistake of seeking help from idols instead of the true God.</summary>
