Such a person would rob Christ—who is both God and man—of his divine honor, and would neither hold nor accept him as the true, natural Son of God. Consequently, such a person would not truly consider Christ's flesh and blood to be a medicine, nor even as food for the soul; they would not believe in Christ, and therefore would not partake in such food and medicine. Let everyone be faithfully warned of this, who intends to be a Christian and wishes to enter heaven and be saved.

# The 23rd Chapter.

### That the Physician Christ is still a true human today, but within the essence of God and in divine glory.

Even though the flesh and blood of Christ, as well as the Word (or the divine nature), is the true spiritual medicine <note>The author uses "medicine" (Arzney) as a metaphor for the restorative power of Christ’s presence and the Eucharist.</note> of the poor, sick soul, one should not understand this as if he were not still a true flesh and blood (or human nature) today. Although Jesus Christ has attained divine power according to his humanity, and has inherited and taken up the new heavenly essence in divine unity, he is nonetheless still a true human—indeed, the very same human born of the Virgin Mary and nailed to the cross, who rose from the dead. He is now, however, made completely perfect—Hebrews 2:7, 9; Chapter 5:5, 7—declared a new heavenly man and made equal to God. In him, all previous transitory weakness has been transformed into an eternal divine strength through his resurrection, ascension, and seating at the right hand of God, as Paul says: *For though he was crucified in weakness, yet he lives by the power of God*, 2 Corinthians 13:4. From this we clearly learn to see and rightly distinguish between the two states of Christ, namely the weak and the strong, the human and the divine. The first state <note>Refers to Christ's "state of humiliation" during his earthly life.</note> has ceased in the man Christ; the second has gloriously begun, as Christ lives in the power of God. Indeed, God’s power is now his entire life, so that Jesus Christ, even according to his humanity, is now capable of everything that he, as the eternal Word, was always capable of with God his Father; for he is now, as the only-begotten Son, both God and man, placed and received into the highest honor of divine unity and essence with God his Father.

<vocab>Christ, humanity, flesh and blood, medicine, food for the soul, divine nature, resurrection, ascension, power of God</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that for Christ to function as a spiritual healer, the believer must acknowledge both his historical humanity and his current exalted divine status. It explains that while Christ's earthly weakness ended with his resurrection, he remains a "true man" now glorified and unified with the power of God.</summary>
<keywords>Christology, Heavenly Physician, Eucharist, Two Natures of Christ, Exaltation, Spiritual Medicine, Salvation, Resurrection</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 23 regarding the glorified nature of Christ. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how Christ, as the "new heavenly man," has been made perfect and equal to God, having moved beyond the limitations of his earthly life.</meta>

-> ~~~ <-

Therefore, when we speak of a new being and life in the man Jesus Christ, it should not be understood as if he had laid aside his humanity, blood, and flesh; that does not follow at all. Rather, it means that the humanity in Christ has been improved, glorified <note>The term "glorified" (verkläret) refers to the theological concept of the Transfiguration and the post-Resurrection state, where the physical body is no longer subject to decay.</note>, and made eternal. Just as Christians, through him, also become partakers of such a being in eternal life—made similar to him, but not thereby erased or brought to nothing. Our heavenly physician Jesus Christ no longer lives in an earthly life and being; he lives much better and more gloriously. Whatever he lives, he lives for **God**, in the power of **God**, as Paul says in Romans 6:9–10 and 2 Corinthians 13:4. Everything about him has now become eternal, spiritual, and divine; how else could he be the food for our souls, our spiritual medicine <note>original: "Arzeney"</note>, and our life? In short, his whole human person lives the life that God has held eternally, and that the Word (or Son of God) has eternally lived and held with God. Thus, a single life of both God and man is found in Christ; otherwise, Christ would be divided within himself in heaven, possessing two kinds of life, two kinds of spirit and being. Every believer can easily imagine what an impropriety would follow from such a division.

Christ Jesus, I say once more with the testimony of Holy Scripture, certainly has two natures; he is **God** and **man** inseparably. But these two natures exist in one divine, eternal life and being. This man’s life and being—now after his glorification, ascension, and indeed his exaltation above all heavens—should not be viewed as the life and being of a "natural" man. Human reason does this because it cannot climb any higher in its knowledge of Christ. Instead, his life should be reckoned and understood as the divine essence, in and with **God** equally—which only faith is capable of, for faith alone rightly knows Christ.

Otherwise, the Holy Spirit, God’s righteousness, the health of the soul, and finally eternal life and salvation could in no way reach us, if such a beginning and progression had not been taken up to the throne at the right hand of **God** with the humanity of Christ as our Head. Therefore, our heavenly physician Jesus Christ, together with the medicine <note>The author frequently uses medical metaphors to describe the Sacraments, particularly the body and blood of Christ.</note> of his flesh and blood, must no longer be recognized or viewed according to the human manner in which he lived, suffered, and walked on earth, now that the office of our redemption is accomplished. Instead, he must be seen in a divine, spiritual manner, according to the essence in which he now exists and reigns. He speaks of this himself in the Gospel, saying: *And I, if I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all men to myself.* John 12:32. For solely because of the exaltation of Christ’s flesh—first on the cross, and thereafter in glory—is the Holy Spirit poured out over all "believing flesh," through which it is also drawn up through Christ into the divine heavenly being toward eternal salvation.

<summary>This page argues that Christ’s humanity was not discarded after his resurrection but was "glorified" into a divine state, allowing him to serve as a spiritual medicine for humanity. It emphasizes that Christ must be understood through faith as a unified divine-human being rather than through limited human reason.</summary>
<keywords>Transfiguration, Exaltation of Christ, dual nature, spiritual medicine, Holy Spirit, glorification, heavenly physician</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from Chapter 23, concluding the discussion on the glorified state of Jesus Christ as the mediator through whom God’s riches are shared. It then transitions into Chapter 24, which addresses the historical question of how Christ acted as a spiritual physician for those who lived before his birth in the Old Testament.</meta>

-> ~~~ <-

Following such a nature and state, the man Jesus Christ must now be rightly recognized through faith, so that one knows how God dwells in him bodily, how He appears and shines through him in the face of Jesus Christ, and how He is found in him with all heavenly goods; for He has also resolved to give and communicate these—indeed, all His heavenly wealth—through no other means than through this man, the one Mediator between God and mankind. Of this, Paul also says in 1 Timothy 2:5–6: There is one God and one Mediator between God and mankind, namely, the man Jesus Christ, who gave himself for all as a ransom, so that this would be proclaimed in due time. He is our Savior <note>original: "Heyland"</note>, our Physician, our Medicine <note>original: "Arzeney"</note>, our Bringer of Salvation, our Help, and our All.

## Chapter 24.

### How it stood with the Medicine and the Physician of souls in the Old Testament, before Christ became man.

At this point, someone might ask: since it has been stated that not only the Word of God (or the divine nature), but also the humanity of Christ—that is, Christ as whole God with his suffering, death, and shedding of blood—is the Physician and Medicine of our sick souls; how then did it stand with such a Physician and Medicine before Christ became *man*, or before the Word became flesh? To this we answer as follows: That regarding the Physician and Medicine of souls with and before God, to whom all future things are present, there was one and the same order of salvation <term>Heilsordnung: the theological plan or "arrangement of health" by which God saves humanity</term> before the incarnation of Christ, before the Law, under the Law, and when the Physician came into the flesh. Furthermore, now that the flesh or the man Jesus Christ has been moved and received into God, Jesus Christ, the eternal Word of God, was already in the beginning—immediately after Adam’s fall—the Physician and Savior of all the chosen, as well as their King, Head, and Lord, and he remains so.

For since Holy Scripture testifies that before God no one can become healthy—that is, righteous or saved—without this Physician; and yet in the Old Testament there were many pious, righteous people, including holy patriarchs and prophets; it must follow that Christ healed, justified, and made all of them healthy and saved (since they were initially shut under sin just as much as all of Adam's children). And even though the Word of God had not yet become flesh there, and although Christ had not yet suffered or died...

<summary>This page defines Jesus Christ as the sole mediator and "spiritual medicine" for humanity, then begins a new chapter explaining that Christ functioned as this savior for Old Testament figures even before his physical incarnation.</summary>
<keywords>Mediator, Physician of souls, Medicine, 1 Timothy 2:5-6, Incarnation, Old Testament, Order of Salvation, Patriarchs, Prophets</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous chapter, completing the sentence regarding how the salvation of Christ was applied to Old Testament figures before his physical birth. The catchword "gebohren" (born) from the previous page likely refers to the "born as a man" mentioned at the end of this page's logic.</meta>

...nor had he yet shed his blood, yet this was promised shortly after Adam’s Fall, and likewise later promised to Abraham: namely, that the eternal Word of God, Christ, should in the future become man to bring back rebellious and lost humanity, to bless them, and to heal them from the wounds of the evil spirit through his own wounds and blood-shedding, making them righteous and blessed.

Those who then grasped or accepted such a promise from God concerning Christ, his suffering, and his salvation through faith, possessed and held Christ as the heavenly Physician—along with his medicine and benefits—according to the truth of faith, just as fully as all true believers do now. They received not only the medicine of the Word of God, but also Christ's suffering and blood-shedding through the word of promise (which was spirit and life) for the healing and health of their souls. This was true despite the fact that the Word had not yet become flesh and that Christ's blood had not yet been shed.

But faith, which makes the future present, brought it home to the hearts of the believers according to the divine eternal truth, so that they enjoyed its benefits as perfectly as if it had already occurred. There, the believing fathers were healed and made healthy from the bite of the ancient serpent <note>A common theological reference to the devil and the Fall of Man in the Garden of Eden</note> by the blood of the Lamb—which was to be slaughtered and sacrificed for the sins of the whole world—and by Christ's stripes <note>original: "Striemen"; refers to the welts or wounds from the scourging of Jesus</note>, through his future cross and suffering. That is to say, they were made righteous, holy, and blessed, just as we are now (provided we truly believe, which may Almighty God grant) after the promise has been fulfilled. We too are released from our soul-sickness—that is, from sin and eternal death—and are made whole and blessed; the fact that Christ’s coming in the flesh was still in the future could not hinder the believers of that time.

We now wish to prove this with several passages from Holy Scripture. First, where Paul writes to the Hebrews: "Jesus Christ is the same yesterday and today, and forever." This means that he is a Savior, a Physician, and a Christ in both the Old and New Testaments (namely, according to faith and before God). Based on this, Paul then admonishes the Hebrews, saying: "Do not be carried away by various and strange teachings; for it is a precious thing that the heart be established, which happens through grace" (Hebrews 13:8–9).

Since the believers before the Law and under the Law were saved precisely through such grace of our Lord Jesus Christ—as Peter says in Acts 15:11: "We believe that we will be saved through the grace of the Lord Jesus Christ, in the same way as they also were"—it follows that Christ, with all his benefits, blood-shedding, cross, and suffering, was just as much their Savior, Physician, and Medicine as he has been ours, even before he was born as a man.

<meta>This page continues the theological argument from page 62, specifically addressing how Old Testament figures like Abraham received the benefits of Christ’s sacrifice before the historical event of the Crucifixion. It concludes the list of points supporting the idea that faith makes future promises a present reality.</meta>

->❦❦❦❦❦<-

Secondly, the Lord Christ himself says of Abraham to the Jews: "Your father Abraham rejoiced that he was to see my day, and he saw it and was glad"; by this, the Lord means his "day"—his coming in the flesh, along with his entire office of the cross and the glory that followed. Abraham saw and recognized this with the eyes of faith through the sacrifice of his son Isaac. Thus, through faith, he became a partaker of all the benefits of Christ, his suffering, and his merit at that time, as if it had already happened, just as all true believers are today. Abraham believed God, who promised him that in his "seed," Christ, all nations should be blessed; and it was accounted to him as righteousness. He was released from his inner, innate sickness <note>original: "innerlichen angebohrnen Krankheit"; a common theological metaphor for Original Sin</note>, treated with the medicine of the soul, and became whole and healthy.

Thirdly, we also have a clear passage regarding this in the Revelation of John, which agrees exactly with the previous ones, where John writes: That the Lamb was slain from the beginning of the world, Revelation 18:8 <note>The author likely intended to cite Revelation 13:8, which refers to the Lamb slain from the foundation of the world</note>. Without doubt, this was not meant physically, but rather in the gracious decree of God; for God had so decided that Jesus should suffer for our sins, and that his blood should be the washing and medicine for them. To faith, this was just as significant then as if it had already been accomplished. Therefore, John does not say that the Lamb *should* be slain, but that from the beginning of the world it *was already* slain. This means that all the elect have enjoyed the benefits of the suffering and bloodshed of Christ from the very beginning—that Christ has been the Physician of all believers, and his blood has been the medicine then just as much as it is now.

Therefore, although the man Jesus Christ did not yet exist at that time, nor was his blood yet shed for the washing away of sins, nor prepared as a medicine for the soul—but only became our Physician through the cross and suffering, and his shed blood became the medicine of the soul in and with the Word—nevertheless, faith made the future promise of Christ (which was to be fulfilled later) present to all believers then, just as it has now been fulfilled. The sick man, wounded unto death—that is, the poor, miserable sinner—was called back into grace, raised up, and accepted by God shortly after the Fall to begin his restoration. The Physician was pledged and promised to him. Even though Jesus Christ did not come immediately, nor become man, nor shed his blood for the forgiveness of sins and the medicine of the soul, it was nonetheless entirely certain to the believers through the promise of the true God that it would happen in the fullness of time. In this certainty of faith, they enjoyed the medicine of Christ’s blood, as well as the Word of God, for the health of their souls as perfectly then as all true believers enjoy it now, after it has come to pass.

And to speak even more clearly about this: although Jesus Christ, according to his divine nature, has been the Physician, Savior, Lord, and King of all believers from the very beginning, he has also—according to his humanity—been given to us by God to be our Physician, Savior, Lord, King, and Head of his entire congregation, as stated in Ephesians 1:18-22. In the same way, he has eternally been the righteousness and wisdom of God, and yet he also *became* our wisdom and righteousness after completing his office of redemption. 1 Corinthians 1:30.

The prophets, through the Holy Spirit, prophesied of this decree and will of God—namely, of the incarnation, suffering, death, bloodshed, resurrection, and glory of Christ, as well as our redemption through him. 1 Peter 1:10, 11, 12. And the beloved holy fathers in the Old Testament never believed otherwise than that it would certainly come to pass, and thus they were saved through Christ. Their faith clung to the promise of Christ; it reached forward into the future incarnation, death, and bloodshed of Christ, which was to happen and be accomplished in the fullness of time. Through this faith, Christ was their Physician just as much as ours, and his suffering, death, and bloodshed were the medicine <note>original: "Arzeney"; commonly used in this era to describe the "healing" power of the Gospel or Christ's sacrifice</note> for their souls for the forgiveness or washing away of sins, and for everlasting eternal health, as well as the food and drink of their souls.

True faith can well comprehend such things, and for the salvation of all believers, it can possess the truth that Christ remains the same yesterday, today, and forever; that to believers, he was the same before his incarnation as he was after—fully God and man. He is not only the soul’s Physician and medicine, but also his flesh and blood are their food and drink, without which eating and drinking no one can be saved. John 6:53-54.

All this is not difficult to understand for those who know the nature, kind, and quality of health-giving—that is, justifying—faith, and who know from Paul that faith is a "substance" or "standing-in-itself" <note>original: "Selbststand"; a German rendering of the Greek 'hypostasis' used in Hebrews 11:1, meaning a foundational reality or substance</note> of the (heavenly) things for which one hopes, which do not appear physically nor are seen. It is a gift of God and a present from the Holy Spirit, a ray of the heavenly sun that shines from the beginning into eternity. This ray brings with it a glimpse of the Divine Essence, in which everything is seen as eternal and present. Therefore, faith does not grasp Christ, God and man, in a human way—constrained by time, place, and form—but according to the Mystery, beyond all place and time, even before he became man, in a divine spiritual essence as he stands with and before God. Faith beholds the entire administration of Christ's office in a single moment, so to speak,

Everything that Christ accomplished here physically for our redemption and salvation, where he also became the Physician and the medicine, has now been transitioned into the Spirit, and everything regarding Christ has become spiritual, so that it might be distributed to believers through the reigning King of Grace, Jesus Christ, in the Holy Spirit—just as the Holy Spirit still carries out in Christ today whatever is necessary for salvation following Christ’s work of redemption.

# The 25th Chapter.

### Concerning the Twofold Manner of Christ's Healing, as well as the Power, Nature, and Effect of His Heavenly Medicine upon the Sick Human Being.

Here we should also note that the aforementioned heavenly medicine generally has two distinct powers, natures, and effects (although it is and remains of one divine essence), just as the Physician acts or medicates in two ways. One effect or power is painful, by which the damage of the old flesh <note>The "old flesh" refers to the sinful, unregenerate nature of man</note> is stirred up, the sin and sickness are attacked, the foul, festering wounds are cleansed, the decaying "wild flesh" <term>wildes Fleisch: proud flesh or infected tissue; here used metaphorically for stubborn sin that must be purged</term> is killed off, and the person is gradually healed.

The other power is lovely, which brings with it the comfort of health and the gentle oil of joy <note>original: "Freuden-Oel"; a reference to the biblical "oil of gladness" used to soothe and heal</note>, which always accompanies...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Christ as the heavenly Physician. The previous page established that faith connects the believer to Christ’s healing power, transitioning His physical redemptive acts into a spiritual reality for the heart.</meta>

...heals, strengthens the spirit, and makes the soul healthy and alive; then, subsequently, all internal pain of the heart is driven out, and the sadness of the mind is transformed into spiritual joy. The heavenly physician, Christ, intended to indicate this twofold power by showing that the Samaritan (by whom he signifies himself) used wine and oil when he healed his patient. <term>Luke 10:34</term>

These two ways, powers, or effects of the medicine and the doctoring subdue the flesh and destroy the kingdom of sin; they keep the healthy person in their office and essence. They also bring them back to the physician if any part of the illness stirs again, and they fully effect the dying-off of the flesh with its evil desires.

The first power of said medicine is called the Word of the Cross. It is for the "old Adam" <note>A theological term referring to the fallen, sinful human nature inherited from Adam.</note>, whose illness it must tame and strike down. Indeed, it must subdue and tear out of the flesh all evil desire, unseemly worldly joy, and false confidence that exists outside of God and Christ. It is the strong, sour myrrh-wine, the bitter syrup of the cross and the suffering of Jesus Christ, the Son of God, which the poor sick person must soon drink if they seek the physician and desire to become healthy. Through this power, they must first be well-purged <note>In historical medicine, a "purge" was a treatment used to cleanse the body of toxins or "bad humors."</note> under the cross of Christ—tested both inwardly and outwardly for the mortification <term>Abtödtung: the spiritual process of "killing" the sinful nature</term> of sin and the old man—before they may attain and possess the perfect health of eternal life. Concerning this, Paul <term>2 Cor. 4, 10. 11.</term> writes as follows: We always carry around in our body the dying of the Lord Jesus, so that the life of the Lord Jesus may also be revealed in our body. For we who are alive are always given over to death for Jesus' sake, so that the life of Jesus may also be revealed in our mortal flesh.

From this power of medicine in the Word of the Cross, one might also understand what Paul writes, <term>Hebr. 4, 12,</term> where he says: The word of God is living and active, and sharper than any two-edged sword, and pierces until it divides soul and spirit, joints and marrow, and is a judge of the thoughts and intentions of the heart. For it happens just as it does with all long-term patients in the beginning: when they first use a strong, unfamiliar medicine, everything in their body is stirred up, and the disease breaks out in such a way that they only become sicker during the first purge and often fear they are dying. Similarly, our heavenly physician Christ initially uses a bitter syrup so that he may gather all the poison—that is, sin—into one place and drive it to the heart, so that it may be fully opened and known to the heart, the soul, and the conscience; there he then gives a gentle digestive <note>original: "Verdauungsmittel" — used here as a metaphor for a soothing remedy that helps the soul "process" the conviction of sin.</note>, strengthening, and refreshment.

...that is the grace of salvation and the spirit of faith, so that the Physician cleanses the weak, sick heart and thus makes the whole person whole and healthy.

Let him only endure patiently under the hand of the proven Physician, Christ, surrender himself to Him entirely from the heart, and let Him work, ensuring that he does not turn the eyes of his mind away from Him in any deadly needs or obstacles. Then, certainly, when the Physician deems the time right and it is useful for the person, health will not be lacking.

Why then would a person willfully rob themselves of such great glory for the sake of a little discomfort? Why then would he not willingly drink such a draught from the cup—which the heavenly Physician Himself drank for the good of the sick, and thus entered into His glory—and follow his Lord, Physician, and Master Jesus Christ through the cross and suffering into the kingdom of heaven?

In short, on both sides it is nothing other than the one grace-filled Christ, crucified for our sake and risen again for the sake of our righteousness, who now sits, reigns, administers medicine, and helps all believers, whom He leads out into the glory of God, toward conformity with Him, and to the inheritance of the kingdom of heaven.

Regarding such twofold power of the heavenly medicine, we have Deuteronomy 32:39 <note>original: "5 Mos. 32" refers to the Fifth Book of Moses</note>, where God the Almighty speaks: "See now that I, even I, am he, and there is no god beside me: I kill, and I make alive; I wound, and I heal: neither is there any that can deliver out of my hand." Although this is to be understood in the historical sense regarding the punishment and judgment of God over the Jews, it can certainly be applied here as well, because it says previously in verse 36: The Lord will judge his people, and he will have compassion on his servants...

mercy: And 1 Samuel 2:6. The Lord kills and makes alive, leads into the grave <note>original: "Hölle" — while translated literally as "hell," in this biblical context it refers to the Hebrew *Sheol* or the state of the dead.</note> and back out again; He kills, wounds, and slays through the **Word of the Cross** within a person that rises up against Him and His Spirit in disobedience. The proud He has humbled and the humble He has exalted.

The first power kills, the other makes alive. The first reveals the sickness, the other gives health. The first is bitter and terrifying, the other is sweet and lovely. The first moves the heart and conscience, creating anxiety, woe, and tribulation, so that the sick person often does not know what is happening to him or where he shall remain; the other lets the dear sun shine, dispelling the clouds, the mist, and the previous storm, so that the soul begins to pour itself out in joy, ceases its terror, and begins to see why the storm arose within it, what it has worked, and where it has gone; so that a beautiful and lovely clear day follows in the heart. The first acts like the goldsmith, who places the silver in the cupel <note>original: "Test" — a specialized vessel used in the refining process of precious metals.</note> within the smelting furnace and stirs it, diligently blowing on the fire he lights for testing, making the furnace where the silver is hot and glowing so that it may melt and purify itself; but afterwards, he takes the silver out of the heat and lets it cool. And let this be a brief indication for further reflection on the two-fold power and effect of the heavenly medicine upon the sick person.

By this, however, I do not wish to limit the freedom of the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ in His many ways of medicating or healing, nor to have His medicine bound solely to these two-fold powers or effects in any way. I wish neither to abolish the ground of God's prevenient grace <note>original: "vorkommenden Gnade GOttes" — the theological concept of grace that precedes human decision.</note>, nor the beginning of rebirth and repentance, nor the elementary principles <note>original: "erste Schulrecht" — likely referring to the "milk" or basic doctrines mentioned in Hebrews 5 and 6.</note> mentioned in Hebrews 5 and 6, but rather that the Physician in His medicating often...

## The 26th Chapter.

### Concerning the Trial of the Physician of Souls and the Heavenly Medicine of the Word of God, Christ.

However, so that we may have an example of both the patient and the sickness, as well as the Physician Christ and His medicine all together, we wish to look at the miracle of the paralytic <note>original: "Gichtbrüchigen" — literally "the gout-broken," a term used in historical German Bibles to describe a paralytic or someone suffering from palsy.</note>, whom the Lord—through the power of His almighty Word—raised up and made healthy, not only inwardly in the soul, but also outwardly in the body and its limbs. This is a true masterpiece of His divine skill, which He demonstrated at that time when He was present on earth, before He allowed Himself to be proclaimed as a proven Doctor of Medicine, as the text in the Gospel clearly states in Matthew 9:2, where it is written: They brought to the Lord a paralytic lying on a bed; and when Jesus saw their faith, He said to the paralytic: "Be of good cheer, my son, your sins are forgiven you."

Here we see the sick man, as well as the two-fold sickness (the physical and the spiritual), likewise the Physician, comfort, and medicine in the Word of the Lord, all present before our eyes, just as the two-fold power of the medicine is also found therein. For after the Lord says to the paralytic: "Be of good cheer, my son, your sins are forgiven," it is easy to see that the first power of the medicine—which works terror, anxiety, repentance, and sorrow for sin—must have preceded inwardly within the paralytic, upon which the Lord comforts him, and the other power of His medicine...

<summary>This page concludes the theological explanation of spiritual healing and begins Chapter 26, which uses the healing of the paralytic in Matthew 9 as a "masterpiece" example of Christ's power to heal both soul and body.</summary>
<keywords>rebirth, prevenient grace, Soul-Physician, paralytic, Matthew 9, forgiveness of sins, spiritual medicine, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Christ as the Physician of Souls, specifically focusing on the internal nature of His healing process as illustrated by the story of the paralytic.</meta>

...reveals in His living Word, which then was the true refreshment and restoration for his sick, wounded soul. Indeed, the other pains of the limbs are as nothing compared to this singular pain, misery, and sickness—namely, when sin, eternal death, and the inscribed curse <note>original: "Maledeyung" — an archaic term for a formal curse or malediction.</note> are stirred up in the conscience, so that the wrath and terror of God's judgment begin to manifest and make a person weary. For this, only this Physician can provide a relief, strengthening, or means of healing, and no other creature, whether in heaven or on earth, can do so.

We can also easily observe here the true art, nature, and manner of the medicine of our souls: namely, that the heavenly Physician always first attacks from within, visiting the heart and beginning to improve it before He makes room and place for outward understanding. He gives the sick person such a purgative <term>purgative: "Purganz," a medicinal substance used to cleanse the body, used here as a metaphor for spiritual purification.</term> that works from the inside out, and thus powerfully takes away all sickness, because all such plagues, sins, or illnesses have their path and origin from within the heart. As our Physician Himself says when He speaks: *Out of the heart come evil thoughts, murder, adultery, fornication, theft, false witness, blasphemy. These are the things*, says the Lord, *that defile a person—that is, make them sick, stained, and leprous before God. Matthew 15, 20.*

Such an inner divine work is proven by the fact that God the Father initially draws the sick to His Son Christ (John 6, 44, 65), whom He sent into this world as a Physician. Whoever then hears and learns it from the Father comes to Christ and allows himself to be helped, so that he may become whole and healthy by means of His lovely medicine. Thus, I say, the Lord begins His divine work of healing in truth and in the very depths of the patient; He stirs, drives, and moves the heart—yes, often his entire innermost being—with prevenient grace <term>prevenient grace: "vorgehender Gnade," the grace that comes before human action to prompt the soul toward God.</term>, and acts from the inside out, not from the outside in, when He wishes to make the whole person whole and healthy. As it is also written here: *He saw their faith*, by which both the sick man and those who carried him are meant. God the Father undoubtedly gave them this faith and thereby drew them to His Son, the Physician Christ, so that they sought Him, recognized Him, learned to trust Him, and became healthy.

For just as the evil spirit begins from within through his seduction and first possesses the human heart—where he initially corrupts the will and the intention toward good, and takes away the love, faith, fear, and obedience of God—when he has thus torn the best out of the heart and breathed into it his filth, lust for sin, suspicion, and <note>original: "nd" — an abbreviation for "und" (and).</note> evil desires...

<summary>This page explains that spiritual healing must begin in the heart rather than the body, using Christ’s cleansing of the soul as a "purgative" for sin. It emphasizes that God draws the faithful to Christ through an internal movement of grace before physical or external healing occurs.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual healing, heart, Christ the Physician, purgative, prevenient grace, faith, original sin, Matthew 15, John 6</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sermon or treatise regarding Christ as the "Physician of Souls," specifically explaining the spiritual sequence of healing using the biblical account of the paralytic from the Gospels.</meta>

...He moves forward, completely spoiling the whole person and acting just like a worm in an apple, which first eats the core as the seed, leaves its waste <note>original: "Koth" — excrement or filth.</note> there, and then moves on to destroy the entire apple. See, in this same way, the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ must begin again from the inside in the healing of a poor human being. He must first cleanse the heart, attacking and driving out the evil spirit in its nest, so that He may take away its weapons. He must drive out unbelief if our limbs are to be freed and used for the service and praise of God. The Lord intended to teach and show us this with the story of the paralytic <term>paralytic: "Gichtbrüchigen," historically referring to one suffering from palsy or paralysis.</term>.

But so that He might even more powerfully demonstrate the strength of His divine medicine—that is, the omnipotence of His healing Word made flesh—and prove Himself as a Physician of Souls, He also performs the outward healing. This was much easier than the inward healing, because it only reaches into this fleeting life, while the inward reaches into eternal life. Therefore, the Gospel continues: *And behold, some of the scribes (when they heard that He forgave the man's sins) said to themselves: "This man blasphemes God; who can forgive sins but God alone?" But when Jesus saw their thoughts, He said: "Why do you think such evil in your hearts? Which is easier to say: 'Your sins are forgiven,' or to say 'Arise and walk'? But that you may know that the Son of Man has power on earth to forgive sins,"* He said to the paralytic: *"Arise, take up your bed and go home."* And he stood up and went home. When the people saw this, they marveled and praised God, who had given such power to men.

With this, we have once again rightly depicted our heavenly Physician Jesus Christ in His work and office: namely, how through the power of His almighty Word in the flesh He first healed the paralytic inwardly in his soul. This was to show that He had not primarily come to earth to heal physical, temporary damage, nor did He wish to shed His blood—and has now shed it—so that He might always heal the body. Rather, He is a Physician of Souls, sent from heaven for the sake of the inner sickness and eternal damage. As He says: *I have come to call sinners to repentance* <note>original: "Buße" — a term for repentance or a turning back to God.</note>. Of this, however, the Pharisees and scribes know nothing true; and although they were most in need of the Physician, few of them are made healthy, for they despise such a Physician and the counsel of God, and in no way wish to hear, know, or accept Him or His medicine.

<summary>This page explains that Christ heals the human heart first to drive out the "nest" of the devil before addressing physical ailments, using the healing of the paralytic as the primary example. <meta>This page continues the theological reflection on Christ as the "Physician of Souls." While the previous page ended with the crowd praising God for the healing of the paralytic, this page applies that biblical narrative to the spiritual life of the individual believer, emphasizing that the "medicine" of Christ—His Word—is still active today. The catchword "d" on the previous page likely anticipated a word like "das" or "die" (the) which might have been part of the concluding sentence of the previous section, though this page begins a new rhetorical application.</meta>

Whoever still hears today how Christ lifts up, comforts, strengthens, and heals this paralytic <term>paralytic: "Gichtbrüchigen," historically referring to one suffering from palsy or paralysis</term>, and recognizes through this that they too are afflicted with the same disease because of sin—indeed, that they are in truth that very same paralytic, whose entire inner being is stiffened by sin and who is entirely incapable of doing good by themselves without grace; if they then only come to the Physician Christ, earnestly reveal and present their damage and disease to Him, take comfort in His grace, help, and mercy, and accept the medicine of His living Word; namely, if they hear in their heart those sweet words from the Lord, which He still speaks forevermore: *Trust, son; your sins are forgiven*; and if they grasp this with faith, then they are still renewed today, gladdened in the sense and spirit of their mind. They will receive inward comfort, strength, and power, so that they may henceforth walk uprightly on the way of the Lord toward His kingdom, until they finally become completely whole and healthy; for the Lord's Word stands forever in heaven, and His faithfulness endures forever and ever, says the Spirit of God in Psalm 119 <note>The author references Psalm 119:89: "Your word, Lord, is eternal; it stands firm in the heavens."</note>.

A fine example of this is also presented to us in the soldier, the centurion <term>centurion: "Hauptmann," a Roman military officer</term> at Capernaum. For although he was a Gentile, he had nevertheless recognized the Physician and the medicine through the preceding grace of God; just as he felt it for all our comfort and, with strong faith, confessed and praised it when he spoke to the Physician Christ: *O Lord, I am not worthy that you should come under my roof, but only speak a word, and my servant will be healed.* The Word of Christ was the medicine and the healing that this centurion desired. To this, however, the Lord replied—He marveled at his faith and said: *Go your way; let it be done for you as you have believed*, and his servant was healed in that same hour.

Now, if this happened to a soldier—a common man of the world, as we might say—to whom the Lord nevertheless gives testimony that He had not found such faith in Israel, then truly no one should ever despair of the Physician Christ (who still pities all the sick just as He did before) or cease to hope for His help. No sick person will ever perish or fail with this skillful, heavenly Physician if they only recognize their sickness and come to Him with the constant earnestness of their heart, seeking Him, pleading with Him, loving Him, and accepting the medicine of His living Word in faith. Even if the sickness be as long-lasting, as sudden, or as deadly as it may, if the sick person only persists in seeking help and comfort from the Physician Christ, they are already helped out of all distress with a single word.

Therefore, it has remained so even in the use of the Holy Sacrament, and when one has wished to become spiritually healthy,

## Chapter 27

### Whereby a person may recognize the effect of the medicine, and that the Physician has taken them into His care.

Having now considered the proof of the heavenly Physician and His mastery, we should also not forget to examine ourselves regarding the medicine and its effect. We must pay attention to whether the Physician has taken us in, whether we are under His care <term>care: "Kur," referring to a systematic course of medical treatment or a "cure" overseen by a doctor</term>, or whether the Physician has left us and we have rejected the medicine, or whether it is working within us, and what it is producing—that is, what in us is being improved, and by how much.

This is experienced in the decrease of the heart’s error and the increase of truth in Christ, of faith, and of a new Christian life; likewise in the dying away of the old man <note>The "old man" (German: "alte Mensch") is a theological term for the sinful human nature that a believer must "put off," according to the Apostle Paul's writings.</note>, his sinful life, essence, lusts, affections, etc. <term>etc.: "ic." is an abbreviation for "et cetera"</term>. When the sick person is convicted of their illness—that is, sin—by the Holy Spirit, is rebuked, weeps, sighs, and bears sorrow; when the heart begins to lift itself toward heaven to Christ and to earnestly concern itself with the knowledge of Him and spiritual things; when one is terrified by the old sinful life; when one keeps God ever more before their eyes and walks in His fear.

Likewise, when the sick person hates their disease—that is, their sinful life—for the Physician hates the disease, and therefore uses His skill to suppress it and make the person healthy; when the sick person also begins to hate their disease, indeed themselves in their sickness, to deny themselves and take up their cross, and to follow the Physician, desiring His medicine and health, then they are in agreement with the Physician. They gladly do what He commands, and the more they recover, the more they love His commandments; indeed, they become dearer to them than many thousands of pieces of gold and silver <margin>Psalm 119</margin> <note>Specifically referencing Psalm 119:72: "The law from your mouth is more precious to me than thousands of pieces of silver and gold."</note>.

<meta>This page continues Chapter 27, expanding on how a believer should interact with Christ as their spiritual Physician and the necessity of confessing "spiritual sickness" (sin) despite Christ already knowing the heart.</meta>

These are certain signs that a person has been accepted by the Physician and that his medicine is beginning to work blessedly; if it does not happen quickly, the sick person should nevertheless wait for the Physician, hope in Him, take notice of His coming, and personally entreat Him to come, even sending others to the Physician on his behalf with prayer, inviting Him and presenting his illness to Him often and many times.

For although the Physician, Christ, knows every sickness—including the origin, cause, and root of all kinds of damage to the body and soul—and is likewise able to heal them all, it is nevertheless required that once one has become aware of the merciful hand of the Physician and His healing plaster <note>original: "Pflaster"; a medicated dressing or ointment applied to a wound.</note>, the sick person should speak with the Physician Christ about his spiritual illness, just as happens with physical doctors. He should reveal and confess it to Him, show Him the unclean boils and festering, pus-filled wounds, truly recognize himself as a sick person, and call upon and pray to the Physician for health. Furthermore, he should frequently hold conversation with the Physician regarding the medicine through faith, with prayer, lamentation, sighs, and so on; he should willingly accept His medicine, rejoice in it, be comforted by it, and after he begins to become healthy, he should always have it at hand. Whenever something of the sickness is stirred up again or a wound breaks open, he should apply the medicine to it, and by faith, smear and heal his wounded soul with it.

To the Physician Christ, we should confess and acknowledge our sickness—that is, sin—not because He does not already know it or understand its cause, for to this Physician the beginning and end of all damage and all human frailty are manifest; He knows them all and well knows what is in man <note>original: "Joh. 2, 25"; referring to John 2:25, "for he himself knew what was in man."</note>. Rather, such confession and acknowledgment happen so that we ourselves may remember our sickness, learning to know it more and more with repentance and sorrow, humbling ourselves before His eyes, admitting our own guilt and lamenting it, so that we also might pay all the more diligent attention to the power of the medicine, to feel it and perceive it. Likewise, we should comfort ourselves in faith with the delight and joy of our hearts regarding our complete health.

Likewise, we should also thank the Physician, praise His medicine and its power, and proclaim His help to others as well, so that they too may hold fast to this Physician and may expect all love, faithfulness, and goodness from Him. This He also requires of us—indeed, He has advised us to do this for our own good, of which much is to be found in the Psalms. It is useful for us to thank the Physician and to have His medicine always before our eyes, never departing from His apothecary <term>apothecary: "Apotheke," a place where medicines are prepared and dispensed; here used metaphorically for the source of divine grace.</term> and cure <term>cure: "Kur," a systematic course of medical treatment overseen by a physician.</term>. All of this, when it happens from the heart, is also a certain sign that the Physician has accepted us into His care.

Thus, when the sick person, having been accepted into grace, has recognized the Physician, felt His medicine, and has been restored to health, so that through the power of the heavenly medicine he may once again come before God into the healthy air and begin to walk in the day—that is, once he has become a believer and a new spiritual person or a Christian—he has the power and authority, through and in such faith, to go to the Physician and to take from His entire pharmacy <note>original: "Apotheke"; in the 17th century, this referred to a place where medicinal recipes were compounded and stored.</note> everything and anything that is necessary for his complete health. Indeed, as often as an infirmity stirs or any harm is found, he can take what he has gathered through faith from this pharmacy and apply it to such harm, binding it, healing it, and mending it again; for he knows where to find the Physician and the medicine at all times. He also knows which prescription <note>original: "Recept"; refers to both the medical formula and the authoritative word of God.</note> the Physician used to help him initially, and that this same medicine always helps and brings comfort. This is the sprinkling <note>A reference to the "sprinkling of the blood of Jesus," a biblical image of purification and atonement.</note> and effect of the blood of Christ in the power of faith, the trust in Christ the Word of Life, the contemplation of Christ’s benefits, and the entrance into God’s sanctuary at the throne of grace, where he then finds the Physician and the medicine, eats and drinks Him <note>A reference to the Eucharist or Holy Communion as a source of spiritual healing.</note>, and through this becomes more and more united with God—that is, he is made spiritual and whole unto complete health.

# Chapter 28.

### On the Service and Servants of the Heavenly Physician Jesus Christ.

Our heavenly Physician, Jesus Christ, also desires to have His appointed servants, service, and ministers, just as other wealthy and proven physicians do; these are the apostles, prophets, evangelists, shepherds <note>Often translated as "pastors" in a modern church context.</note>, and teachers, etc. Their office is to proclaim or call out the aforementioned Physician as their Lord throughout the entire world as the Healer and Savior of all people, as far as is possible; to boast of His faithfulness, love, and mercy which He has toward mankind; to praise Him highly; and to make Him rightly known to all the sick—that is, all poor sinners—with His riches, grace, medicine, and benefits. They are to diligently serve Him in the restoration of health as well as in the recognition of the illness, directing all the sick to Him personally, leading and inviting them gently. For there should be no physical coercion or pressure; nor should any wrath, hatred, envy, or bitterness dwell in the hearts of those who serve the gentle Physician Jesus Christ in the office of healing.

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section where the individual believer’s access to Christ’s spiritual pharmacy was established. Here, the focus shifts to the specific "office" or duty of ministers and apostles, who are described not as the physicians themselves, but as the Physician's assistants or servants.</meta>

Also, their office is this: that they well preserve in their hearts the medicine which Christ Himself gives and distributes—namely the word of salvation, the word of health, and of eternal life—and that they, in their service, faithfully carry it after Him as the Master and Physician as an example to others and bear witness to it. Likewise, they must demonstrate the Physician's powerful medicine within themselves, by which they have been made healthy. They should undertake nothing with the sick without the Physician's counsel, will, and knowledge, but in all things look to Him alone, point to Him alone, and learn to have recourse in Him, whom they should also rightly assist in obedience everywhere as their Lord and Master. They shall not falsify the medicine, nor sell old <term>species<note>In early modern medicine, "species" referred to the individual dry ingredients or raw herbs used to compound a medicinal formula.</note></term> as new, or mix them in. This is a servant’s service, office, labor, and character; therefore, he must be, above all things, faithful, truthful, steadfast, and sincere. He must also be well-informed by the Physician and about the medicine, and rightly know the herbs, waters, oils, and ointments in the pharmacy <note>original: "Apotheke"</note>, just as this Physician desires no other servant, regardless of what he may be called.

Such a servant was the holy Apostle Paul (as well as other apostles, prophets, and evangelists), namely a proven, steadfast servant of the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ, a servant of the New Testament, and a diligent, faithful steward in the service of God. For he writes quite beautifully of his service to all the sick when he says of himself and Timothy: "We preach not ourselves, but (the heavenly Physician) Jesus Christ, that He is the Lord, and we your servants for Jesus' sake." That is to say: we are not the physicians or saviors, but only servants of the one Physician and Savior, Christ, who serve you toward everlasting health, carrying the ointment around among the people and making the Physician and medicine rightly known. For God, who commanded the light to shine out of darkness, has given a bright light into our hearts, so that through us might come the enlightenment of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Jesus Christ. 2 Cor. 4:5-6.

There we see the Physician and Lord Jesus Christ, and likewise God His Father, from whom He received the office and full power of healing, and by whom He was also sealed for that purpose. The servant Paul was to proclaim this by divine command, witnessing it with the Word and a holy life. He was to bring joyful good news from Him to all the sick, being faithful in the service of the Physician and making Him known as his Lord throughout the world. He was permitted to do, speak, or undertake nothing in his service unless the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ worked it through him, as he himself says in Romans 15:18.

# The 29th Chapter.

### That the situation of the servants of the heavenly Physician is not the same in all respects as that of the servants of physical doctors.

Here it must be considered that the situation is not the same in every way for the servants of the heavenly Physician, Christ, as it is for the servants of a physical doctor, who carry their master’s medicine box <note>original: "Büchse"</note>, ointment, syrup, or medicine after him, with which he treats or heals the sick; they also perhaps help to prepare these and often, in the absence of the doctor himself, administer or apply them to the sick. But the ministers of the spiritual Physician are able to do nothing to create or produce the medicine of the soul, nor can they administer it to the sick or provide it on their own; rather, they only point to the Physician, describe His medicine, and serve Him in that capacity.

And although they do, so to speak, carry the medicine box after the Lord in their service, and also make the ointment and medicine known through their holy lives—through which they themselves were first made whole and healthy—the Physician Christ (who is always present and to be found) does not take His medicine from *their* boxes. Nor may they sell, apply, or distribute the ointment or medicine as their own property, any more than they are the Physician Himself; nor are they to be compared to Him in any way, even though they serve the Physician in His office or work of making souls healthy through the outward person <note>This refers to the "outer man" or the physical presence of the minister as a vessel for God's work.</note>.

By the **apothecary** one may here understand the **Holy Spirit**, who must in no way be separated from the Physician Christ in the work of the spiritual healing of the sick person or sinner; just as the **pharmacy** <note>original: "Apotheke"</note> is the **grace of God**. In short, the ministers or servants of this Physician, Christ, certainly have the ointments, oil, or medicine for themselves and to serve the Physician, but not to anoint and heal others on their own. And although they are devoted to the good heavenly Doctor of medicine and His Spirit, the Physician, the medicine, and the entire healing remain free, and are the sole property of the Physician Christ and His Spirit, who heals both with the service and without the service, and not because of the servant's "carrying after."

Likewise, the Physician Christ also first made His servant Paul (as well as others) healthy, and proved the heavenly medicine upon him, so that he might constantly wit- <note>The text cuts off here with the catchword "zeu-", likely for "zeugen," meaning to testify or bear witness.</note>

<summary>This chapter uses the metaphor of a medical practice to explain that while ministers are "servants" who carry Christ's message, they possess no innate power to heal souls; the Holy Spirit is the true Apothecary, and God's Grace is the Pharmacy.</summary>
<keywords>Christ the Physician, Holy Spirit, Apothecary, Pharmacy of Grace, Ministry, St. Paul, Spiritual Healing, Salvation</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy of the Christian ministry as an assistant to a physician. The previous page ended mid-word ("apothecar-") describing the ministers as "apothecary-servants" or assistants who handle the medicine but are not the healers themselves.</meta>

-servants, and could serve the Physician with a healthy, free heart; the servant Paul certainly possessed health and medicine, ointment or oil—that is, the Holy Spirit in his heart—first for himself, for his own eternal health and salvation, and then to serve the Physician faithfully in that regard. But that he was the Physician himself, or that he could have presumed to heal souls in the Physician's stead—that is not the case; his faithful service did not allow for it, as it would have been shameful and detrimental to the office of the Physician Jesus Christ.

Therefore, although Paul (as well as other apostles, prophets, evangelists, and true servants of Christ and His Spirit) was made healthy by the Physician and received the medicine to serve faithfully for himself, they nevertheless point no one to themselves as those who could heal souls, or as if they possessed the medicine and could help others with it. They point no one to themselves, nor to their ministry, nor to any external thing through which to obtain salvation and health; instead, they point all people to this Physician who made them healthy—namely, to Christ who helped them. For they well know that only a single Physician has been ordained by God the Lord to heal the corrupted soul, whose medicine alone heals—indeed, is always healing and powerful. They, however, serve this Physician and lure all sick, poor sinners to this one Physician, whom they themselves have faithfully felt and experienced as powerful, skilled, and expert in the art of medicine. Thus Paul did, along with all other appointed servants of the Physician Christ, as his writings and those of several others still testify.

Concerning such service and the office of a servant, he also wrote to the Corinthians, for he could not bear that any of the honor, glory, and majesty of the heavenly Physician Christ be diminished, or that the Lord's honor be given or attributed to the servants. For this reason, he clearly distinguishes the Lord's work and mastery from his own service and says: *For when one says, "I follow Paul," and another, "I follow Apollos," are you not worldly?* <note>original: "fleischlich," often translated as "carnal" or "fleshly," referring to people acting on human impulse rather than spiritual wisdom.</note> *Who is Paul? Who is Apollos? They are servants through whom you have become believers* (1 Corinthians 3:4–5). That is to say, they are neither lords nor givers of living faith, nor of the health of souls, but only servants through whose ministry you were led to the Physician Christ and made healthy by Him.

"We are servants," Paul continues in verses 6 and 7, "and that as the Lord has given to each one. I planted, Apollos watered, but God gave the growth <note>original: "Gedeihen," referring to the spiritual increase or prosperity of the soul.</note>; so then neither he who plants is anything, nor he who waters, but God who gives the growth." But what is this "growth" other than the health of the soul, which comes from the forgiveness of sins—

In this, the appointed servant Paul serves externally; he proclaims this, leading people to the Physician as the reigning King of Grace, Christ, making His praise, diligence, and skill pleasant and great. Indeed, he acts among the sick as if he knows nothing else but to speak of his Lord and Physician: namely, that through the cross and suffering, He became both Physician and medicine for us in His blood, as he said shortly before: **I decided that I would know nothing among you except Jesus Christ the Crucified**, <note>A translation of 1 Corinthians 2:2.</note> in whom the knowledge of the illness, the medicine, and full health blessedly begins, as was heard previously.

# Chapter 30.

### Concerning how the Christian Church and its members stand in regard to medicine and health, etc.

And just as a proven personal physician in a city often has many and various sick people whom he has taken into his care, and yet intends to heal them from one pharmacy; <note>The original German "Apotheke" refers to the apothecary's shop, the source of all prepared remedies.</note> even though every sick person in the city remains here and there, each in his own place and lying ill; so that the physician must visit and attend to each one in his own place, first in person and then also through his servants or assistants.

It is very much the same with Christ, the heavenly Physician; He has His sick scattered throughout the whole world, lying here and there among all peoples and in all lands. Before Him, however, they all lie as if in one city. He visits each one through His grace itself, and has them all directed by His servants to one pharmacy, to remain constantly under Him and His care. Although they are often physically scattered and far from one another, and often do not know each other, they all know the Physician and His medicine, through which they are helped. Through this, they are also baptized by one Spirit into one kind of spiritual health in one body or community under the head, Christ, and are made to drink of one Spirit, according to 1 Corinthians 12:13. From this nation of the aforementioned sick, the Physician Jesus Christ gathers for Himself a company of the healthy, who shall [inherit] with Him the kingdom of heaven...

according to the health, spirit, and grace they have received; their sins are forgiven them through Christ and the Holy Spirit is given, which is a perfect medicine for the soul. Whatever evil, weakness, or sickness remains in their "Old Adam" flesh <note>A theological term referring to the fallen, sinful human nature that remains even after conversion.</note> is not counted against them for the sake of Christ and his Spirit. For they do not walk according to the flesh in their former blindness, but according to the Spirit in the light. In this Spirit, they put to death the works of the flesh and here become ever healthier, remaining under the treatment of the **Heavenly Physician** while they live here. Afterward, however, in the completion and expansion of the whole person in the resurrection of the flesh, they shall then become perfectly healthy in body, soul, and spirit, and be eternally blessed.

Paul speaks to the Ephesians about such a Church and the spiritual health of its members, as it already stands before God, when he says: That Christ is the head of the community and the Savior—indeed, the Helper and Physician—of his body; and afterward: That he loved the community and gave himself (out of great love) for her unto death, for her redemption and healing, so that he might sanctify her (with his blood) and has cleansed her through the washing of water in the Word of life, that he might present the community to himself in splendor, not having a spot or wrinkle or any such thing, but that she should be holy and without blemish, Ephesians 5:25–27.

Certain teachers, who do not distinguish between the new and old man nor know the Christian Church, want to interpret such a passage as referring to the *triumphant Church* <note>The "Church Triumphant" refers to the redeemed in heaven, while the "Church Militant" refers to believers currently living on earth.</note> after it has been fully realized there; they view it as something in the future. However, the text itself convicts us of this incorrect interpretation, and it is proven therefrom that Paul is speaking of the *militant Church*—namely, how she and her members are here in this time generated from the flesh of Christ and from his bones, and are also built up through the ministry of the apostles, prophets, evangelists, shepherds, and teachers (of which he speaks in the 4th chapter), being holy and without blemish; such were the Ephesians and all true believers.

Paul says: Christ Jesus, the heavenly Physician and Savior, has sanctified, washed, and cleansed the community and its members—namely, from their impurity and the spiritual sickness of sins. Why? Well, so that they should walk to the praise of his grace and glory, holy and without blemish, as is fitting for the children of God: that from now on they do not serve sin unto death, but righteousness unto life, as he also writes in that place, that the community should be subject to Christ. On this, one should look at the 5th and 6th chapters of Romans. ...to inherit and possess: for Jesus was to die for the people, and not for the people alone, but that he might bring together the children of God who were scattered abroad, John 11:52.

This is now the **holy Christian Church**, the community of the people of God, the bride and the body of Christ; it is the number of the elect <note>The "elect" refers to those chosen by God for salvation, a central concept in Reformation theology.</note> (as Augustine describes the Church) <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian who defined the Church as a spiritual body of believers rather than just an earthly institution.</note> scattered here and there throughout the whole world, yet gathered into one community or church in the unity of the Spirit and faith through Christ. In this gathering, they are healed and made whole. This Church is truly known only to Christ, her King, Lord, and Head, just as she is governed by him and grows, is nourished, and is preserved by his spiritual influence.

We are not speaking here of the Christian Church and its members as they will appear before God according to His decree and the fully prepared assembly in eternal life. In that state, her members will no longer need any medicine, for all clinging illnesses will be taken away, and there will be nothing but perfect, eternal health. There, all tears will be wiped from their eyes, and God will be all in all. Some call this the **triumphant church** <note>The "Church Triumphant" refers to believers who have died and are now in heaven, having "triumphed" over sin and death.</note>, which is not our subject now. Instead, we are speaking of how the Church stands here on earth: how she is generated in Christ by his Spirit, joined together in him as the Head into one body, and gathered and built up through the ministry of the apostles and servants of Christ. Her members have been healed by the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ, who continually adds more to her number, healing them and making them whole and blessed.

The holy Christian Church, in which we believe, is scattered throughout the whole world and known to Christ alone. In her, the children of God are gathered and healed or made whole; indeed, they are already healthy before God because of the rebirth in the spirit, the mind, and their newborn hearts through true faith. However, they are still partly sick because of the clinging flesh and indwelling sin, for which reason they always need the Physician as long as they live. They are healthy according to the **new man** <note>The "new man" is the part of the believer regenerated by the Holy Spirit, while the "old man" refers to the remaining sinful nature.</note> born of God, but sick according to the old man, who nevertheless decreases day by day and is renewed so that they become increasingly healthy.

Briefly, if one wishes to look at it before God, the members of the body of Christ—which body is the community or Church—are without a spot or wrinkle or any such thing, <note>original: "Eph. 5, 27"</note> Ephesians 5:27, because of the divine skill of the Physician, his knowledge, and their faith. Almighty God no longer looks at them according to their old man—that is, according to what is still sick and unhealed in them—but according to the new man...

Those are i[n error] who interpret the aforementioned saying of Paul, where he speaks of the purified, ho[ly], and blameless Church, as referring only to the future world [for] the saints in heaven. Such people view the Christian Church he[re] on earth only according to those who call themselves Christians, or by the [ex]ternal gathering of those who participate in worship, teaching, [and] the sacraments. They do not look at the Church according to the foundation of its spi[ritu]al essence—how it stands before God—nor [that] Christ is her head and they are his body, just as the body is joined to the He[ad] and is genera[ted] from the Head through the Holy Spirit. For in this way and no other, as just heard, ho[ly] Scripture speaks of the Christian Church, as in the Epistles of Paul, [es]pecially 1 Corinthians 12, 2 Corinthians 11, Colossians 2, Ephesians 4 and 5, and like[wise] also in the Revelation <note>original: *Apoc.* (Apocalypsis)</note>, in the Song of Solomon, and Psalm 45, as is clearly to be fou[nd.]

Consequently, [such] hypocrites are also often held by the ministers of the Church of Christ to be [he]althy, righteous members of the Church and children of God, until they betray themselves, so that their hypocrisy comes to [light], and they are afterwards distinguish[ed] and put to shame by Christ, the Physician and King.

The aforementioned parables should be understood as referring to such hypocrites who mingle [with] the people of God, and not to open sinners. These hypocrites do not even want [to be] sick; they puff [themselves up] on account of their works or ceremonies, and in their conceited mind consider themselves healthy and righteous. Ho[wever], before God they are the very sickest of all. Because of their spiritual pri[de] and reckless judgment, they are the most wretched sinners. They are n[ot] mixed among those who have become healthy through Christ the Physician, or [who] still constantly need his medicine on account of their walk in the flesh; rather, they rema[in] entirely deprived of the influence of the Head [of] the Church and of the healing medicine of Christ.

<meta>This page continues the argument that those who consider themselves "pure" without the aid of Christ are in error. It contrasts self-righteous hypocrites with the true Church, which acknowledges its ongoing "sickness" (sin) while relying on Christ for healing.</meta>

...because they do not cling to the Physician <note>original: *Arzt*; a common Reformation-era metaphor for Christ</note>, nor make use of his cure and medicine, but rather wish to help themselves or, in their own estimation, believe they have already been helped, considering themselves no longer sinners, but righteous.

In sum, the Christian Church is holy and, as the noble bride of Christ, entirely pure and glorious before the face of God; she may well have been sick and unclean, but the Son of God, Jesus Christ, has sacrificed himself for her, cleansed her through his blood, sanctified her, and made her healthy. Although the members of the Church—on account of the human nature <note>original: *Fleisches* (flesh)</note> still clinging to them and the indwelling sin—are yet somewhat sick, frail, and, as James <note>original: *Jacobus*; referring to James 3:2</note> says, fail or fall into guilt in many ways, and for that reason daily require the Physician, the Healer, and the Forgiver of sins until they are fully led out [of this life]: yet, before God and according to his divine providence, they are already healthy through faith in his grace. They are cleansed with the blood of Christ and sanctified in the Word of Life, with which Christ the High Priest also daily sprinkles them <note>an allusion to the Old Testament ritual of purification</note> for their salvation and the increase of their health; indeed, according to the word of the Lord, they are entirely clean, except that because of their walk in the flesh they must still always wash their feet (John 13:10) and pray with all the saints of God in the Our Father: *Forgive us our debts*; as those who know with Paul that nothing good dwells in their flesh, and that it is often found rebellious against the Spirit of God; as those who also say with John: *If we say we have no sin* (that is, if we wish to be here before ourselves not sick but entirely healthy, as if we no longer needed Christ the Physician) *we deceive ourselves and the truth is not in us; but if we confess our sins, he is faithful and just* (in his promise) *to forgive us our sins and cleanse us from all unrighteousness* <note>original: *Untugend*</note>—that is, he helps us from all the clinging spiritual sickness of sin (1 John 1:6–7). Likewise, all Christians have sin, but they should not for that reason commit sin; therefore, whoever is not healed by Christ the Physician, nor practices the works of a spiritually healthy person, should by no means be considered a member of the Christian Church.

From such a gathering of healthy members of the Christian Church, whom the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ has helped, he chooses for himself special ministers or servants, who make him known to everyone as the spiritual Physician of souls, and who also through their teaching and life bring many to the Physician; for they know that all people are by nature sick, corrupted, and frail—that is, full of sin—and therefore all have need of this Physician. There, then, the servants of the Physician may well comfort and promise health to all who come to Christ the Physician with faith; the servants themselves are not able <note>original: *vermögen*</note> to give it, nor to heal the wounds of the souls,
