<meta>This page continues the discussion on the role of ministers as servants to Christ, the "Great Physician." It transitions from describing the sick state of the Church to how the Gospel serves as a spiritual medicine administered through "earthen vessels" like the Apostle Paul.</meta>

therefore they kindly direct all people to Christ the Physician; on the other hand, they do not withhold from those who despise the Physician their condemnation and the harm that must ultimately arise for them from it; thus being long-suffering, kind, and patient toward the sick. For we (says Paul) preach not ourselves, but Jesus Christ, that he is the Lord, and we are your servants for Jesus' sake, 2 Corinthians 4:5. They thus apply themselves so that some might be won and saved, because they know that no sick person can help themselves.

Let this be said, following the previous section, concerning the service of praise, honor, and the announcement of the Physician and heavenly Master, Christ, whose servant Paul was; who, in short, desires that the restoration of health be attributed neither to him nor to anyone else but to the one Physician, Christ himself. He indicates that he only serves, and where the Physician spiritually treats and heals, he speaks of it outwardly, thus exhorting the sick to run to the Physician, and encouraging and awakening them to seek him.

Saint Paul, speaking in the Holy Spirit in 2 Corinthians 4, also speaks of the medicine <note>original: *Arzeney*; refers here to the healing power of the Gospel</note> and of the grace of the Gospel, and says what he does and undertakes in this regard—that he carries it about at the command of the heavenly Physician—where he says: "But we have such a treasure," namely the ointment and medicine of Christ, with which we and all wounded consciences are healed, all weak hearts comforted, and all sick people, in body and soul, can become healthy unto eternal life; "such a precious treasure," he says, "we have in earthen vessels <note>A biblical metaphor for human frailty and mortality</note>, so that the surpassing power may be of God (the heavenly Physician, Christ) and not from us." There we see clearly that the medicine is not the servant Paul’s, nor does it come from his own ability; therefore, the power of health—that is, righteousness and salvation—does not come from the servant into the heart and soul of the sick person, so that no flesh may boast; rather, it proceeds without intermediary from God out of Christ, who is the head of the congregation, the wellspring of health, of salvation, and of life; then it grows and drives upward again to the praise of God the heavenly Father into eternal life.

But so that this may also be understood regarding the heavenly medicine for sick mankind, it is proven by what Paul says shortly before in 2 Corinthians 2:14–17: "But thanks be to God, who always gives us victory in Christ, and reveals the fragrance <note>original: *Geruch*; often translated as 'savour' or 'aroma' in older Bibles</note> of his knowledge in all places; for we are to God a sweet fragrance of Christ, both among those who are saved (or made healthy) and among those who are lost; to the latter, a fragrance of death leading to death, but to the former, a fragrance of life (that is, the restoration of health) leading to life; for we are not like..."

<summary>This page uses medical metaphors to describe the Gospel, portraying Christ as the true Physician and the Apostles as servants who carry His "medicine" in "earthen vessels." It emphasizes that spiritual healing and righteousness come solely from God's power, not the human minister.</summary>
<keywords>Christ as Physician, spiritual medicine, 2 Corinthians 4, earthen vessels, fragrance of Christ, salvation as health, Paul the Apostle</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Apostle Paul’s ministry, specifically contrasting those who "corrupt the word" with true servants who act as assistants to Christ, the Great Physician. It concludes the thought from the previous page regarding the "treasure in earthen vessels."</meta>

...quite many who corrupt the word; but as out of sincerity and as from God, in Christ we speak (of such heavenly medicine of souls) before the face of God.

In short, Christ himself heals in the Spirit, who is indeed much more present to us with his grace than all ministers can be; as it is also written in the Psalm: *The Lord is near to all who call upon him, to all who call upon him in truth*, Psalm 145:18. Thus, the true knowledge of God and Christ according to the Spirit—which Paul serves and through his ministry works upon the outer person, yet gives nothing inwardly—such life-giving, healing knowledge is the ointment and medicine <note>original: *Arzeney*; the author uses medical metaphors to describe spiritual salvation</note>. For it brings health, a new spirit, heart, courage, mind, and eternal life, John 17:3.

Thus one now thoroughly understands: although the appointed servant has received much potent restorative, divine strength, and various gifts from Christ the Physician out of the heavenly pharmacy <term>Apotheke: a storehouse of medicines; here, the spiritual source of grace</term>, both for his own health and to serve others with it—and thus in speaking carries the medicine after his Lord, the Physician Jesus Christ, and a strong, pleasant fragrance is about him—nonetheless, the medicine should not properly be sought from the servant, nor in his word or ministry by the sick person, but from the Lord and Physician himself. The servant should point the sick person toward the highway to heaven at the right hand of God, because he is unable to put the medicine into anyone's heart or pour it into others. Rather, the Holy Spirit himself does so, through the ministry of the servant or without the ministry, in his freedom to whomever he wills, 1 Corinthians 12:11. Wherever he himself is not present at the ministry of healing, certainly nothing is accomplished before God for the sick person; no improvement follows, everything is dead and in vain, no matter what the servant does or how he does it.

He, the Lord and heavenly Physician Jesus Christ, wants to apply the plaster <note>original: *Pflaster*; a medicinal dressing or poultice applied to a wound</note> to the deadly wound of the souls himself and make them whole and healthy. This should be shown to the sick for their comfort, since no one can reach the depths of the heart, the bottom of the soul, or the secrets of the conscience, except God in Christ through his living Word itself.

Therefore, Paul speaks in the aforementioned places that the power and strength belong to God and not to the minister; that the fragrance of the ointment with which we are healed is Christ's and not his own; as he also says elsewhere: *The grace of God in me has not been in vain, but I have labored much more than they all*; then he immediately softens such speech and says: *yet not I, but the grace of God which is with me*, 1 Corinthians 15:10.

<meta>Continuity from page 85: The text concludes the argument that spiritual healing comes directly from Christ rather than through the person of the minister. Catchword: Salvation/Healing.</meta>

Therefore, in the ministry, one should not direct the sick toward external <unclear>prescriptions</unclear>, people, ceremonies, or church customs, <unclear>as if</unclear> health were to be fetched and relied upon there. Instead, one <unclear>should</unclear> direct them through all of these to the heavenly Physician, Christ—to His name and grace—and likewise to the medicine of His living <unclear>Word</unclear>, Spirit, blood, and flesh, and to the complete knowledge of Him. This is done so that the sick are not deceived nor disturbed in their health, and so that they do not buy or accept a written, external <unclear>medicine</unclear> in place of a heavenly, spiritual medicine. It also ensures <unclear>that</unclear> the office of <unclear>healing</unclear> in the Holy Spirit remains purely with the Physician and Lord Jesus Christ, and that all skill, <unclear>honor</unclear>, and mastery in the medication of corrupted humanity is <unclear>attributed</unclear> and given solely to Him, as Psalm 115:1 <unclear>says:</unclear> *Not unto us, O Lord, not unto us, but to your name give the honor, for your mercy and truth's sake.* <note>The author uses medical language to argue that religious rituals are merely tools, while the actual "cure" for the soul is Christ himself.</note>

# The 31st Chapter.

### How the heavenly medicine is witnessed in Holy Scripture and named according to its nature and various properties, as well as by various names.

Regarding the aforementioned heavenly medicine, which the appointed servants of God proclaim to His congregation—carrying it after the Physician, Christ, <unclear>in</unclear> their speech and serving Him therein, etc.—Paul also speaks elsewhere, where he calls it the Word of Salvation <note>original: *Wort des Heils*; in German, *Heil* carries the dual meaning of "salvation" and "healing," which reinforces the medical metaphor</note>. This is because the sick soul and the wounded conscience are <unclear>healed</unclear> by it and become healthy through it. For in Acts 13:26, he preaches to the Jews thus: *Men and dear brothers, children of the family of Abraham, and those among you who fear God, to you the word of this salvation is sent,* etc. Here, one must first note to whom this medicine is given or sent: namely, to the children of the faithful Abraham, among both Jews and Gentiles, in those who fear God. These are they who are ordained for eternal life and belong to the Kingdom of God; the "rightly evangelical sick" <note>Those who recognize their spiritual "illness" or need for God</note> who feel their sin or the sickness of their souls, and have truly felt the venomous bite of the ancient serpent <note>A reference to the devil and the fall of man in Eden</note> in their flesh. They recognize themselves and sigh and long for the Physician and the medicine. These are the good-hearted, gentle people who have a true fear of God and carry sorrow for their sin. For *the fear of the Lord is a fountain of life, that one may avoid the snares of death,* Proverbs 14:27. And again: *Those who fear the Lord will prepare their hearts,* namely for the Word of Salvation which medicines them, *and will humble themselves before him.*

<summary>This page argues that spiritual healing should be sought through Christ's grace and Word rather than external ceremonies, defining "Salvation" as a heavenly medicine for the soul. It identifies the "rightly sick"—those who recognize their own sin—as the proper recipients of this divine remedy.</summary>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Christ as the "Heavenly Physician" and His Word as the "Medicine," specifically through the lens of the Apostle Paul’s sermon in Acts 13. It maintains the medical allegory for spiritual salvation established on the previous pages.</meta>

-> ~ ~ ~ ~ ~ <-

Thus, the sermon of Paul continues: And we, he says, proclaim to you the promise (namely of salvation, life, and eternal health) that was made to our fathers, that God has fulfilled this same promise for us, their children, in that he has raised up Jesus (the <term>heavenly Physician</term><note>The author uses "Physician" as a title for Christ to emphasize His role in healing the soul's sickness of sin.</note> of our souls), as it is written in the first Psalm: *You are my Son; today* (namely, in the resurrection from the dead) *have I begotten you* etc. <note>original: "Du biſt mein Sohn; heute... habe ich dich gebohren." While the text cites the "first Psalm," this verse is found in Psalm 2:7 in modern Bibles.</note> For this reason, our Lord Christ has also been called the firstborn from the dead, as he has joyfully brought forth that life and eternal health for which all the patriarchs long waited through his resurrection. Thereafter, he concludes everything in a summary of the Physician and the medicine, of the sickness and the health, when he says: *Be it known unto you therefore, men and dear brothers, that through this Jesus is preached unto you the forgiveness of sins, and forgiveness of all things from which you could not be justified in the law of Moses; but whoever believes in him is made righteous in him,* says the Holy Spirit through Paul in Acts 13:38-39.

Thus, we have the entire content of this divine transaction briefly contained in Paul's short sermon—though in different words that nonetheless mean the same thing—encompassing both the heavenly Physician Jesus Christ and the medicine of his living Word, who is both God and man. He also reports on the sickness of the soul, which is sin, disobedience toward God, and all vice; thereafter, he establishes health—that is, the forgiveness of sins, life, and righteousness. All of this is proclaimed in the ministry of Paul, as it is centered on the Physician, Christ, and should be sought and received in him through faith. For neither Moses nor his law (much less the teachings or laws of other men) is able to heal the sickness of sin; rather, Jesus Christ alone is the Physician, and his Word—which is spirit and life and the power of God—is the heavenly medicine. Through this, we are released from the wounds of sin, healed in our souls, and made righteous, joyful, healthy, and fit for the inheritance of the kingdom of heaven.

Therefore, I say that the aforementioned heavenly medicine of Jesus Christ was not called the **Word of Salvation** by Paul in vain, because it inwardly heals the wounded conscience and makes the person pious and righteous. For this reason, he also calls such spiritual medicine in the letters to Titus and Timothy the **healthy, wholesome doctrine** and the **wholesome word** of our Lord and God Jesus Christ; namely, that through this ointment and medicine of the blood of Christ, the broken heart is bound up and the sick soul is made healthy. Furthermore, he calls the aforementioned medicine the **Word of Consolation**; for what can be more comforting to a sick person than that which alleviates his pain—indeed, that which brings him eternal health?

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical allegory of salvation, specifically interpreting the writings of the Apostles Paul and Peter. It connects the "Word of Reconciliation" and the "Gospel" to a healing medicine that restores the soul from the "sickness" of sin.</meta>

...bestows or brings through the Holy Spirit? He also calls the aforementioned medicine in 2 Corinthians 5:18, 19 the **Word of Reconciliation**, because it reconciles the rebellious, disobedient human being with the living God and grants fellowship with Him in everlasting divine health. "All this is from God," says Paul, "who has reconciled us to Himself" (that is, who has healed us in Himself) "and has given us the office that preaches reconciliation; for God was in Christ, reconciling the world to Himself, and does not count their sin" (that is, their soul's disobedience, sickness, and transgression) "against them, and has established among us the Word of Reconciliation." He has sealed us for this, given us the Holy Spirit, and made us capable, so that we should publicly proclaim such medicine to you, serving the Physician and pointing toward Him.

Thus we are now **ambassadors**, says Paul, in Christ's stead, for God exhorts through us; so we now pray in Christ's stead: **Be reconciled to God!** It is as if he wanted to say: Christ the Physician no longer speaks or acts for Himself externally, and yet He desires that your <term>outward man</term><note>The "outward man" refers to the physical body and the external, sensory life of a person, as opposed to the "inward man" or the soul.</note> also be informed of and perceive the medicine of God and His healing; therefore, He has chosen us for this office, that we should serve Him from the outside for your recovery. Oh, let Christ treat you with medicine through His Spirit and become healthy, etc. Following this, a truly beautiful passage follows at the conclusion, where he says: "For He," namely God the Father, "has made Him" (His beloved Son), "who knew no sin"—that is, no sickness, disobedience, or transgression in Himself—"to be sin for us, so that we might become a righteousness, a health that is valid before God, in Him," namely in Christ our Lord. Whoever rightly considers the mentioned text in the grace of Christ will soon come to know the heavenly Physician and medicine more closely.

Likewise, in Romans 1:16, Paul also calls such medicine of the soul the **Gospel of Christ** and says: "that it is the power of God unto salvation" (unto salvation, he says, that is, for recovery and <term>blessedness</term><note>The German word *Seeligkeit* encompasses both "blessedness" and the ultimate "salvation" of the soul.</note>) to everyone who believes in it. It is also for this reason that he further calls the aforementioned medicine in Romans 10:9 the **Word of Faith**, because it must be taken and received into the heart and soul from the Physician Christ through faith alone, and also be felt and experienced. Through this medicine, the believing person is <term>purged</term><note>original: "purgiret." The author uses this medical term for a cleansing of the internal system to describe the spiritual purification of the heart.</note> in his heart (Acts 15:9), cleansed in his soul, healed in his conscience (Hebrews 9:14), and becomes an entirely different person—truly healthy, new, and **born again** to eternal life. Concerning this, Peter says: "Love one another with a pure heart, as those who are born again, not of corruptible but of incorruptible seed, namely from the living Word of God, which remains forever" (1 Peter 1:22–23).

Note how Peter there calls the heavenly medicine of the living Word a **seed**, which falls into the believing heart, through which one is born again and brought from death to life; for it is certain, as said before, that whatever the mentioned heavenly medicine effects in the believing person, it is essentially that itself: it is health itself, righteousness, power, strength, peace, joy, life, and eternal salvation. All this, along with countless heavenly riches, it also effects in the Christian-believing soul; it creates new, healthy heavenly people who are not minded or natured according to corrupt blood and flesh, but according to God and His Spirit—something that no other plaster or herb, no element or sign, indeed no creature in heaven or on earth is capable of doing.

There are also more names in the Holy Scriptures by which the heavenly medicine is called, such as **B r e a d**, the true bread of the children of God, which drives away the hunger of the souls and strengthens the weakness of our heart. Of this bread, the <note>original: "Cananäische Weiblein"</note> Canaanite woman desired only a crumb for her and her daughter's health; of this bread the Prophet David also says that bread strengthens the heart of man, Psalm 104:15. And this is the bread that comes from heaven, so that whoever eats of it does not die but lives for eternity. John 6:58.

Likewise, the heavenly medicine is also called **W a t e r**, a water of grace and life that Christ gives, by which the sick, fainting souls are refreshed, quenched, and made healthy. Of this the Lord also says to the Samaritan woman: whoever drinks of the water that I give him will never thirst, but the water that I give him will become in him a spring of water welling up into eternal life, John 4:14. To this healing water, the Spirit of God in the Prophet Isaiah also invites all sick, fainting souls: "Come, all you who are thirsty, come to the waters," Isaiah 55:1, and Revelation 22:17 similarly: "Let the one who is thirsty come; and let the one who wishes take the water of life freely." And everything shall be made healthy and live wherever this stream flows. Ezekiel 47:8, 12.

Likewise, this medicine is also called **F i r e**: the divine fire, which consumes the internal sickness of the souls—that is, sin with its evil lusts—and purifies the heart. Of this, the Physician Jesus Christ says: "I have come to set a fire on the earth, and how I wish it were already burning!" And Malachi 3:2-3: "He is like a refiner's fire <note>original: "Feuer eines Goldschmieds"</note> and like launderers' soap; he will sit and melt and purify the silver; he will purify the children of Levi and refine them like silver."

<summary>This page illustrates the "heavenly medicine" of the Word through three major biblical allegories: Bread that nourishes the soul, Water that quenches spiritual thirst, and Fire that purifies the heart from sin.</summary>

...and gold, which is then the first and initial effect of the medicine in the <term>Word of the Cross</term> <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 1:18; the author views the message of Christ’s sacrifice as a transformative power that "refines" the soul like gold.</note>, through which the kingdom of sin in the soul-sick person is subdued and destroyed.

Furthermore, this heavenly medicine is also called **Wood** in Holy Scripture: the Wood of Life <note>The German "Holz" is translated here as Wood, though it refers specifically to the "Tree of Life."</note>, regarding which Ezekiel 47:12 [says] that the fruit of this tree shall serve for food and its leaves for medicine; and Revelation 2:7: "To him who overcomes, I will give to eat from the Wood of Life, which is in the paradise of God." And who can recount all the names of this manifold divine wealth and of the fragrant oil, ointment, and balsam of the heavenly medicine, with which the merciful, kind God—out of pure love and grace through the physician Jesus Christ, His Son, our Lord—heals and makes healthy our very poor, corrupted humans in soul, heart, and mind, indeed in the whole person? He has graciously promised to bring us thereby into the fellowship of Himself and His kingdom. He also has heartfelt compassion for our ruin and for us miserable, sick people when we despise such medicine and do not wish to be helped; regarding this, Jeremiah 8:21–22 [says]: "I am heartily grieved that my people are so ruined; I mourn and behave poorly. Is there then no <term>balm in Gilead</term> <note>Gilead was famous in the ancient world for its healing resins; here it serves as a metaphor for spiritual healing that is available but ignored.</note>, or is there no physician there? Why then is the daughter of my people not healed?"

# Chapter 32.

### Concerning Holy Scripture, how useful and serviceable it is to recognize the heavenly Physician and medicine through faith.

Since we have now spoken and heard of the various names of the heavenly medicine—that the eternal living Word of God, as well as the human nature of Christ in the Word and the name of the Lord Jesus, as well as the Holy Spirit and the grace of God, along with many other names, are recounted here and elsewhere. And in summary, that Christ Jesus, with all His riches, is entirely the Savior, Physician, and medicine of our souls: there is then a question regarding Holy Scripture, whether it might not also be called a medicine of the soul. To this we answer with a distinction: That the Holy Scripture, both of the New and Old Testament, spoken by the Spirit of God and written at God's command and impulse by holy men for teaching, reproof, correction, training, and instruction, is therefore highly useful to all Christian believers, just as it serves for the knowledge of Christ in faith. We should gladly hear it and gladly read it,

<summary>The author concludes a list of metaphors for divine healing (Wood of Life, Balm of Gilead) and begins a new chapter discussing whether the Bible itself should be considered "medicine" or a guide to the "Physician."</summary>
<keywords>heavenly medicine, Tree of Life, Jesus Christ as Physician, Balm of Gilead, Holy Scripture, spiritual healing, Word of the Cross</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "heavenly medicine" by distinguishing between the Bible as a record and Christ as the actual healing power. It emphasizes that while Scripture points to the cure, it is not the cure itself.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<sig>M 2</sig>

...exercise ourselves therein through the grace of God and concern ourselves with it; that, in short, we hold Holy Scripture in all honor, that we should always love it as a dear, worthy treasure above all other writings on earth; yet it is nonetheless neither the soul’s Physician nor its medicine, for it cannot of itself make anyone healthy, whole, or blessed. This is sufficiently demonstrated by the scribal Jews in the time of Christ, and even today. For such an office belongs alone to the heavenly Physician, Jesus Christ our Savior and <term>Redeemer</term> <note>German: *Seligmacher*, literally "one who makes blessed" or "saves."</note> in the Holy Spirit, to whom the Father has given full authority and has also sealed Him for this purpose: that He Himself should work the health and <term>quickening</term> <note>German: *Lebendigmachung*, the act of bringing the spiritually dead to life.</note> of our souls through His Spirit, body, flesh, and blood. This the Lord Himself also teaches and distinguishes when He speaks thus to the Jews: "You search the Scriptures, for you think you have life in them, and it is they which testify of Me, and you will not come to Me, that you might have life (that is, the true health of your souls)," John 5:39–40.

There we see clearly what the office of Holy Scripture is, and also what the sole office of Jesus Christ is—the heavenly Physician and Son of God, who alone, as He says Himself, is able to give eternal life (that is, the soul's health) through His Word (which is spirit and life) and to distribute it to all believers. Therefore, Holy Scripture certainly says who the Physician is and what the medicine is, just as it speaks of the sick and the sickness. <term>Likewise</term> <note>German: *Item*, a common Latin term used in lists or to introduce a related point.</note>, it also describes how the heavenly Christ was promised, what He should work and accomplish through His medicine among sick humanity; how the process of healing occurs—of such things Holy Scripture testifies perfectly. But it is not the medicine itself. Therefore, it may no more be accepted or essentially regarded as the heavenly medicine of the soul than the servant may be mistaken for the Physician, Christ.

Of this we have a fine figure and <term>prefiguration</term> <note>German: *Fürbildung*, a biblical "type" or foreshadowing of a future spiritual reality.</note> in 2 Kings 4:31. There, the servant Gehazi could accomplish nothing with the prophet's staff toward the quickening of the Shunammite's boy, but rather the prophet Elisha himself had to go down with the Shunammite and make her child alive and healthy with his breath. Christ our Elisha, the true heavenly Physician, had to descend from heaven Himself and make the poor sinner alive; neither servant nor staff, neither prophet nor Scripture, neither Moses nor the Law was able to accomplish such a thing. He must also today personally enliven our dead spirit and soul through His quickening breath, if we are to become healthy, pure, holy, and partakers of the divine eternal life.

<summary>This page distinguishes between the Bible as a testimony of the truth and Christ as the actual living healer, using the story of Elisha and Gehazi as an allegory for the limitations of the Law versus the power of the Savior.</summary>

Therefore, just as the Shunammite woman <note>A reference to 2 Kings 4, where a woman from Shunem refuses to be satisfied with the prophet Elisha's staff or his servant, demanding the prophet himself come to heal her dead son. The author uses this as an allegory for seeking Christ directly.</note> would be satisfied neither with the servant nor with the staff, but insisted that Elisha must come himself; we too must first seek Him and come to Him in true faith, like the Shunammite. This occurs when we turn the eyes of our heart away from all other things and lift them directly upward toward the mountains to Christ at the right hand of God in heaven; so that, through His grace, we direct our ailing spirit as much as possible toward Him and, through Him, to God Himself. In short, where we surrender our bodies—indeed, our whole being—as a sacrifice to God in faith in Jesus Christ, according to the teaching of Paul, we shall obtain help and become whole and healthy.

Such is also testified to us for our comfort almost everywhere in the Psalms, as the Holy Spirit promises us help and a hearing through the Prophet, such as in Psalm 123:1, where he says: *O Lord, I lift up my eyes to You, who dwell in heaven*; and further in Psalm 121:1–2: *I lift up my eyes to the mountains, from whence my help comes. My help comes from the Lord, who made heaven and earth.* Paul also directs us toward this when, regarding the initial effect of the **heavenly medicine**, he speaks to the Christian believers thus: "Since we then, dear brothers, have the boldness to enter the <term>sanctuary</term> <note>German: *Heiligthum*; here referring to the "Holy of Holies" or the immediate presence of God.</note> of God (that is, in heaven) through the blood of Jesus, etc., let us draw near with a true heart in the full assurance of faith, our hearts sprinkled clean from an evil conscience and our bodies washed with pure water; and let us hold fast to the confession of hope without wavering, for He is faithful (O, a noble and loving Physician!) who has promised it (namely, eternal health and the heavenly life); and let us consider one another, to stir up love and good works, and not forsake our assembly," Hebrews 10:22[-25]. It is primarily through love and good works that one recognizes a person is holding fast to the Physician; that his healing has already begun inwardly, and that he desires to become completely healthy in both body and soul. Oh, blessed are those people whose strength is Christ, and who follow Him from the heart, Psalm 84:5 <note>The text cites Psalm 84:6, which corresponds to Psalm 84:5 in many English translations: "Blessed is the man whose strength is in You."</note>.

<page-num>93</page-num>

# The 33rd Chapter.

### Why God ordained the ministry of the Church, and how it is to be distinguished, such that even the servants of the heavenly Physician and Shepherd are sometimes themselves called spiritual physicians or spiritual shepherds.

Although we have now proven with Holy Scripture that Christ Jesus, the heavenly Physician, Himself conducts, promotes, and confirms the mastery and the office of **healing souls** for all sick, poor sinners; and that He gives the medicine, the recovery, and health from within Himself through the outflow of His power in the Spirit: yet He nevertheless desired to have the ministry and His appointed, commissioned servants present. These are those who understand these ointments, oils, and **heavenly medicine**, who also rightly know the Physician, are able to serve Him in the administration of the medicine, and know how to wisely point it out to the sick. For because man is not only spirit or soul, but also body, flesh, and blood—that is, composed of two unequal things, one spiritual and one bodily—God also wished to deal with him in two ways. He wished to let man be informed of the knowledge of His kingdom both internally and externally: internally through Himself, through Christ and His Spirit; but externally through ministers, scriptures, visible sacraments, creatures, and other means. *The Spirit of truth will testify of me, and you will also testify* <note>John 15:26–27</note>.

Thus, alongside His internal healing in Christ, the merciful God has—out of love and goodness—also ordained the external ministry of His chosen servants, teachers, and preachers, in whom He places a great treasure <note>A likely reference to 2 Corinthians 4:7: "But we have this treasure in earthen vessels."</note> for the sake of the external man. This was done so that the whole sick person might not only be healed internally (where he is most corrupted and suffers the greatest damage) but also receive instruction externally from the Physician and regarding the medicine. In this way, he is reminded of his internal illness, awakened, tended to, led to the Physician, and finally both body and soul may be kept whole and healthy for the Day of the Lord. For this purpose, the gracious, merciful God and Lord Christ has ordained or instituted the external ministry of His Word and the visible sacraments for His praise and honor in His gathered community or church, over and above the internal work of God.

<vocab>Church ministry, Christ Jesus, heavenly Physician, medicine, souls, sinners, sacraments, teachers, preachers, community</vocab>

<summary>This chapter explains that while Christ is the true internal healer of souls, God established the external Church and its ministers to guide the "external man" toward this spiritual medicine. It justifies the existence of the visible church as a necessary assistant to God's direct, internal work.</summary>
<keywords>Church ministry, Christ as Physician, spiritual healing, role of preachers, sacraments, dual nature of man, internal vs. external grace</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while Christ is the ultimate healer who acts freely of any human institution, the human ministers he appoints are to be honored as his "under-physicians" who deliver his spiritual medicine. It transitions from the previous page's focus on the necessity of external means to the specific dignity of the pastoral office.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

But nonetheless, the free Master, the Lord and Physician Christ, is bound neither to this place nor that, neither to the ministry nor the minister, nor is he tied to anything external; for God acts in Christ according to His freedom, without law, prescription, or rule, just as He wills. Christ is and shall remain the sole Savior of His body; the Spirit blows <note>original: "geistet"—a play on the word for Spirit, "Geist," suggesting the active movement or "spiriting" of the Holy Spirit</note> where He wills and when He wills. Just as an earthly physician can heal and make healthy by himself, without the help of assistants or servants, wherever it pleases him.

On account of such a glorious and comforting service and office, which is established for the promotion of health and the salvation of the soul, the appointed faithful servant should also be held in all honor, worthy and esteemed for the Lord’s sake. This servant is the one who carries the noble balsam and the fragrant oil of the Spirit and the medicine of the living Word—by which he himself was made well—to his Lord in gratitude, and thereby serves the sick. Of such a person, the Spirit of God commands through Paul, where he says: *1 Timothy 5:17. Let the elders who rule well be considered worthy of double honor, especially those who labor in the word and doctrine.* The Word of God is the original medicine of the soul and the healing doctrine of the Holy Spirit, which rescues one from eternal death and makes one spiritually healthy. It is also written: *Honor the physician with the honor due him, so that you may have him in your time of need, Sirach 38:1.* <note>Though Sirach refers to physical doctors, the author applies this "honor" to spiritual healers as well.</note> Although this could well be understood as referring to the Lord Christ, it can also, after Him and for His sake, refer to all "under-physicians" or <term>pastors</term> <note>original: "Seelsorger"—literally "soul-carers"</note> who care for spiritually sick people according to His command and will, and faithfully offer them the medicine of His Word. However, they should not point to themselves, but rather to the true Physician, of whom they speak and bear witness.

For since our Lord Jesus Christ, out of pure love, grace, and goodness, often attributes to His servants and appointed ministers that which He Himself does through them—for the sake of the ministry and the fellowship of the Spirit—as He says in *John 14:12: Truly, truly, I say to you, whoever believes in me will also do the works that I do; and greater works than these will he do, because I am going to the Father.* Thus it follows that the ministers of the great <term>Chief Physician</term> <note>original: "Erzarzt"—referring to Christ as the Arch-Physician or Supreme Doctor</note> Christ are themselves called "physicians" under Him. This is just as they are called "shepherds" under the great Shepherd of the sheep because of their service, or "bishops" and "pastors" under the Arch-Bishop of our souls. To those whom the Chief Physician has thus appointed and sent out in the Holy Spirit as a pastor, servant, or under-physician, He gives a modest portion of the medicine, so that he may serve the sick with it and present it to them blessedly in his ministry. Then the Lord and Master applies it further to the Christian believer; He works with the servant in divine power, strengthens, gives, and helps,

<vocab>Christ, Chief Physician, Pastor, Medicine, Holy Spirit, 1 Timothy 5, Sirach 38, John 14</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that while Christ is the only true healer who acts with total freedom, He uses human ministers as "under-physicians." These ministers are to be honored because they distribute the "medicine" of the Word, though the actual healing power remains Christ's alone.</summary>
<keywords>Christ as Physician, pastoral office, spiritual medicine, honors for clergy, divine freedom, Seelsorger, Erzarzt</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the role of ministers as "under-physicians" to the Great Physician, Christ. It moves from the previous page's honor of the pastoral office to the theological distinction that while ministers perform the work, the power and glory belong solely to God. It concludes by comparing spiritual care to the precise prescriptions of earthly medicine.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

...so that the medicine, of which the servant testifies and which he praises and honors, may benefit the sick person and be blessed and fruitful for their health.

In the same way, the Lord also sent out his apostles in the beginning, commanding them to raise the dead, cast out demons, cleanse the lepers, and heal all kinds of sick people; for this purpose, he first gave them power and strength. These are then called "God’s fellow workers" in 1 Corinthians 3:9. Indeed, as has been said several times, He himself dwells in them and performs the work through their service. Nevertheless, because of the privilege granted to faithful people through the <term>exalted humanity of Christ</term> <note>This refers to the Lutheran and Pietist doctrine that Christ's human nature participates in divine attributes, allowing Him to work through human agents.</note>, God allows them to participate in this work. However, the faithful servant who is wise in God can clearly distinguish this, for he knows that everything done for the healing of the sick under Christ, the heavenly Physician, flows from the ability, power, and Spirit of the Physician and not the servant. For that same one Spirit works all these things, distributing to each one as He wills (1 Corinthians 12:11). He knows that God works everything in all believers; therefore, he directs everything so that Christ the Physician, with God His Father and the Holy Spirit, may be fully praised, honored, and glorified.

Paul did the same when the people of Lystra wanted to offer sacrifices to him and Barnabas after he healed a man who had been lame from his mother's womb. He spoke: "Men, what are you doing? We too are humans like you" (Acts 14:15). And Peter also gives all honor to the Lord when he healed the lame man in the name of Jesus; he spoke to the Jewish people, who were amazed and terrified by it: "Men of Israel, why do you wonder at this? Or why do you look at us as if we had made this man walk by our own power or merit? Faith in Jesus Christ has given this man health before your eyes" (Acts 3:12, 16).

Since we are speaking here of the <term>under-physicians</term> <note>original: "Unterärzten" — assistant physicians or medical subordinates who carry out a master doctor's orders.</note>, that is, the servants of the one true Physician, Christ, it should also be noted that just as wise physicians order and prepare their physical medicine according to each person's age, nature, or <term>constitution</term> <note>original: "Complexion" — in early modern medicine, this refers to the specific balance of "humors" or bodily fluids that determines a person's health and temperament.</note>—namely for children, adults, those in middle-age, and the elderly. They know how to prescribe what is appropriate for each person, both internally and externally, and what serves each specific illness, so that it may be useful and benefit everyone. They also show this to their assistants and instruct them, so that one medicine is not mistaken for another, but rather a good order, measure, and distinction is maintained among various sick people. In the same way, it should be among the heavenly Physician...

<summary>This page explains that while ministers (the "under-physicians") perform acts of spiritual healing, the power originates entirely from Christ. It uses the biblical examples of Peter and Paul to warn against human pride and compares spiritual guidance to the careful, individualized prescriptions of earthly medicine.</summary>
<keywords>apostles, God’s fellow workers, Christ the Physician, Paul and Barnabas, Peter, under-physicians, spiritual medicine, constitution, Lystra, humility</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical metaphor for spiritual care. The previous page established that while ministers (under-physicians) perform the work of healing, the power comes solely from Christ (the Chief Physician). This page expands on the responsibility of ministers to properly "prescribe" the Word and the responsibility of the "patient" (the believer) to discern whether their minister is truly leading them to Christ.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

This is also how it proceeds with Jesus Christ, after he has prepared the medicine <note>original: "Arzeney"</note> of his Word, his death, suffering, flesh, and blood for all kinds of people (who only desire health), so that it may be presented to each according to their character, weakness, strength, or understanding; just as he is milk to some and strong food to others <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 3:2, where spiritual maturity dictates the "diet" of teaching a believer can handle.</note>, and desires that all people recover, be healthy, and be saved. The peace of the Lord is over all the earth, says Sirach <note>Referring to the Book of Sirach/Ecclesiasticus in the Apocrypha.</note>: Therefore, the servants and under-physicians <note>original: "Unterärzte"</note> in this spiritual trade of heavenly medicine must also have great diligence and insight, so that they first learn to know everything correctly and distinguish everything well. In this way, they may know how to present to each person their due in service at the right time, according to the command of the Master, Christ, and constantly exhort them to pay attention to the Physician himself everywhere, expecting help from him alone, so that because of their service no sick person entrusted to their care may perish, be neglected, or be overlooked.

As much as the inner sickness of the soul is greater, more dangerous, and heavier than any sickness of the body may ever be, so much more should we pray to God the Almighty and the Chief Physician <note>original: "Erzarzt"</note> Jesus Christ for good, faithful under-physicians, under-shepherds, servants, or soul-carers <term>Seelsorger</term> <note>A traditional German term for a pastor, literally "one who cares for the soul."</note> and workers, as he himself has commanded. Through them, the weakness, lack of understanding, and forgetfulness of our weak flesh may be helped, whereby we are encouraged to move forward in faith, our sickness is revealed to us, and others are also brought near.

We should also take good notice of the Physician's servants, or those who present themselves as spiritual physicians, in all their actions, and "test the spirits whether they are of God" <note>1 John 4:1</note>, so that we may know in truth who comes to us, what medicine he presents and proclaims in his service; likewise, whether he points toward the Physician, Christ, and glorifies and praises him: yes, whether he also truly knows the Physician, Christ, and carries the heavenly medicine of his Word in his heart. This is perceived by the fruits, the scent, the helpful medicines, and the works; whether he, as a faithful servant, leads the sick on the highway by the shortest path to the Physician. All this we must diligently observe above all things, so that we do not perhaps invite or receive a false servant for the right one, an unfaithful one for the faithful, or a bungler <note>original: "Stümpler"</note> for the proven one. I mean such a person who points us more away from the Lord or Master and from his heavenly medicine than toward it. The sick person himself must also learn to distinguish between the Master and the servant, between the true Physician and the under-physicians, and what medicine he takes—whether it is working within him, whether it contributes to his health, and how everything is settling or taking effect. In short, he should know, and not merely imagine, whether he is being comforted, made joyful, and healthy by the medicine.

**Vocabulary**
Medicine, Chief Physician, Under-physicians, Soul-carer (Pastor), Jesus Christ, Sirach, Faith, Health

<summary>This page describes the duty of ministers as "under-physicians" who must skillfully apply the "medicine" of the Gospel to individual needs. It also charges believers with the responsibility of discerning whether their pastors are truly leading them to Christ or are merely "bunglers."</summary>
<keywords>spiritual medicine, pastoral care, discernment, Jesus Christ as Physician, Seelsorger, testing the spirits, spiritual health</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical allegory for spiritual health. The previous page (96) emphasized that while ministers act as "under-physicians," the actual power of healing comes from Christ. This page serves as a warning against "inexperienced physicians"—false or untrained spiritual leaders—and concludes with a prayer for Christ to be the ultimate medicine and restorer of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<sig>G</sig>

->❦❦❦❦❦<-

All this must be truthfully considered in Christ by those who take comfort in or seek after Eternal Life; it must also be diligently sought and recognized through Him, because the old proverb still exists today, which says: **Beware of inexperienced physicians**; which should be weighed much more highly and diligently in spiritual distress of the soul and conscience than in any physical distress. 

But what are inexperienced physicians? They are those, both physical and spiritual, who are inexperienced, young, and untrained; who understand neither a person’s stomach, constitution <term>complexion: in early modern medicine, this refers to the balance of the four "humors" that determined a person's health and temperament</term>, nor the nature of the body, nor do they know his sickness or how to distinguish it correctly. One should beware of such people, who often use only one recipe for all illnesses, who inquire little about health, show no diligence toward the sick—indeed, who are themselves always sick and cannot help themselves. They are also charlatans <term>Partierer: an archaic term for a trickster, swindler, or one who deals in fraudulent goods</term> who often adulterate the medicine <note>original: "Arzeney"</note>, taking their remedies from places other than the true apothecary <note>The "true apothecary" here is a metaphor for the Holy Scriptures or the grace of Christ</note>, or who cannot present them in such a way that the poor, sick, and troubled soul is refreshed, strengthened, and comforted by the grace of God. 

Likewise <note>original: "Item," a common Latin scholarly marker for "also" or "furthermore"</note>, they are those who can give no account, ground, or origin for their art—neither from the experience of the heart nor from the Scriptures. This is what it means to: *Beware of inexperienced physicians*; and all those who wish to become permanently healthy in their soul should take this well to heart.

May the Lord and heavenly physician, Jesus Christ, heal our hearts from all disease, and make us free and unburdened from all evil desires and sins. May He himself be and remain our salvation, comfort, and medicine in the Holy Spirit, so that we may be inwardly healthy and well-cleansed, fit to love Him, courageous in suffering for His name's sake, and steadfast in remaining within the recognized truth. May we be perfected in divine health and become fully capable of dwelling with Him and God His Father in the Kingdom of Heaven. Amen.

# Printing Errors in the First Part,
### Or:
## Concerning the Heavenly Medicine.

Page 10 in the 2nd paragraph, 8th line, read **noble** <note>original: "vornehmen," which can also mean "to undertake"</note> instead of **perceive**.
Page 16 in the 3rd paragraph, 9th line, read **valued as the body**, etc.
Page 27 in the 2nd paragraph, 8th line, **are**—instead of **they**.
Page 31 in the 1st paragraph, 4th line, read: **not hear illness**.
Page 45 in the 2nd paragraph, 9th line, **daily**—instead of **more daily**. <note>A grammatical correction from "täglicher" to "täglich"</note>
Page 51 in the 2nd paragraph, 3rd line, **offers**—instead of **builds**. <note>The original "beut" is an archaic form of "bietet" (offers/presents), which was likely misread by the printer as "baut" (builds)</note>
Page 55 in the 1st paragraph, 4th line, **him**—instead of **her**.
Page 58 in the heading of the 22nd Chapter, **of the Essence**—instead of **Essence**. <note>A correction of the German case from "Wesen" to the genitive "Wesens"</note>
Page 60 in the 3rd paragraph, 9th line, the word “shall” is superfluous.
Page 66 in the 3rd paragraph, 13th line, read: **for**—instead of **now**.
Page 67 in the 4th paragraph, 6th line, read: **for the sake of our sins**.
Page 77 in the 2nd paragraph, 9th line, **in the**—instead of **never**.
Page 77 in the 3rd paragraph, 5th line, **briefly**—instead of **arbitrarily**. <note>original: "kürzlich" (briefly/recently) instead of "kürlich" (an obscure or misspelled term)</note>
Page 78 in the 2nd paragraph, 11th line, **Medicine**—instead of **Physician**.
Page 88 in the 2nd paragraph, 8th line, **him**—instead of **him**. <note>This is a grammatical correction in German from the accusative "ihn" to the dative "ihm"</note>
Page 91 in the 2nd paragraph, 9th line, read **heavenly physician Christ**.

## Caspar Schwenkfeld. <note>Caspar Schwenkfeld von Ossig (1489–1561) was a prominent German Reformer who broke with Luther over the nature of the Eucharist and emphasized a "Middle Way" focused on the inner spiritual life. His followers, the Schwenkfelders, eventually fled persecution and settled in Pennsylvania.</note>

->**Allentown,**<-
->(Lehigh County, Pennsylvania)<-

<summary>This is the title page for an 1820 Pennsylvania German edition of Caspar Schwenkfeld's treatise on the Christian life and the nature of true worship. It marks the enduring legacy of Schwenkfeld’s radical Reformation theology within the German-speaking immigrant communities of Lehigh County.</summary>

# Preface.

Here in this little book, the kind-hearted reader will find impartial and sound instruction on what a righteous <note>In this context, "righteous" (German: *rechtschaffen*) refers to a life lived with sincere integrity and moral uprightness, rather than just following rules.</note> Christian life is, where it proceeds from, and what its practice, nature, and fruits are. It also teaches what true worship <note>German: *Gottesdienst*. In this period, this term referred both to formal church services and the "service to God" performed through daily living.</note> is, and what it primarily consists of. Likewise <note>original: "Item," a Latin term commonly used in older German texts to introduce a new point in a list.</note>, it explains the difference between an honorable, virtuous worldly life and a godly Christian life. Similarly, it addresses the distinction between good works and their reward; namely, that the kind and merciful God will leave no good work unrewarded.

<summary>This preface introduces a devotional guide intended to teach readers the foundations of a sincere Christian life and the nature of true worship. It emphasizes the practical "fruits" of faith and the distinction between mere social virtue and genuine godliness.</summary>
<keywords>Christian living, true worship, virtue vs. godliness, good works, divine reward, religious instruction</keywords>

# Preface.

From this, that famous point of the Holy Scripture concerning the twofold righteousness—namely, human and divine righteousness, or the righteousness of works and the righteousness of faith <note>The "twofold righteousness" is a central concept in Reformation theology, distinguishing between the moral uprightness a person achieves through actions and the spiritual standing given by God through faith.</note> (concerning which Paul teaches so often and in various ways in his letters to the Romans, Galatians, Philippians, and Hebrews)—may be more easily understood through the guiding light of Christ. This includes what Paul means by the righteousness of faith and the works of the law, where he says: **Therefore we conclude that a man is justified by faith without the deeds of the law, Romans 3:28.**

And it shows that the righteousness of faith, or true faith in Jesus Christ, does not exist in such a meager way that it lacks the conversion of the sinner, a God-fearing way of life, or good works. That is to say, it is not as if a person could possess a true Christian faith without repentance <note>German: *Buße*. In this theological context, it implies a deep internal turning away from sin rather than just a formal act of penance.</note> and without a new, godly life; but rather that true faith, or such righteousness of faith, transforms the whole person, both internally and externally, his words, deeds,

desire, emotion, and conduct—indeed, to govern his whole life in all godliness <term>Godliness (German: *Gottseligkeit*): A 17th-century term describing a life of deep personal piety and moral uprightness.</term>. In this, the Lord Christ, following the rebirth and renewal of the person, leads him in His Spirit from day to day toward perfection and the taking up of the inheritance of the Kingdom of Heaven, moving ever more from faith to faith and from one virtue to another.

Briefly, it is proven herein that a new heart and a new spirit are necessary for the righteousness of faith <note>The "righteousness of faith" refers to the theological concept that a person is made right with God through trust in Christ's merits rather than their own actions.</note>, as well as for true divine service. God the Lord has promised in the Prophets to give both of these to His people—that is, to all the elect—out of grace, for the sake of the benefit of Christ His beloved Son, and because of His cross, death, martyrdom, and bloodshed, in and through Him. Therefore, we who desire to be saved should pray daily, yearn for it, sigh, and possess a heartfelt longing with true faith, until we abundantly receive these two things from the grace of God: namely, a new spirit and a new heart in Christ.

# Preface.

We must also cast away, hate, and discard the old, evil, and deceitful heart, along with sin and all transgression, in the name of Christ and through his prevenient grace <term>Prevenient grace (German: *vorkommende Gnade*): A theological term for grace that "comes before" human action, predisposing a person to seek God before they are even aware of the need.</term>; on the other hand, we must walk in true repentance <term>Repentance (German: *Buße*): In this context, it implies a total change of mind and heart, not merely a feeling of regret.</term>, godly fear, and humility before the eyes of God as the beginning of a Christian life. We must learn to rightly recognize Christ our Lord and his many blessings, and give place and room to his calling toward eternity within our hearts and consciences, through the obedience of faith.

Then he will mercifully have compassion on us, accept us anew, grant us rebirth, and blessedly establish within us the righteousness of faith <note>The "righteousness of faith" refers to the status of being right with God through trust in Christ, rather than through one's own efforts or following laws.</note> alongside an upright Christian life and a good conscience. May the dear Lord Jesus grant and do this soon, for your glory and for our eternal salvation. Amen.

<meta>This page begins the main body of the work following the preface. It transitions from the general call for repentance to a specific classification of the human condition, categorizing life into three distinct moral and spiritual states.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece consisting of two rows of repeating floral or circular patterns within scalloped borders.</image-desc>

### Of
# The Three Kinds of Human Life.

---

It is to be noted that there are three kinds of life among men, in which the entire human race is included, namely:

1. A carnal, godless life.
2. A rational, honorable life.
3. A Christian, godly life.

---

## The 1st Chapter.
### Concerning the Carnal, Godless Life.

The carnal life of man is when one lives entirely wicked and godless, even against the law of nature <term>Law of Nature (German: *Gesez der Natur*): The innate sense of right and wrong that was believed to be written on every human heart by God, independent of scripture.</term> and against all rational decency. It is when man, without fear of God's punishment—as if there were no hell, nor judgment, nor even a kingdom of heaven—lives shamelessly like a beast in vices and mortal sins <note>Mortal sins (*Todsünden*) are considered "deadly" because they were believed to destroy the divine life in the soul, leading to spiritual death if not repented.</note>, and persists in them without ceasing, without repentance or sorrow for sin.

When man lives according to all his self-will in the desires of the flesh, in drunkenness, gluttony, greed, usury, unchastity, wrath, vengefulness, and in all unrighteousness, without the fear of God. When he [neglects] moral virtues, decency, and

<note>The text here breaks off mid-sentence, likely continuing on the following page.</note>

**Vocabulary:**
Three kinds of life, carnal godless life, rational honorable life, Christian godly life, law of nature, mortal sins, fear of God

<summary>This page introduces a tripartite classification of human existence, focusing specifically on the first category: the "carnal, godless life." This state is defined by a total lack of divine fear, a rejection of natural law, and a persistent indulgence in vice.</summary>

<keywords>three kinds of life, carnal life, law of nature, mortal sins, godlessness, vices, self-will</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the "carnal, godless life" begun on the previous page, detailing the lack of spiritual concern in such a life before transitioning to the second category of human existence: the rational and honorable life.</meta>

...does not regard justice, nor applies himself to the works of natural human compassion and mercy; much less does he seek after the future eternal life and the redemption and grace of Christ; for many of these same godless people want to know or hear nothing of a future life; indeed, they do not even believe in the **resurrection of the flesh** <term>Resurrection of the Flesh (German: *Auferstehung des Fleisches*): The traditional Christian belief that at the end of time, the physical bodies of the dead will be raised and reunited with their souls for judgment.</term>. Whatever they may justify or mask with some outward appearance before the world, they have no further care, fear, or sorrow regarding it, acting as if there were no life after this one; indeed, as if God, who is the Lord and Creator of all men, were not also a just and strict judge, who will give to each according to how he has acted, whether it be good or evil, 2 Cor. 5, 10. <note>2 Corinthians 5:10: "For we must all appear before the judgment seat of Christ; that every one may receive the things done in his body, according to that he hath done, whether it be good or bad."</note>

Such a life, in which man neither recognizes nor fears God his Creator—in which, unfortunately, almost the whole world is drowned and lies in wickedness—is actually not a truly human life, but a beastly, irrational life; indeed, it is complete godlessness and a damned, evil, shameful life, punishable before God and all decency; therefore, let us leave it be.

Regarding the reward of the godless, however, and how they have hell both here and there; as the 49th Psalm v. 15 says: *They lie in hell like sheep, death gnaws them,* <note>original: "Sie liegen in der Hölle wie Schaafe, der Tod naget sie." In modern English translations of Psalm 49:14, this is often rendered as "Like sheep they are laid in the grave; death shall feed on them."</note> etc., more shall be said hereafter concerning the reward of works. So much for this first life.

# The 2nd Chapter.

## Concerning the Rational, Honorable Life.

The second life is called a rational, honorable life, which is lived according to the **Law of Nature** <term>Law of Nature (German: *Gesetz der Natur*): The innate moral sense or "conscience" believed to be inherent in all humans by virtue of their creation.</term>, or else according to the Law of Moses, or otherwise according to prescribed rules, orders, and statutes or teachings—rightful, honest, civil, and human, even pious, yet still living without Christ and His Spirit.

This occurs when a person lives solely out of his own strength, according to the use and instruction of his **reason** and the natural gifts <note>natural gifts (*creaturlichen Gaben*): The inherent abilities or talents granted to human beings as part of their created nature, distinct from the supernatural grace of the Holy Spirit.</note> that God charitably bestowed upon man in the work of creation; or when he applies himself to living morally and virtuously according to the letter of the Scripture alone; so that he serves God only in outward actions, without heart or faith, honoring Him only with **ceremonies** <term>Ceremonies (*Ceremonien*): External religious rites or formal observances, which the author argues can be performed as a matter of habit or duty without genuine internal faith.</term>, fearing His judgment only for the sake of punishment, honoring parents only for the sake of prosperity and according to nature, and helping his neighbor only for his own advantage...

<summary>This page concludes the critique of the "godless" life as sub-human and introduces the "rational" life, which follows laws and ethics through human reason but lacks the spiritual presence of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>resurrection of the flesh, judgment, godlessness, Law of Nature, Law of Moses, reason, ceremonies, civic virtue, morality</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion on the "beastly" godless life to the second category of human existence: the "rational and honorable life." It explores the distinction between outward morality and inner spiritual state.</meta>

...does good for the sake of reward or praise, offends no one, keeps faith with people, acts sincerely with them, and guards against vice out of fear of pain, etc. This is, in short, a rational, honorable life, when a person lives in such a way that common decency can find nothing to punish or rebuke in them. This occurs when someone—for the sake of prosperity, self-interest, or the praise of men—conducts themselves outwardly in a moral, moderate, chaste, peaceful, and just manner, as a modest and honorable man, woman, or maiden; yet, inwardly, they remain impure of heart due to self-love, pride, and wicked sins—a hypocrite <term>Hypocrite (German: *Gleißner*): Literally "one who glimmers" or shines on the outside while being dark within; a person who puts on a false appearance of virtue.</term>, and fundamentally not righteous in the eyes of God.

Such an honorable and rational life is called human piety, an outward **civil righteousness**, a righteousness of works, or one's own righteousness, which receives praise before men, as mentioned in Romans 2. To promote and maintain this righteousness, the governing authorities and the **sword** <note>The "sword" refers to the legal authority of the state to enforce laws and deliver corporal or capital punishment.</note>—namely, to protect the good and punish the evil—have been ordained and established by God for His praise and for the benefit of mankind, for the sake of common peace, so that one may live and remain with another. Concerning this, it is written in Romans 13, where Paul speaks: *Let every soul be subject unto the higher powers, for there is no power but of God,* etc. And further: *For rulers are not a terror to good works, but to the evil. Wilt thou then not be afraid of the power? Do that which is good, and thou shalt have praise of the same: for she is the minister of God to thee for good. But if thou do that which is evil, be afraid; for she beareth not the sword in vain: for she is the minister of God, a revenger to execute punishment upon him that doeth evil,* Romans 13.

It should be noted there that since worldly power was established by God for the sake of human righteousness and temporal peace, as has been heard, Paul meant nothing else by "good works" or "doing good" in that context than such a civil, peaceful life and human righteousness and order. This is common to both heathens <note>original: "Heiden"; refers to non-Christians or those in the classical world.</note> and Christians alike, and everyone is duty-bound to live honorably, justly, and peacefully, and to obey the authorities—whether they be evil or good—in these matters, giving them tribute, custom, fear, and honor; for they are God’s servants who are meant to uphold such protection of human righteousness. Saint Paul is not speaking here of the obedience and subjection of faith, nor of matters of religion; for at the very time he wrote this to the Romans, the wicked pagan Emperor Nero was reigning over them.

But regarding the life of human righteousness, the pagan philosophers—such as Aristotle, Plato, Seneca, Socrates, Cicero, and many others—have taught much, and also in virtues for themselves...

<summary>The author defines "civil righteousness" as outward moral behavior motivated by self-interest or social order, distinguishing it from true spiritual righteousness. He explains that God established secular government and the "sword" to maintain this outward peace among all people, regardless of their faith.</summary>
<keywords>civil righteousness, governing authorities, Romans 13, Saint Paul, Emperor Nero, pagan philosophers, human piety, the sword, social order</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "rational and honorable life," distinguishing between outward moral behavior and the inner spiritual transformation required by the Gospel.</meta>

...they lived thus themselves, placing all their glory and even their salvation in such a life and in their works. This righteousness and moral life is demanded by God of all people through the **law of nature** <term>Law of Nature (*Gesetz der Natur*): A historical theological concept referring to an inherent sense of morality and conscience that God supposedly implanted in all humans, regardless of their specific religion.</term>, just as the Law of Moses also teaches such righteousness according to the letter and commanded it to all Jews (though with a condition) under heavy penalty, as the Holy Scriptures of the Old Testament testify.

For this reason, the Lord Christ, in the Gospel, called such a life and the aforementioned fleshly piety a "righteousness of the Scribes and Pharisees." This is because the Scribes and Pharisees (as well as the philosophers of the heathens) know of no other righteousness than such a legal, civil piety or righteousness that comes from human strength and the love of moral virtues. That is to say, they know only of their own righteousness—a righteousness of works—which occurs without faith and without a change of heart. Therefore, the Lord compares them to "beautiful-looking whitewashed tombs" and to those who "clean only the outside of the cup" (Matthew 23:27).

Even today, there are many people who consider such outward piety and a life blameless before men to be the only true Christian life. They believe this because the world is so deeply washed away and drowned in vice, and has even fallen into a beastly, irrational way of living—even worse than that of the heathens. Furthermore, because the true Christian life (which comes solely from Christ through **rebirth** <term>Rebirth (*Wiedergeburt*): Also known as the "New Birth," this refers to a spiritual transformation or internal renewal by the Holy Spirit, which the author distinguishes from mere moral self-improvement.</term> in the Holy Spirit) has been completely obscured, buried, and lost through a lack of knowledge of Christ and His living Gospel—and because the Spirit of Christ has become unknown—they believe that such a life as mentioned above is the best before God, and that there is or could be nothing better, simply because it is so rare in these times.

<summary>This page distinguishes between "civil righteousness"—outward moral behavior achieved through human effort—and the true Christian life characterized by inner spiritual rebirth. The author argues that many mistake outward decency for salvation, comparing such people to the biblical "whitewashed tombs."</summary>
<keywords>civil righteousness, Law of Nature, Law of Moses, Scribes and Pharisees, works vs. faith, hypocrisy, rebirth, Holy Spirit, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general definition of the "righteousness of the Pharisees" to a deeper explanation of why God requires moral behavior from all humanity, while simultaneously explaining why such behavior is insufficient for true salvation due to the corruption of human nature.</meta>

# Chapter 3.

### That God Demands a Virtuous, Just, and Honorable Life from All People.

Now, although Almighty God rightly demands the aforementioned life of human righteousness and piety from all people—for he created us in the beginning as rational creatures in his own image and likeness, and obligated us to live justly and piously according to his will—it is also right that he, for the sake of his benevolence as our God, Lord, Creator, and Sustainer, should be praised and loved by humanity, as well as feared because of punishment and honored because of his glory.

Likewise, one’s neighbor should be loved for the sake of God his Creator, and because he is of one "fleshly father," Adam, with us; and a civil peace and unity should be maintained between us. All people are duty-bound to do this before God and the world: namely, that they should live justly and piously to the praise of God their Creator as much as is possible for his creatures. To this end, Almighty God has given humans natural gifts, such as reason, understanding, knowledge, intellect, strength, a free will, and—to encourage and guide it—the **law of nature** <term>Law of Nature (*Gesetz der Natur*): The innate sense of right and wrong that theologians believed God imprinted on the human heart, independent of the Bible.</term>, as well as a natural conscience. Through these, God obligates everyone to act rightly, so that no one can excuse themselves; just as he fairly punishes evildoers. Likewise, the **magistrate** <term>Magistrate (*Magistrat*): The civil authorities or local government leaders responsible for upholding public law and order.</term> is duty-bound to maintain and promote such civil righteousness and a peaceful, disciplined life, as previously mentioned, for which he also receives his temporal <note>Meaning "worldly" or "earthly," as opposed to eternal.</note> reward from God.

But alas, poor man has been so corrupted by the evil spirit and by sin, and has become so disobedient and unfit toward God, that everything good he does by nature, or undertakes of himself without Christ and his grace, he does either out of fear of punishment or his own fleshly desires. He does not act according to God’s pure will—that is, he does not do the good God has commanded with true affection, heart, and love. Nor does he live by his own strengths (let them be as "good" as they may) in a way that might please God, because by nature man does not seek God's glory alone; rather, in everything he does, he follows the nature of the corrupted flesh permeated with sin, seeking his own honor and only himself.

<vocab>Righteousness, Piety, Creator, Adam, Reason, Law of Nature, Conscience, Magistrate, Sin, Christ, Grace</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that while God endowed all humans with reason and a conscience to live morally for the sake of social order, this "civil righteousness" is fundamentally flawed because it is driven by self-interest rather than a pure love for God.</summary>
<keywords>natural law, civil righteousness, human corruption, conscience, role of government, original sin, motives of the heart</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 9 regarding civil order and the "righteousness of the Pharisees." It bridges the previous thought about magistrates maintaining civil order by explaining that while God demands moral conduct, it is not the same as the spiritual rebirth required for salvation.</meta>

...lived in such a way themselves, in which they then placed all their glory and even their salvation, just as they did in their works. This righteousness and moral living is demanded by God of all people in general through the **law of nature**, just as the **law of Moses** <term>Law of Moses (*Gesetz Mosi*): The religious and social laws given to the Israelites in the Old Testament, often contrasted by later writers with the "spirit" of the New Testament.</term> teaches of such righteousness according to the letter; he commanded the same to all Jews (though with a condition) under heavy penalty, as the Holy Scripture of the Old Testament testifies.

Therefore, the Lord Christ called such a life and this aforementioned fleshly piety in the **Gospel** <term>Gospel (*Evangelium*): The "Good News" or teachings of Jesus Christ recorded in the New Testament.</term> a "righteousness of the **Scribes** and **Pharisees**." This is because the Scribes and Pharisees (as well as the **philosophers of the heathens** <note>original: *Philosophen der Heiden*; referring to Greek and Roman thinkers like Aristotle or Seneca who taught ethics without Christian revelation.</note>) know of no other righteousness than such a legal, civil piety or righteousness that comes from human strength and the love of moral virtues—that is, from their own righteousness and the righteousness of works, which occurs without faith or a change of heart. For this reason, the Lord compares them to "beautifully appearing whitewashed tombs" <note>original: *übertünchten Gräbern*</note> and to those who clean only the outside of the cup. Matthew 23:27.

Just as today there are still many people who consider such external piety and a life blameless before men to be the only true Christian life. This is because the world is so deeply washed away and drowned in vice, having fallen into a brutish, irrational way of life—even worse than among the heathens. Furthermore, because the true Christian life (which comes solely from Christ through **rebirth** <term>Rebirth (*Wiedergeburt*): A core concept in this period of theology, referring to an internal spiritual transformation rather than just following outward rules.</term> in the **Holy Spirit**) has become entirely darkened, buried, and lost due to a lack of the knowledge of Christ and his living Gospel, and because the Spirit of Christ has become unknown, they then believe that such a life (as mentioned above) is the best before God—or indeed that there can be no better—simply because even that is so rare in these times.

<summary>This page defines the "righteousness of the Pharisees" as an outward, civil morality based on human effort rather than spiritual rebirth. The author argues that while God requires moral behavior from everyone, many modern people mistake this external "blamelessness" for true Christianity because the world has become so corrupt.</summary>
