# The 3rd Chapter.

### That God requires a virtuous, righteous, and honorable life from all people.

Although Almighty God justly demands the aforementioned life of human righteousness and piety from all people—for He did, in the beginning, create us as rational creatures in His own image and likeness—He has obligated us to live righteously and godly according to His will. It is only right that, because of His benevolence, He should be praised and loved as our God, Lord, Creator, and Preserver; likewise, He should be feared because of His punishment and honored because of His glory.

In the same way, one’s neighbor should be loved for the sake of God his Creator, and because he shares with us the same forefather, Adam. A civil peace and unity should be maintained between us, which all people are bound to do before God and the world. Namely, they must live righteously and godly to the praise of God their Creator as much as is possible for His creatures. To this end, Almighty God has given man natural gifts, such as **reason**, **understanding**, **knowledge**, **wit** <note>original: *Witz*; here meaning intellect or mental acuity rather than humor.</note>, **strength**, a **free will**, and—to encourage and guide that will—the **Law of Nature** <term>Law of Nature (*Gesetz der Natur*): The inherent sense of right and wrong that theologians argued God imprinted on all human hearts, regardless of their religion.</term>, as well as a natural conscience. Through these, God binds everyone to act rightly so that no one can excuse themselves; just as He also rightly punishes evildoers. Furthermore, the **Magistrate** <term>Magistrate (*Magistrat*): The civil authorities or government officials responsible for upholding public order and law.</term> is obligated to maintain and promote such civil righteousness and a peaceful, disciplined life, as previously mentioned, for which he also receives his temporal reward from God.

However, poor mankind is, alas, so corrupted by the evil spirit and by sin, and has become so disobedient and unfit <note>original: *ungeschickt*; in this context, it means spiritually incapacitated or "unhandy" in performing divine will.</note> toward God, that everything he does that is "good" by nature—or everything he undertakes by himself without **Christ** and His **grace**—is done either out of fear of punishment or for his own fleshly desires. It is not done according to God’s pure will. That is to say, he does not perform the good that God has commanded with true affection, heart, and love. Nor does he live by his own strengths (let them be as great as they may) in such a way that it might please God. This is because man, by nature, does not seek God’s honor alone; rather, in everything he does, he follows the nature of the corrupted flesh, permeated by sin, seeking his own glory and himself above all else.

<summary>This chapter explains that God demands moral behavior from all humans through natural law and reason, yet asserts that without divine grace, these "good works" are driven by fear or selfishness rather than true love for God.</summary>
<keywords>Law of Nature, Natural Conscience, Civil Righteousness, Human Corruption, Free Will, Magistrates, Original Sin</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section (labeled page 11) regarding the Law of Nature and God’s requirements for human conduct. It transitions from the general obligation of all people to live morally to a critique of those who rely solely on outward behavior for salvation.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>

...[they] lived thus themselves, placing all their glory and even their salvation in it, as they did in their works. Such righteousness and moral living is demanded by God from all people generally through the **Law of Nature**, just as the **Law of Moses** teaches this righteousness according to the letter and commanded the same to all Jews (though with a condition) under heavy penalty, as the Holy Scripture of the Old Testament testifies.

Therefore, the Lord Christ called such a life and this aforementioned carnal piety in the **Gospel** a "righteousness of the scribes and Pharisees." This is because the scribes and Pharisees (as well as the philosophers of the heathens) know of no other righteousness than such a legal, civil piety or righteousness that comes from human strength and the love of moral virtues—that is, from their own righteousness, from the **righteousness of works**, which occurs without faith and a change of heart. For this reason, the Lord compares them to "beautiful-looking whitewashed tombs" <note>A reference to Jesus’s critique in the Gospel of Matthew, suggesting something that looks clean on the outside but is spiritually dead within.</note> and to those who "clean only the outside of the cup." Matthew 23:27.

Just as even today many people hold such outward piety and a blameless life before men to be the only true Christian life. This is because the world is so deeply washed away and drowned in vice, and has fallen into a brutish, irrational life—even worse than that of the heathens. Since the true Christian life (which comes alone from Christ through **rebirth** <term>Rebirth (*Wiedergeburt*): A central theological concept referring to a spiritual transformation or "being born again" through the Holy Spirit, rather than just changing one's outward behavior.</term> in the Holy Spirit) has become entirely obscured, buried, and lost due to a lack of knowledge of Christ and His living Gospel, and because the Spirit of Christ has become unknown, they believe that such a life as mentioned above (because it is so rare in these times) is the best before God—indeed, that there is or can be no better.

---

# Chapter 3.

### That God Demands a Virtuous, Just, and Honorable Life from All People.

Although Almighty God rightfully demands the aforementioned life of human righteousness and piety from all people—for he created us from the beginning as rational creatures in his own image and likeness, and bound us to live justly and piously according to his will—it is also right that he should be praised and loved by mankind as our God, Lord, Creator, and Sustainer for the sake of his benevolence. Likewise, he should be feared because of punishment and honored for the sake of his glory.

Similarly, one's neighbor should be loved for the sake of God his Creator, and because he shares with us the same father of the flesh, Adam; civil peace and unity should be maintained between us. All people are obliged to do this before God and the world: namely, that they should live justly and piously to the praise of God their Creator, as much as is possible for his creatures. To this end, Almighty God has given man natural gifts, such as reason, understanding, knowledge, wit <note>In this context, "wit" refers to intelligence or mental acuity.</note>, strength, a free will, and—as a prompt and guidance for these—the **law of nature**, as well as a natural **conscience**. Through these, God binds everyone to act, so that no one can excuse themselves; just as he rightfully punishes evildoers. Likewise, the **magistrate** <note>The civil authorities or government officials responsible for upholding the law.</note> is obligated to maintain and promote such civil justice and a peaceful, disciplined life, as previously mentioned, for which he also receives his temporal reward from God.

However, poor humanity is unfortunately so corrupted by the evil spirit and by **sin**, becoming disobedient and unfit toward God, that everything good he does by nature, or undertakes by himself without Christ and his **grace**, is done either out of fear of punishment or for the selfish desires of the flesh. It is not done according to God’s pure will—that is, he does not perform the good commanded by God with such affection, heart, and love, nor does he live by his own strengths (however good they may be) in a way that might please God. This is because by nature, man does not seek solely God’s glory, but instead, according to the nature of the corrupt flesh permeated with sin, seeks his own honor and only himself in everything.

<vocab>Righteousness, Piety, Creator, Law of Nature, Conscience, Magistrate, Sin, Grace, Adam</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that while God demands a moral life from all people through the Law of Nature and conscience, human nature is too corrupted by sin to perform these duties with the pure heart and love that God truly requires.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Law, conscience, original sin, civil righteousness, human corruption, magistrate, image of God</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section (page 11) regarding the limitations of human righteousness. While the previous page established that God demands a virtuous life through natural law, this page explains why such outward virtue is insufficient for salvation without divine grace.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

Even if someone applies themselves to the moral virtues—as is right—at God's command, guided by reason, the law of nature, the Scriptures, and admonition, or even out of fear of punishment; and even if they lead a seemingly pious and honorable life before other people (as Paul did before his conversion); nevertheless, the curse and inward sin are still present. This sin maintains its rule within the heart and blackens, corrupts, and makes everything unclean in the eyes of God. God cannot look favorably upon this condition because it is the nature of the evil spirit. Furthermore, a person cannot escape or be rid of this nature without the grace of God, without Christ, and without his blessing. Therefore, the piety of the flesh alone (no matter how good and holy it may seem) is imperfect and insufficient before God; likewise, a person can by no means earn eternal life through it, nor can one become a true Christian through such human righteousness.

## Chapter 4.

### That Everything in Man Without Christ and His Spirit or Grace is Sin Before God.

In the natural man, the internal, innate evil nature of sin, self-seeking, and self-love—along with the fleshly desires of the human heart and the total disorder <note>The original German "Verrücktheit" here implies a "displacement" or "derangement" of man's original, God-given nature.</note> of nature—is still present. Indeed, it is so great and significant that everything a person might most correctly undertake without faith—that is, without Christ and his grace and Spirit—becomes foul-smelling and unpleasant in the sight of God, just as it is reluctant and self-serving in the person themselves.

The natural man, who is without faith, directs everything in which he intends to serve God toward his own benefit; he twists every matter to his own advantage <note>The original idiom "drehet jedem Ding eine Nase auf" suggests manipulating or distorting things for one's own gain.</note> and accomplishes nothing according to the pure, good will of God. Instead, he acts according to the will of his own flesh, for his own use, praise, and honor in everything he might ever attempt or accomplish by his own strength without grace, even if it is outside of God's specific command.

We have a fine mirror <note>In this period, "mirror" was a common term for a clear example or illustration.</note> of this in the Pharisee in the temple in Luke 18; the prophet Isaiah also speaks of this when he says: "We are all as an unclean thing, and all our righteousnesses" (he is speaking there of such human righteousness) "are as a filthy garment." Isaiah 64:6.

<summary>This page argues that outward morality and "fleshly piety" are insufficient for salvation because, without Christ's grace, the human heart remains internally corrupted by self-love and sin. It uses the biblical examples of the Pharisee and the words of Isaiah to illustrate that human righteousness is viewed as "filthy" if it lacks faith.</summary>

<keywords>original sin, human righteousness, grace of God, natural man, self-love, Paul the Apostle, Pharisee and the Publican, Isaiah 64:6, justification by faith</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the corruption of human nature. Having established that internal sin renders even "virtuous" works unclean in God's sight, the author now transitions to the distinction between spiritual holiness and outward morality, and how God perceives the latter.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

And in summary, where the Spirit of God does not sanctify and Christ does not grant grace, there is sin, damnation, and hell. God alone and His Son Jesus Christ are holiness itself. Without Him, man is nothing but vanity <note>The author uses "vanity" in the biblical sense (from the Latin *vanitas*), meaning futility, emptiness, or a lack of lasting substance.</note>. And although God may allow the chosen ones to live honorably even before the time of their visitation—looking to Him, fearing Him, and performing virtuous works—it is still not holy and good in His eyes until He blesses, grants grace to, rebirths, and gifts people with His Spirit through Christ. Afterward, however, these things are no longer counted as evil, but are understood in the best possible light and applied to them as great riches.

Therefore, no one is righteous by nature before God, nor can anyone become righteous through their own strength or works. (The tree must first be good if it is to bring forth good fruit—Matthew 12.) No matter how it may appear before the world, all people have been corrupted, made wretched, and become incapable through Adam's Fall; there is none who does good (that is truly good before God), not even one. (Romans 3:10–12; Psalm 14:3.)

# Chapter 5.

### That God Nevertheless Also Rewards This Life of Human Righteousness, But with Temporal Gifts.

Now, the kind and merciful God nevertheless promises (regardless of the corruption of the flesh and the indwelling evil of sin) to reward all people for the aforementioned honorable life of human righteousness and works of mercy. This is so that they may be certain that God is a rich rewarder of all who hope in Him and seek Him from the heart. Regarding this promise, it is written in Matthew 10 <note>The original text cites Matthew 10:4, but the quotation corresponds to Matthew 10:41-42.</note>, where the Lord says: "He who receives a prophet in the name of a prophet shall receive a prophet's reward," etc. "And whoever gives one of these little ones only a cup of cold water to drink in the name of a disciple, truly I say to you, he shall not go unrewarded," says the Lord.

However, He does not reward such good works—which occur while still in unbelief and without the Spirit of Christ—with eternal life, nor with heavenly goods or gifts, but only with outward <term>temporal gifts</term> <note>Temporal gifts (*zeitliche Gaben*) refer to earthly or worldly blessings—such as health, physical safety, or material prosperity—rather than the eternal gift of salvation.</note> and goods. As Paul says, quoting Moses: "Whoever does them (the works of the law, which constitute a human...

<summary>This page argues that while human virtue is insufficient for salvation without Christ's grace, God still rewards outward moral behavior with earthly blessings. It emphasizes that a person's nature must be transformed by the Spirit before their works can be considered truly "good" in a spiritual sense.</summary>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 5 regarding how God rewards human morality. The previous page ended with the promise that God rewards honorable lives with temporal gifts, even if those actions lack the spiritual perfection required for salvation.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

to present <term>righteousness</term> <note>The German *Gerechtigkeit* refers here to "civil righteousness"—living according to the laws and moral standards of society.</note>) does so (that is, whoever keeps the commandments according to the letter), he shall live therein. Conversely, whoever broke the Law of Moses had to die without mercy on the evidence of two or three witnesses (Hebrews 10:28). He who does not kill outwardly shall not be killed. He who honors father and mother, it shall go well with him, and he shall live long upon the earth. And in short, whoever is humanly pious, righteous, charitable, and merciful—whether they be Jew or Pagan—shall enjoy the benefit of it and receive a temporal reward for it. For this reason, everyone should be stirred up and exhorted toward piety, toward the well-being of their neighbor, and toward works of mercy.

Regarding the Pharisees, the Lord Christ says: They have already received their reward, for they do all their works so that they may be seen by people (Matthew 23:5).

But the Kingdom of Heaven and eternal life can by no means be attained through this [outward] righteousness. Instead, another righteousness—a new, holy piety—must be gifted to the human being from above, out of heaven. It must be given and planted into their heart, soul, and conscience through Jesus Christ in the Holy Spirit. This is called the <term>Righteousness of Christ</term>, a righteousness of faith, the internal righteousness of the heart; and in short, it is the only true righteousness that counts before God, about which we shall hear more hereafter.

The Lord Christ meant this when he spoke to his disciples: "For I say to you, unless your righteousness is better, or more abundant, than that of the Scribes and Pharisees, you will not enter the Kingdom of Heaven."

The Lord is not speaking here of the wicked, evil Pharisees or the godless Scribes who lead a blameworthy and scandalous life. Nor is he speaking of those who seek their justification through human traditions alone. Rather, he is speaking of the Pharisees and zealots of the Law of Moses—of the "pious" Pharisees and Scribes who placed their trust and salvation in their outward virtuous works and human righteousness. He speaks of those who are zealous for the <term>letter of the scripture</term> <note>The "letter" refers to the literal, outward performance of religious rules without the inner spirit or motivation.</note> or for their ceremonies. Indeed, those who were outwardly the best among the Jews according to the works of the Law, but were inwardly without the Spirit of God; they possessed an unclean heart, full of sin, full of extortion, spiritual pride, and filth before God. He presents these to his disciples as an example and intends for them to aim much higher; not that they should only be righteous in outward works before men, but he desires that their hearts also become pure and pious before God.

As if the Lord wanted to say: You all, my children and disciples, who wish to share in the Kingdom of God with me, must look for a better life and a higher <term>righteousness</term> <note>righteousness (*Gerechtigkeit*): In this context, it refers to a spiritual standing before God, rather than just following social rules.</note> than human powers are able to provide or demonstrate, and more than human eyes alone can see and recognize. Indeed, you must become much more pious than even the "pious" Pharisees and the <term>scribes</term> <note>scribes (*Schriftgelehrten*): The professional scholars and teachers of Jewish Law in the time of Jesus.</note> of the Law are. For while they maintain an honorable conduct and a good appearance outwardly before men, they are inwardly in their hearts full of injustice, envy, hatred, greed, pride, and deceit. You must become more pious, better, and more righteous than they are, if you wish to enter into Heaven and the Kingdom of God.

For the Lord intended first (as he does in many other places in the <term>Gospel</term> <note>original: *Evangelio*</note>) to point out the deficiency of all "flesh" <note>The author uses "flesh" (*Fleisch*) in the Pauline sense, meaning human nature apart from God's grace.</note>, which he had come to improve and take away, and to dissolve the works of Satan (who is the head of all hypocrites as well as the godless). He had come to seek and to save that which was lost; yes, to give birth to the human being anew through his Spirit, to reform the heart, and to heal the inward damage, etc., as this is his own work and the office of his Holy Spirit.

Therefore, no "old Adamic flesh" <note>old Adamic flesh: Referring to the fallen nature inherited from the biblical Adam.</note> or natural human being is righteous before God, no matter how righteous and pious they may appear in the eyes of men. Instead, everything that wishes to enter Heaven must first come to the second, heavenly, "new Adam"—which is the Lord Christ—and through him be reformed in the Holy Spirit, improved, and reborn. Indeed, through his grace alone, one must become truly pious, saved, and righteous; regarding this, see Romans 5, verses 17, 19, and 21, and 1 Corinthians 15:22.

Let this now be said briefly regarding the "other life" of man and regarding outward piety—or rather, regarding human righteousness. Paul called this a "righteousness of works," a "righteousness of the Law," or even the "own righteousness" of the Jews (who wished to be the people of God, just as they were a figurative and literal people of God). Paul strongly proves in the Epistles to the Romans and the Galatians that no one can stand before God with such a righteousness, so that no "flesh" may boast in his sight, but "let him who boasts, boast in the Lord" (1 Corinthians 1:31).

# Chapter 6.

### What Paul writes concerning human righteousness and this other life of men.

Regarding such human righteousness and the life of the pious (yet unbelieving) Jews, which Paul also calls a <term>righteousness of works</term> <note>righteousness of works (*Gerechtigkeit der Werke*): The idea that one can earn a standing before God solely through performing religious rituals and following moral laws.</note>, he writes in Romans 10, verses 1–4: Dear brothers! My heart's desire and my prayer to God for Israel is that they might be saved; for I bear them witness that they have a zeal for God, but not according to the right understanding or knowledge. For they do not know the <term>righteousness</term> <note>righteousness (*Gerechtigkeit*): In a theological sense, this refers to being "right" or "justified" in God's eyes.</note> that is valid before God, and they seek to establish their own righteousness, and thus are not subject to the righteousness that counts before God; for Christ is the end of the Law for righteousness to everyone who believes.

With this, Paul clearly intends to show that the aforementioned human righteousness alone is nothing and has no value before God. He wishes to point from the imperfect righteousness of the Law toward the perfect righteousness of faith, which alone is valid before God—that is, from the outward to the inward foundation and essence, and from Moses to Christ. In Christ, and through the knowledge of him, all true righteousness, grace, peace, and salvation are to be found, both inwardly and outwardly. Whoever believes in him shall not be put to shame. Joel 2, verses 26 and 27; Romans 10:11.

And in Philippians 3, Paul also writes in this manner about himself, concerning his former human righteousness and his pious life as a <term>Pharisee</term> <note>Pharisee: A member of a Jewish sect known for strict adherence to the traditional and written Law.</note>. He says that according to righteousness <note>original: "die Gerechtigkeit im Gesetz" — the righteousness in the Law</note> he was blameless.

And further: But what things were gain to me, those I counted loss for Christ's sake; for I count all things as loss compared to the surpassing knowledge of Christ Jesus my Lord. (Paul had, according to the standards of <term>Judaism</term>, given up much spiritual honor, fame, and works, as well as his commendable reputation, good name, and standing for Christ's sake.) For whose sake, Paul says, I have counted all things as loss and regard them as dung <note>dung (*Koth*): A translation of the Greek word *skubala*, used by Paul to emphasize how worthless his previous status was compared to knowing Christ.</note>, that I may win Christ and be found in him, so that I do not have my own righteousness (that is what I mean) which is from the Law, but that which is through the faith of Christ, namely

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 16, which began a quotation of Philippians 3:9–10. The text transitions from the specific scriptural citation to a broader theological explanation of how to read the Epistles of Paul and distinguish between the "Old" and "New" ways of relating to God.</meta>

...the righteousness that comes from God through faith, that I may know him (Christ) and the power of his resurrection and the fellowship of his sufferings.

Beyond this, everything we have said here regarding human righteousness and an outward Pharisaic life—as described in our account of the "other life" of humans—will be easily understood, allowing us to distinguish divine righteousness from it. Just as Paul writes to the Romans and Galatians: that no one can be justified before God through the works of the Law, but rather the righteous shall live by his faith <note>original: *der Gerechte wird seines Glaubens leben*. This is a core Reformation teaching based on Habakkuk 2:4 and Romans 1:17, emphasizing that trust in God, rather than following rules, is what makes a person right with Him.</note>. This does not mean that an outwardly pious life or good works are thereby abolished; rather, it means that all outward righteousness should flow from the inner fountain of God's grace through faith in Christ, so that both outward and inward piety may exist together, if our lives are to truly please God.

Whoever pays close attention to this distinction between these two kinds of life or righteousness—indeed, whoever truly learns this in the "school of Christ"—will only then be able to orient themselves within the epistles of Paul and understand what Paul means by the "works of the Law." He will also begin to truly long for and see the knowledge of Christ (from which flows true <term>godliness</term> <note>godliness (*Gottseligkeit*): A state of living in a way that is devoted to and reflects God's character.</note> and all kinds of divine power that serve our Christian life and conduct) and ask after it with sincerity, etc. Such a person will also know how to properly distinguish between the people of the Law and the people of Grace, as well as the Old and New Testaments.

But whoever does not perceive this, and does not know how to distinguish the people of the Law from the people of Grace—with their two different types of righteousness, promise, doctrine, conscience, life, and worship—will be able to understand neither the righteousness of faith nor the treasure of Christ's overflowing benefits, much less the epistles of Paul. This is clearly visible today among many, where everything—Old and New—is mixed together in a disorderly fashion, and people do not know how to maintain a proper distinction between the two ways God deals with humanity. May our Lord Jesus Christ, who is the sole master of all heavenly art and wisdom, graciously improve everything.

# Chapter 7.

### Concerning the Christian Godly Life.

Thus follows the third kind of life, which we call a Christian godly life and the righteousness of faith, to which Christ has directed his disciples—indeed, all Christians. This life does not consist solely in outward virtuous works, customs, and conduct, or the piety of the "old man" <note>the "old man" (*der alte Mensch*): A Pauline term referring to the human nature before spiritual rebirth, which is governed by worldly desires rather than God's Spirit.</note> guided by reason (as found among the pious Jews or philosophers). Rather, it proceeds in a newness according to the inner impulse and guidance of the Spirit of Christ, and it springs from a virtuous, pious, loving, and pure heart.

However, since the human heart is entirely corrupted by the deception of the evil spirit and by disobedience toward God, it is by nature—as Holy Scripture testifies and experience confirms—evil, wicked, hopeless, treacherous, and full of self-seeking, vanity, and sin. Therefore, another Master must come over this "mastery of ruin" to take away the evil—that is, sin and disobedience toward God—and in its place plant the good—that is, His power and Spirit. Through this, the heart is purified, the innate filth is swept away, and the person is blessed and sanctified. In short, to begin the Christian life, a person must first be made fit and capable through the preceding grace of God; indeed, they must receive a new heart and a new spirit (for which the Prophet David prays in Psalm 51) if they are to lead a Christian, good, and pious life and fulfill the righteousness of God according to His will.

This Master, however, from whom all this comes, is the Son of God, our Lord Jesus Christ. Through Him, in the faith of the sprinkling of His blood <note>original: *Besprengung seines Bluts*. This refers to the biblical imagery of purification and the sealing of a covenant through Christ's sacrifice.</note>, a person is cleansed from sins, reborn, and through the benefit of His cross made capable of such a new life, of obedience to God, and of all good works. This life is therefore called a "Christian" life after Christ; namely, because it takes after the nature of Christ, just as Christ gives, forms, and establishes it through the Spirit of His grace.

All Christian preachers and godly, faithful teachers are duty-bound to promote such a life; for it is their sole office to point faithfully to Christ the Lord, who gives such a Christian life, and to make people rightly acquainted with His Spirit, office, and benefits. Which life alone is an upright,

<summary>This page introduces the "third life"—the true Christian life of faith—contrasting it with mere outward morality or reason-based philosophy. It emphasizes that the human heart is naturally corrupt and requires the transformative grace of Jesus Christ to be reborn and made capable of genuine godliness.</summary>
<keywords>Christian life, righteousness of faith, original sin, grace, Jesus Christ, rebirth, Psalm 51, sanctification, Holy Spirit</keywords>

is a godly life, and the **righteousness of God**—that is, a holy, divine piety that comes through faith; indeed, it brings with it peace and joy of conscience.

This life is a life of righteousness in which Almighty God takes great pleasure; which He will also finally crown and reward as His present and gift, and to whom He will grant His heavenly kingdom for Christ’s sake; so that such a person shall dwell with Him eternally in all bliss and joy. Of this—the reward for the good works of the Christian life—we shall hear more hereafter.

## Chapter 8.

### That the Grace of God is the Beginning of the Christian Life and of All Goodness Within Us.

Such a Christian life comes through Christ, along with all that is good, from the **grace of God**. In this grace, it does not remain idle, nor does it stay at the initial stage, but rather it grows and increases daily toward perfection in Christ.

In the grace of God which *precedes us* <note>precedes us (*zuvorkommt*): This refers to "prevenient grace," the theological concept that God's grace acts on a person's heart before they have even decided to seek Him.</note>, and in which all the elect are chosen from eternity, lies the beginning, foundation, and origin of the new Christian life. In this grace, through its movement and discipline, we also perform repentance, recognize sin, regret it, mourn it, and obtain forgiveness of sins.

The Christian spiritual life is not bound to specific persons or social ranks, nor to any race of people, nor to time, site, or place. God gives it for Christ’s sake, out of mercy, entirely for free, to the praise of His grace, when and to whom He wills. It matters not what or how a person was before.

The Christian spiritual life is also not bound to works, ceremonies, external laws, or church ordinances; such that one might prepare oneself for it through these things or obtain it by them. Rather, it is a life of faith, a life of heavenly novelty, a life of Christ and His Spirit, a divine holy life—a life given out of pure grace and not out of merit <note>merit (*Verdienst*): A central theme in Reformation theology, arguing that humans cannot "earn" their way into God's favor through good deeds.</note>. This may happen to a great, poor sinner just as soon—indeed, much sooner, if he humbles himself before God, repents of his sin, and turns to Christ—than it might to a self-made, "righteous," or arrogant hypocrite...

The author emphasizes that this transformation is available to everyone regardless of status and cannot be earned through religious rituals or laws.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 8, concluding the thought that God's grace is not restricted by social status or location, and then moves into a detailed scriptural defense using the Epistle to Titus.</meta>

not who you are or where you are; as the Lord Jesus spoke to the Pharisees in Matthew 21:31: "Truly I say to you, the tax collectors and prostitutes will enter the kingdom of heaven before you." And elsewhere: "I have come to call sinners to repentance, and not the righteous." Matthew 9:13; Mark 2:17; Luke 5:32.

See, this is the work of the overflowing riches of the goodness, love, and healing grace of God in Christ Jesus our Lord, to whom we rightly give praise and thanks, for He has also mercifully taken pity on us poor sinners. Of these riches of God’s grace, Paul writes to Titus as follows:

> For we also were once unwise, disobedient, deceived, serving various lusts and pleasures, and walking in malice and envy, hating one another. But when the kindness and philanthropy <note>philanthropy (*Leutseligkeit*): The original German text glosses this as "the love toward mankind." It refers to God's benevolent disposition toward humanity.</note> of God our Savior appeared; not for the sake of works of righteousness which we have done, but according to His mercy He saves us through a washing of regeneration and renewal of the Holy Spirit, whom He poured out upon us abundantly through Jesus Christ our Savior, so that we, being made righteous by His grace, should be heirs according to the hope of eternal life.

From this passage, one sees clearly that God with His grace is the beginning of the Christian life and of all good within us. He Himself gives birth to people as children and heirs of His kingdom through grace, as He transforms the "old man" within us with heavenly novelty, converts the sinner, washes the unclean soul in the blood of Christ, and purifies the conscience. Indeed, He makes the wicked, unjust person pious, righteous, and blessed before His face through the "washing of rebirth," as Paul says—which in this context is nothing other than the salvific Word of God, Jesus Christ, and the renewal of the Holy Spirit, who prepares, sanctifies, strengthens, and seals the person for the aforementioned Christian life.

# Chapter 9.

### That Christ alone bestows upon us true righteousness, grace, and heavenly blessings.

The Law, the Psalms, and all the Prophets point toward such a Christian, holy life, renewal, righteousness, and piety, showing that it is established among all true believers through Christ in the Holy Spirit, and that they should become righteous and blessed through him. This is also written in Romans 3, where Saint Paul says that all people are enclosed under sin; <note>The author is referencing Romans 3:9–18, which describes the universal human condition of sinfulness.</note> moreover, not one is righteous before God, nor is there any fear of God before their eyes, and yet no one can become righteous before God through the works of the Law.

But now, says Paul, without the contribution of the Law, the righteousness that counts before God is revealed (he means the righteousness and renewal of the heart), which is witnessed by the Law and the Prophets. I mean, however, says Paul, the righteousness of God which comes through faith in Jesus Christ, in all and upon all who believe; for there is no difference, they are all together sinners and lack the glory of God, being made righteous as a gift of His grace through the redemption that is in Christ Jesus. <note>This is a close paraphrase of Romans 3:21–24.</note>

There we have it once again clearly: where true piety, divine righteousness, along with all good works and heavenly blessings come from; what they are and what they can do; through whom they are given, and how they are received. Likewise, we see that the Holy Scripture, the Law of Moses, the Prophets, and all the servants of God can well testify to these things. However, they are able to give these things neither to themselves nor to anyone else, nor can they in any way distribute God’s heavenly gifts into the heart—just as little as they, with all external things (regardless of what they are called), can reach a person’s heart or renew and regenerate it. <note>The author distinguishes between "external things" like religious rituals or the written Law, and the internal work of the Spirit.</note> For such a task belongs to Christ alone, the Messiah and King of Grace, who was promised there in the Old Testament, but in the New is proclaimed and fulfilled through the Gospel. Therefore, everyone should be directed to Christ alone. Christ is lauded, praised, and proclaimed through the Gospel with His grace, office, and kingdom—that He wishes to make poor, sick, and unrighteous people healthy, blessed, pious, and righteous, if they believe in Him with a true heart, recognize Him, and love Him fervently, etc.

<summary>This chapter argues that while the Law and Prophets testify to righteousness, only Jesus Christ can actually transform the human heart and bestow divine grace. It emphasizes that internal spiritual renewal is a gift received through faith rather than through external adherence to the Law of Moses.</summary>
<keywords>righteousness, grace, faith, Christ, Gospel, renewal of the heart, Paul, Romans 3, Law of Moses</keywords>

# Chapter 10.

### That the True Righteousness of God Must Come Home into Our Hearts Through Christ.

It is also to be noted that the aforementioned righteousness of faith does not remain outside of us in the person of Christ alone, nor is it merely believed and credited to us in the way a master might pardon a wicked servant—showing him favor, looking the other way, or failing to punish him. Not at all; rather, righteousness and <term>justification</term> <note>The theological process by which a person is declared righteous or "made right" before God.</note> in and with Christ must be truly brought down from heaven into our hearts and souls through faith. It must be effectively implanted so that it dwells within us and we possess it tangibly. Through this, we are inwardly renewed, born again, pacified, and made righteous—that is, we become pious, holy people. We become the kind of people who henceforth no longer serve unrighteousness, but rather serve the indwelling righteousness with our minds and bodies. Indeed, the righteousness of God must become like our own disposition, our own property, and—so to speak—our very nature according to the inner man. This is demonstrated outwardly through a Christian life and should be recognized by others through its fruits to the praise of Christ, if we are to be esteemed as righteous in the eyes of God. For "whoever does what is right is righteous" (1 John 3:7). and although Paul complains about the clinging sins and desires of the flesh, he nevertheless says alongside this that he serves the law of God with his mind <note>A reference to Romans 7:25, where the Apostle Paul describes the internal struggle between the "law of the mind" and the "law of sin."</note>, which at its core is nothing other than that divine righteousness of which we speak here.

From this we also understand how Christ, the founder of the true Christian life, did not come to reject or abolish the Law, but to perfectly fulfill everything it pointed toward, everything it testified to, and everything it foreshadowed in shadows. He came to reveal and explain the final will of God and of the commandment—the <term>sum total</term> <note>original: "Hauptsumma." This refers to the core essence or the condensed heart of a teaching.</note> of which consists in love, which comes from a pure heart, a good conscience, and sincere faith—and also to establish and grant it through faith (John 13:35; Romans 3:22–28).

Christ did not come to abolish the life of outward piety or to take away human virtues, honorability, and righteousness, but rather to improve them and to so prepare the corrupted human being that he might fulfill such righteousness required by God (both in the Law of Nature and in the Law of Moses, as well as in the Gospel), according to the command and wi-

<summary>This page argues that divine righteousness is not merely a legal credit from God, but an internal transformation where Christ's nature is physically and spiritually "implanted" into the believer's heart. It emphasizes that Christ came to fulfill and internalize the Law through love and faith, rather than simply abolishing outward moral standards.</summary>
<keywords>justification, indwelling righteousness, renewal, Law of Moses, Law of Nature, internal transformation, 1 John 3:7, Romans 7</keywords>

...the will of God, and through him and his grace can accomplish it, Romans 8. Yes, so that a righteous life and a righteousness well-pleasing to God—namely, a life of the heart and a righteousness of the spirit—may arise from it. Ezekiel 36:27: *I will make such people out of you who walk in my commandments.* 1 John 3:22: *We keep God's commandment and do what is pleasing to him,* and *his commandments are not heavy* <note>original: "nicht schwer." Usually translated in modern Bibles as "not burdensome."</note>, 1 John 5:3.

In short, it is so that from the old person a new person, from the condemned one a blessed one, and from a sinner a holy child of God and heir to the kingdom of heaven might be made. This is the office of Christ, for which reason he also descended from heaven and became man, suffered for us, and died.

# Chapter 11.

### Concerning the State of a Person Who Has Become Righteous Through Faith.

The Lord Christ Jesus has acquired from God through his death, for all his true believers, not only the grace of God for reconciliation, but also the gift within grace—namely, the Spirit of <term>sanctification</term> <note>The theological process of being made holy and purified from sin through the Holy Spirit.</note>. Thus, through Christ, we receive not only the forgiveness of sins but also the power to stop sinning, or to desist from sinful works. We may then fight bravely <note>The original German uses the word "ritterlich" (chivalrously or like a knight), suggesting a noble, spiritual combat.</note> against the fleshly lusts, evil desires, and the innate sinful inclination—as Paul calls it—which still dwell in our flesh. We can also overcome these blessedly in Christ, so that they do not break out into action but are instead subdued and put to death through the power of the Spirit of Christ. In this way, a Christian believer walks not according to the flesh but according to the spirit; for in this life, no one at all is without sin, nor so perfectly righteous and pious that they do not still sin occasionally.

Therefore, we must hold more and more firmly to Christ. He is the one who gives and distributes the Spirit, who fights against the indwelling, innate sin and puts it to death within us. It is this Spirit who makes us children of God, leading us to a new Christian life and to the inheritance of the kingdom of heaven. Through Him, the love of God (which heals and renews the human will) is poured out into our hearts, Romans 5:17–21. How then, while possessing the righteousness of faith, could we wish to live and persist in sin? We have died to sin through <term>regeneration</term> <note>The spiritual rebirth or "being born again" mentioned in the New Testament.</note> and shall in the future live a godly life in Christ our Lord. The faith that makes one righteous is the kind of faith that is active and works through love, Galatians 5:6, Romans 6:4.

Therefore, above all things, one must learn to rightly recognize the Lord Jesus Christ with his benefits, <term>Office of the Cross</term> <note>original: "Kreuzamt." One must understand that Almighty God does not take man into grace, nor refrain from reckoning his sin to him, in such a way that the man remains fundamentally godless or forever an old sinner, or so that sin rules in his mortal body as it did before. 

Not at all; whoever imagines such a thing deceives himself. Rather, as has been heard, after sins are forgiven through Christ, He grants and gives the person in <term>rebirth</term> <note>Also known as regeneration; the spiritual renewal of a person by the Holy Spirit.</note> the Spirit of Grace and his divine power, so that in the future they may live a Christian and godly life. Furthermore, they may, through this granted grace, cleanse themselves from the defilement of sins, fight against temptations, and finally overcome through the Lord Christ.

In the same way that sin ruled unto death before the rebirth of faith, so too in the future must the Spirit and grace rule in righteousness unto eternal life. This is so that the converted Christian believer, having been made righteous through Christ, may now have secure access to God the Father as a child of God, as well as a peaceful, good, joyful, and blameless conscience—all in Christ Jesus our Lord.

To this also belongs what Paul writes in 2 Corinthians 7, verse 1, where he says: Since we then have such promises, beloved (namely, that the Almighty Lord God wants to be our Father through Christ, and we shall be his sons and daughters), let us cleanse ourselves from all defilement of the flesh and the spirit, and continue with <term>sanctification</term> in the fear of God.

But so that the believers need not plead <term>inability</term> <note>original: "Unvermöglichkeit." The argument that one is naturally too weak to do good.</note>, Paul says in another place: I can do all things through him who makes me strong, namely in Christ. From this, it is easily seen from whence the believers’ ability to do good, or their free will to serve God, comes: not from nature, but from Christ and his gifted grace. As the Lord also says in the Gospel: All things are possible for the believer. Therefore, true Christian faith is a magnificent, precious thing and a dear gift of God.

Thus, Christ is also given by God as the <term>Mediator</term> and Head of the congregation, which is his body, so that he may pour such divine power, grace, life, and whatever is necessary for our <term>salvation</term> <note>original: "Seligkeit." Often translated as "blessedness" or "salvation."</note> through the Holy Spirit into the members of his body—that is, into all...

# Chapter 12.

### What weak-believing Christians are.

Here we should also note that the situation with true-believing Christians is not as some now think out of lack of understanding—those who, despite their unrepentant, godless lives and sinful nature, still want to be considered believers and good Christians. When one confronts them with their sinful and offensive lives, they say they are "weak" and that one should "bear their weakness," citing Paul in Romans 15 <note>Romans 15:1: "We who are strong ought to bear with the failings of the weak and not to please ourselves."</note> for this purpose. But what kind of "weakness" is their weakness? Since they have neither accepted Christ with true faith, nor ever seriously begun a Christian, godly life.

Therefore, the saying of Paul to the Romans, Chapter 15, where he writes: "We who are strong (or capable) ought to bear the weakness of the weak (or the incapable)," has a much different meaning than such carnal people wish to consider regarding their weakness, or rather, their godlessness.

For although the beginners in faith are at first weak, so that they still enjoy <term>milk-food</term> <note>A biblical metaphor for basic Christian teachings suitable for "spiritual infants," as opposed to "solid food" for the mature (1 Corinthians 3:2).</note> and have senses unpracticed in the word of righteousness; yes, like children in the kingdom of God, they soon go astray, often stumble, or even fall and do not understand everything immediately, nor can they quickly grasp in their conscience the <term>freedom of Christians</term> <note>The theological concept that a believer is liberated from the condemnation of the law and the power of sin.</note> of which Paul speaks there—they are nevertheless believers, also <term>reborn</term> and children of God.

These are people devoted to God, who have now entered through Christ into a new Christian, godly life, as they

...[they should] also earnestly strive to become ever more understanding in the knowledge of Christ, stronger in faith, and more perfect from day to day in the growth of God's grace and of the new man.

Saint Paul commands that such "weak ones" in the faith be welcomed, that their weakness be borne, and that their consciences not be confused (with unnecessary things), etc. Of such weak ones, he also says in that same place that they eat herbs. <note>original: Röm. 14, 2. This refers to Romans 14:2, where Paul discusses believers with sensitive consciences who restricted their diets to vegetables to avoid ritual impurity.</note>

But our modern "weak" Christians want only to eat and drink the best, living in all manner of greed, pride, and the lust of sin; yet they still wish to be compared to those good-hearted, weak-believing children of God—using this saying of Paul's as a pretext to sugarcoat their unrepentant lives. Woe unto them, however, if they do not repent, because they willfully abuse Holy Scripture as a cloak for their sins, doing so in such a ruinous and wretched way to their own harm.

However, what truly weak-believing Christians are can also easily be learned from the disciples of Christ, whom the Lord called "weak-believing" <note>original: "Schwachgläubige." In many English Bibles, this is translated as "you of little faith."</note> during the days of His flesh. He also often rebuked them because of their weak faith, yet He did not cast them away for it; instead, He faithfully helped them toward strength of faith (for which they also prayed and cried out: "O Lord, strengthen our faith!").

Therefore, let everyone take care that they do not deceive themselves with their imagined weakness—or rather, their godlessness—nor let themselves be deceived by hypocrites into considering themselves "weak-believing" Christians when they have never yet become truly believing, nor a child of God, nor even a Christian—that is, they have not yet become an "anointed one" <note>The author is playing on the etymology of "Christian," derived from the Greek *Christos*, meaning "Anointed."</note> of the Lord. One can quickly perceive this: if he is not God-fearing and does not know Christ according to the Spirit, nor loves Him from the heart, nor enters into repentance through His name; indeed, if he has never seriously begun a truly new Christian, godly life, but instead persists continually in the old godless worldly life and in all unrighteousness, and yet still wishes to console himself with the rights of the children of God and be called a Christian and "weak-believing." He who has ears to hear, let him hear, says Christ our Lord.

# Chapter 13.

### Regarding Christ's Office of Salvation, and How He is the End and Fulfillment of the Law. Romans 10, v. 4.

In conclusion, in the glorified, reigning Christ, all the fullness of God, the heavenly divine truth, and the one true righteous essence are to be found. Whoever accepts Christ with true faith and earnestly attends to the office of His grace cannot remain forever weak; rather, he grows and becomes strong in Him to fight against sin, death, the devil, and hell, as well as against the whole world. For indeed, the faith of Christ is the only victory that overcomes the world. 1 John 5.

It was thus decreed by God the Heavenly One that Christ should be the end, ground, sum, and fulfillment of the Law, for the righteousness of all who believe. <note>original: "Christus des Gesetzes Ende." This refers to Romans 10:4, a central tenet for the author: that Christ replaces the requirement of following the Mosaic Law with a life of faith.</note> The Law was the shadow and presented a figurative, outward righteousness, as heard before; but Christ, as the body <note>By "body," the author means the "substance" or "reality" that casts the shadow.</note>, was to produce the true righteousness—the inward piety—which the outward then easily follows.

He was to pour the oil into the lamps <note>A reference to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25, where oil represents the Holy Spirit or true faith.</note>, bring the Spirit to the works, and give the heart to piety; yes, He was to establish and set upright the true love of God and of neighbor. The entire fulfillment of the Law was placed upon Christ, so that He should not only fulfill it for His own person and make satisfaction for us on the cross <note>original: "genug thun." This refers to the doctrine of "penal substitutionary atonement" or "satisfaction," where Christ pays the debt for human sin.</note>, but that He should also lead us through His Spirit into the ultimate will of God. He was to make us partakers in the truth of His fulfillment—indeed, of the divine eternal essence—through faith.

I say once again: Christ did not fulfill everything in such a way that we merely believe in His fulfillment or satisfaction in a historical manner, as some think with a fictional, intellectual faith <term>Vernunftglauben: a faith based only on human reason or intellectual assent to facts, lacking a spiritual change of heart</term>. Such people cling to the comforting sayings of God’s mercy promised in Christ, comforting themselves with them, and yet never earnestly amend their deeds and lives; instead, they continually remain old, godless, sinful people. This is, in truth, a futile and false comfort.

In such a way, our righteousness would not be any better—indeed, it would be worse—than the righteousness of the scribes and Pharisees. Rather, everything is fulfilled through Christ in such a way that we...

This implies a superficial, second-hand faith—one that follows along with others like a journeyman apprentice without having a personal, internal conviction.</note>, nor is it a righteousness merely credited to us <note>The author is cautioning against a "forensic" justification that remains external and does not change the person's character.</note>, which would only lead to human fables, deceit, and seduction; rather, more is required if we wish to enter heaven and become righteous, pious, and blessed before God.

He places His law—the law of the Spirit and of life, which converts souls (Psalm 19, verses 8 and 9)—into our hearts. This law is essentially nothing other than the reigning, spiritual Christ, the living, incarnate Word of God, written into our minds as promised by the prophet Jeremiah, so that the righteousness pointed to and figuratively presented by the Law might also be fulfilled in us through Christ in His Spirit (Romans 8:4). Thus, Christ may be "all in all" (Colossians 3), and we may henceforth walk not according to the flesh but according to the Spirit, to the praise of God and for eternal life. May the Lord Jesus Christ grant us this, Amen.

# Chapter 14.

### Regarding the Reward of Good Works in General.

Since the Christian, godly life of the believing "new man" is full of all good works, and God leaves no good works unrewarded, we also wish to speak of the reward of good works. We will summarize what was previously written about the other life and human righteousness, and explain it further for better understanding. In my opinion, a sound and consistent account in this matter is necessary, because in times past, much that is absurd regarding works and faith has been preached and taught here and there—often without foundation or judgment—and with such corruption of consciences that people generally now hardly know either

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of righteousness. The previous page ended with a description of the "living, incarnate Law" (Christ). This page begins by concluding a thought about the state of someone who has lost faith and works, then moves into a detailed distinction between "natural" good works and works of faith.</meta>

->❦❦❦❦❦<-

...has faith nor works anymore; may the Lord Christ Jesus improve it all with grace.

First, a distinction should be well noted: that Holy Scripture speaks of two kinds of good works—those performed apart from faith and out of natural piety, as well as those arising from faith—just as it speaks of a twofold righteousness and a twofold reward.

First, it speaks of those good works performed without grace and the Spirit, out of a person’s own strength, either for the sake of a reward or out of fear of God’s punishment and the desire to turn it away.

Secondly, however, it speaks of good works as they occur in the grace of Christ through God, done for His praise alone with a voluntary, reborn heart; these are called works of faith and works of Christ, and are the primary works of the Law or of moral virtues.

Such are all the outward works of love, charity, and mercy which people perform for one another or show to the poor and needy while they still stand outside the grace of God, without Christ and His Spirit, for the sake of their own benefit.

For example, when a blasphemer, a usurer <term>usurer: historically, a person who lends money at interest, often seen as a predatory or sinful practice</term>, an adulterer, or some other unjust person shows someone a kindness—when he gives alms, makes offerings, prays, fasts, or otherwise practices works of mercy—and yet never seriously intends to improve his sinful life; or when a rich man thinks to buy heaven with his works, in which he trusts and hopes, indeed, making an idol of them: and yet otherwise pursues neither the true knowledge of Christ nor a Christian life or godliness. All of these are called works of unbelief, which can be common to Pagans, Turks <note>original: "Türken." In the 17th and 18th centuries, this term was frequently used by European writers as a general designation for Muslims.</note>, and Jews.

Although such good works are good only before men, but are not truly good before God—in such a way that they do not reach the Kingdom of Heaven—the Almighty Lord, according to His goodness and mildness, still wishes to reward them, because He commanded them to be done; just as He has promised a reward for them, He also intends to punish the transgressors of the same, and the sinful, evil works, in manifold ways—and if one does not practice repentance <note>original: "Buße." This implies both the internal change of heart (repentance) and the external act of turning back to God.</note>, with hell and eternal damnation.

However, the Lord God does not reward such good works in the same manner as the works of faith, with the joy of eternal li—

<summary>This page distinguishes between "natural" good works (done out of fear or for self-interest) and "spiritual" works of faith (done through Christ’s grace). It explains that while God rewards natural virtues on earth, only works of faith through a reborn heart lead to the Kingdom of Heaven.</summary>
<keywords>good works, faith, righteousness, grace, reward, natural piety, works of unbelief, repentance, charity, moral virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 29 regarding the distinction between works of faith and natural "good works." The previous page ended mid-word, concluding that a person focused only on outward works without Christ does not lead a true Christian life.</meta>

...life, but rather here in this time, with bodily gifts, goods, health, children, desired peace, long life, with good peace, also with victory against enemies, a prosperous state, worldly honor, and whatever else constitutes such temporal welfare. This is prefigured <note>The author uses the story of Abraham's inheritance as a "figure" or symbolic type for how God distributes rewards.</note> in how the patriarch Abraham gave all his goods to Isaac, the son of the free woman: but the children of the concubines he sent away with gifts, Genesis 25:6.

That such human good works are also rewarded by God is not hidden from those who read the scriptures of the Old Testament, such as Genesis 20:7. There, God rewarded King Abimelech for the kindness he showed to Abraham, the friend of God, with the temporal gifts of children and the increase of his lineage.

He rewards the God-fearing midwives of the Egyptians and builds houses for them because they let the newborn children of His people live—namely the Jews—and refused to kill them according to the strict command of King Pharaoh, Exodus 1:21.

Regarding such a reward, God the Lord also speaks through a prophet to the Jews, saying: "Your work has its reward," 2 Chronicles 15:7. And following this in the 19th chapter, we have a very clear example of such temporal rewards in King Jehoshaphat <note>original: "Josaphat"</note>, upon whom the wrath of God rested because he helped the godless and loved those who hated the Lord, walking in the evil ways of his father Asa. However, because he performed a good work in abolishing the high places and groves—that is, the idolatries—proclaimed fasting and prayer, and brought the people together to seek the Lord, God nonetheless rewarded him for this, but temporally: by giving him rest in his kingdom and letting him see victory and joy over his enemies. Yet Jehoshaphat did not turn from his evil ways so that he might have done what would fully please the Lord; therefore, such a good work as the abolition of idolatry and false worship <note>referring to the removal of pagan shrines</note> could ultimately not help him regarding his soul's salvation.

Thus, God the merciful Lord also rewards all kings, princes, authorities, and potentates—yes, even those who still remain in unbelief and outside of God’s grace—for their kindnesses and good works which they show to Christians and His children, when they allow pious, God-fearing people to dwell among them, give them shelter <term>shelter: the original term "Unterschleif" implies providing harbor or refuge</term>, and offer support and goodness for the sake of God or Christ. He rewards them all, I say, even the heathens and unbelievers, but only with temporal goods and gifts: with rest, peace, good government, the turning away of temporal punishment, and a long life.

<summary>This page argues that God rewards even non-believers or flawed rulers with "temporal" or worldly blessings (wealth, peace, children) when they perform naturally good deeds, though these rewards do not grant eternal salvation. It cites biblical figures like Abimelech, the Egyptian midwives, and King Jehoshaphat as examples of this earthly recompense.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that God rewards temporal good works even when performed by those outside the faith. The previous page discussed how God rewarded King Abimelech and the Egyptian midwives with earthly blessings for their kindness toward His people.</meta>

-> ❧ ❧ ❧ ❧ ❧ <-

This is also what the Lord Christ promises in the Gospel <note>original: "Evangelio"</note> to those who do good to His own; and where He speaks: "Whoever gives you a cup of water to drink in my name, because you belong to Christ, truly I say to you, he will not go unrewarded," etc., as we have heard before.

Now, however, Christians are also faithfully bound to pray to God the Lord for such benefactors, protectors, superiors, and patrons, even if they were heathens or unbelievers. As the prophet Baruch writes in Chapter 1, verse 11: that the people of God, namely the Jews who lived in Babylon under the shadow of King Nebuchadnezzar <note>original: "Nabucadnezar"</note> and his son Belshazzar <note>original: "Belſazer"</note>, offered sacrifices and prayed for their long lives.

We read the same in the book of Ezra, that King Cyrus <note>original: "Chores"</note> of Persia allowed the Jewish people to build the temple in Jerusalem, even at his own expense, and gave back to them the silver and golden vessels of the house of God (which was truly a good work). He desired that they should offer sacrifices to the God of heaven and pray for the life of the king and his children, Ezra 6:10; in which matter God undoubtedly heard them and richly rewarded him, the king, for his aforementioned good work.

And in Jeremiah 29:7, the Lord of Sabaoth <term>Sabaoth: A Hebrew title for God meaning "Lord of Hosts" or "Lord of Armies."</term>, the God of Israel, speaks to all the captives whom He had let be carried away from Jerusalem toward Babylon: "Seek the best for the city where I have let you be carried away, and pray for it to the Lord, for when it goes well for the city, it goes well for you too."

What else is such prosperity than a temporal reward for the good work of even the unbelievers, in that they let the children of God dwell among them, do not harm them, and even support them so that they may serve God and lead a quiet, peaceful life in all godliness.

Toward this then Paul also looked in 1 Timothy 2:1 and 2, and exhorted Timothy to instruct the Christians that they should offer petitions, prayers, intercessions, and thanksgivings for kings—including those of the heathens among whom they lived—and for all authority, indeed for all people. For this is good, he says, and also pleasing before God our Savior, who wants all people to be saved <note>original: "geneſen," here in the sense of spiritual healing or salvation</note> and to come to the knowledge of the truth. Just as the prayer of the righteous man avails much, James 5:16, so that it can obtain from God not only physical health or temporal welfare, but also the conversion of the sinner, faith, and knowledge of the truth—which are things that every person must have for themselves if they are to be saved.

It cites biblical examples such as Cyrus of Persia and the Babylonian exile to demonstrate that the welfare of the faithful is linked to the stability of the state.</summary>

# Chapter 15.

### That everyone should be exhorted to give alms and to perform works of love and mercy, also for the sake of the promised reward.

However, the good moral works of human righteousness, repentance, love, and mercy just mentioned—which occur even among the heathens <note>The author uses "heathen" to refer to anyone outside the Judeo-Christian faith tradition.</note> in their unbelief—as well as the giving of alms, are not in vain. Rather, they are useful and often turn away God’s wrath and physical punishment. We have an example of this in the prophet Jonah regarding the Ninevites. Upon the proclamation of God’s wrath, they entered into a state of sorrow and repentance; their king had a fast and prayer proclaimed, and he even covered himself with sackcloth and sat in the ashes. He commanded that everyone should turn from their evil ways and from the violence of their hands. When God saw their works, that they turned from their evil ways, He relented of the disaster that He had said He would bring upon them, and He did not do it (Jonah 3). All of this occurred as a prefiguration <note>original: "in einer Figur"</note> in outward actions and works, pointing toward the true giver and preacher of repentance, namely toward Christ our High Priest and toward His Christian church.

At this point—namely, regarding the reward for works—the passage from Daniel 4:24 also belongs. There, the prophet interprets for King Nebuchadnezzar <note>original: "Nabucadnezar"</note> his heavy dream of the wrath and punishment of God, and then says: "Therefore, O King, let my counsel be acceptable to you: break off your sins by righteousness, and your iniquities by showing mercy or kindness to the poor, so that God may have patience with your transgression or sins." That is: be righteous, practice mercy, give alms, and do good to the poor, and so God will turn His punishment away from you.

The prophet is not speaking here of the forgiveness of sins that reaches into eternal life and brings the Holy Spirit with it, but rather of the turning away of temporal, physical punishment, which was to come upon King Nebuchadnezzar on account of his transgression and injustice, according to the indication of the heavy dream.

The passage in the book of Tobit <note>original: "Tobia"</note> concerning the reward for alms also belongs here, where the angel of God speaks to him and his son: "Alms deliver from death, purge away sin, and keep... <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; the catchword "bey" suggests the next page continues with "bey Leben," meaning "keep one in life."</note>

<summary>This page argues that outward acts of mercy and almsgiving, even by non-believers, can avert God's earthly punishment, using the biblical examples of Nineveh and Nebuchadnezzar. It distinguishes these temporal rewards from the eternal salvation that comes only through the Holy Spirit.</summary>

Then you will gather a righteous reward in the time of need, for alms deliver from all enemies, even from death, and do not leave one in distress, Tobit 4:7-11.

In the same way, God has promised a reward for alms and good works through the prophet Isaiah, where he says: Share your bread with the hungry, and bring those who are in misery into the house. Then you will call, and the Lord will answer; when you cry out, He will say, "Here I am." Isaiah 58:7-9.

You help him through all his illness.

All this is spoken and promised regarding the works of mercy <term>Barmherzigkeit: the active practice of compassion, specifically through charitable deeds</term>, which God demands of all people. He wants everyone to be stirred, exhorted, and invited to this for the sake of the reward, just as one is accustomed to do with young children <note>The author compares God’s promise of rewards to a parent encouraging a child to behave well through small incentives.</note>. As the nature of the works is, so also is their reward: if they are done in faith from a new, reborn heart, solely for the praise and glory of God, then the merciful God will crown and reward them with spiritual gifts, and finally with the joy of eternal life. But if they are done only outwardly according to the letter, then an outward, literal reward is also promised for them.

Thus, the land of Canaan was promised to the Jews—a land flowing with milk and honey—which likewise granted protection and shield from their enemies and much other good, on one condition: namely, that they would fear the Lord and keep His commandments or the law according to the command of Moses, who...

Since the Jews did not do so <note>Likely referring to failing to look beyond the "letter" to the spiritual meaning.</note>, they were abandoned by God, driven out again by the King of Babylon, and scattered into all lands and cast out.

-># Chapter 16.<-

->### That good works are always valid and useful.<-

Since this is how it stands regarding God's commandments and good works, it follows that the common man sings quite indiscreetly—even wrongly—in a song: *Works never count for anything*, etc. <note>This likely refers to a popular hymn or proverb that emphasized "faith alone" so strongly that it seemed to dismiss the value of all actions.</note> For although faith, indeed Christ with His grace alone, makes us righteous and blessed, good works commanded by God are nevertheless always valid, insofar as they are good and useful. However, one must properly distinguish their benefit—as has just been explained—according to the order of God and Holy Scripture. One should not throw everything into one pile or mix them together, nor should one indiscreetly cast aside works of love and mercy.

Although temporal goods and prosperity are often given to wicked, godless tyrants—even to those who lead a beast-like life before God and practice no righteousness or works of mercy—some therefore believe that such goods should more appropriately be called a gift from God than a reward. While this is true regarding the ungodly, we nevertheless abide by Scripture and the aforementioned words of the Lord Christ, where He says generally of all people: *Whoever gives a cup of water to the least of His disciples, it shall not remain unrewarded.* <note>A reference to Matthew 10:42.</note> With what else should such good works be rewarded for a repentant unbeliever <note>original: "bußfertigen Ungläubigen"; likely referring to someone who performs a "good" act externally without having the Christian faith.</note> other than with physical goods, honor, peace, health, and temporal prosperity? Therefore, such temporal things are not unfairly called a reward, according to the stated order of God's promise.

<summary>This page argues that while faith in Christ is the sole source of salvation, good works are still commanded by God and result in temporal rewards like health, peace, and prosperity, even for those who are not yet believers.</summary>

# Chapter 17.

### Concerning the Reward of the Ungodly.

God, the kind, merciful, and almighty Lord, certainly scatters temporal goods among mankind as among His good and evil creatures alike; He also gives the ungodly temporal goods and honor so that He might thereby awaken them to the knowledge, praise, and obedience of Him. He causes it to rain upon the righteous and the unrighteous, Matthew 6 <note>Original: Matth. 6. The author is referencing the famous passage about God's universal providence, though the verse is actually found in Matthew 5:45.</note>. Of this also the 73rd Psalm speaks, noting that God does this to see how one intends to use His gifts, and whether one will be thankful to Him for them, etc., so that no flesh <note>A biblical idiom meaning "no human being."</note> may have an excuse before Him.

However, such ungodly wealthy people—who do no good for their neighbor, who rule tyrannically and are unmerciful, or who live like beasts without any fear of God—often have little true joy or delight in their temporal goods and riches. Instead, they have much tribulation, anxiety, adversity, and suffering; yes, even in the midst of their best days, many of them have an ever-gnawing worm <note>A traditional metaphor for a guilty or restless conscience, often referencing Mark 9:44.</note> and a restless conscience, yet because of their blindness, they cannot perceive nor consider where such things come from. They know so little of sin and God's wrath; and as the Psalm says: God lets them be cut down like grass and suddenly wither like green herbs <note>Reference to Psalm 37:2.</note>. So also says Psalm 55:24: That the bloodthirsty and deceitful shall not live out half their days; death overtakes them, and they must go down alive into hell.

And as it is written in Job: That the glory of the ungodly does not last long, and the joy of the hypocrite lasts only a moment, etc. His children shall go begging, and his <insert>miserly</insert> hand shall give him trouble as his reward. The food within his body shall be transformed into <term>viper's gall</term> <note>Original: Ottergallen. In early modern German, "Otter" referred to adders or vipers. "Gall" was considered the source of a snake's poison; the text suggests the wealth they "swallowed" turns to internal poison.</note>. The goods he has swallowed, he must vomit up again, and God will drive them out of his belly. He shall suck the poison of vipers, and the tongue of the serpent shall kill him. He shall not see the streams nor the water-brooks that flow with butter and honey. He shall labor and yet not enjoy it, and his goods shall go to others so that he has no joy in them, for he has oppressed and forsaken the poor. He has seized houses that he did not build, for his belly could not be filled, etc. Therefore, his good life shall have no endurance; even when he has plenty and enough, he will nonetheless be filled with anxiety. All kinds of trouble will come upon him, and God will send the fury of His wrath upon him. He will not hear his crying when the distress comes upon him. Terrors shall overtake him like water; in the night the storm shall carry him away, etc. This is the reward of an ungodly person before God.

<summary>This chapter explains that the worldly success of the wicked is a temporary test from God, which ultimately results in internal misery, a restless conscience, and divine judgment.</summary>
<keywords>ungodly, temporal goods, divine justice, conscience, Job, Psalms, punishment, wealth, greed</keywords>
