...and the heritage of the tyrants, which they shall receive from the Almighty. Job, in the 20th and 27th chapters.

Let this suffice regarding the reward for the two types of life previously mentioned—the virtuous and the godless lives of men—and the utility of works of human righteousness. Now follows the third type: the reward for works of faith and a Christian, godly life.

# Chapter 18.

### What is Meant by "Works of Faith" and a 
### "Christian, Godly Life."

What is meant by "works of faith" and a Christian life has already been heard in part: namely, that they are the works of a new, reborn heart, the fruit of a good tree, a testimony of God's grace, and an assurance of eternal salvation. These works of faith are modeled for us from the very beginning in Abel and his sacrifice, just as the works of unbelief are shown in Cain and his sacrifice, of which it is written in Genesis 4: 

Cain brought an offering to the Lord from the fruits of the field, and Abel also brought of the firstlings of his flock and of their fat; and the Lord looked favorably upon Abel and his sacrifice, but He did not look favorably upon Cain and his sacrifice. When Cain grew angry and his countenance changed <note>Original: "sein Geberd sich verstellte"—referring to his expression falling or becoming distorted with rage.</note>, the Lord said to Cain: "Why are you angry?" etc. Is it not so: if you are pious, you are accepted; but if you are not pious, sin rests at the door (that is, it will be revealed). But do not let it have its way, but rather rule over it. From this, it is easy to understand that a person must first be made good and pious through the grace of God in Christ before he can perform good works pleasing to God.

Therefore, works of faith are works of the "new man" <note>A theological term referring to the spiritually transformed person as described in the New Testament (e.g., Ephesians 4:24).</note>, where the Lord God looks not only at the work itself, but primarily and more importantly at the person of the worker—indeed, at Christ and His Spirit within the faithful, reborn heart. It is through this Spirit that such good works occur, and for this reason, they are judged by God to be worthy of a heavenly reward. This is not for the sake of the works in themselves—so that no one may place their pride, trust, or satisfaction in their own deeds—but they are good for Christ’s sake, who dwells in all believers and works all good within them through His Spirit, so that whoever boasts should boast only in Christ our Lord.

Using the story of Cain and Abel, the author explains that a person's spiritual state must be made "good" through Christ before their actions can be pleasing to God.</summary>

...of which Paul also writes to the Ephesians, where he says: By grace you have been saved, through faith, and this is not from yourselves; it is the gift of God, not of works, so that no one may boast, for we are his workmanship <note>original: "Factur und Gemächte"—referring to something fashioned or manufactured by a creator</note> (Paul means the new creatures and believing people) created in Jesus Christ for good works, for which God has prepared us beforehand (namely, through his preceding grace and rebirth) so that we should walk in them.

## Chapter 19.

### That the Reigning Christ Himself Performs the Good Works of the Christian Life within the Hearts of Believers.

Therefore, Paul exhorts elsewhere in the name of the Corinthians that we should test ourselves whether we are in the faith, that we should examine and recognize whether Jesus Christ is in us; for if that were not so, then we would be disqualified <note>original: "untüchtig"—meaning unfit, useless, or failing the test</note>, 2 Corinthians 13:5. Now, if we are in faith and Christ is in us, then we can easily be assured of Christ and the righteousness of faith, as well as our good works and their heavenly eternal reward, without any doubt or hindrance; for we are not the ones who work, but it is Christ who works in us, both the willing and the doing, just as he is all in all, Colossians 3:11, Philippians 2:13. As we should then also say with the Prophet: Not unto us, O Lord, not unto us, but to your name give the glory, Psalm 115:1.

But that Christ himself works good works in all believers through his Spirit, we may soon notice from the Gospel parable of the vine and the branches, if we will rightly consider it. Also from what we know and understand of how Christ is the head of his congregation; therefore he says, just as the branch can bring forth no fruit from itself unless it remains on the vine, so also you cannot, unless you remain in me; I am the vine, you are the branches, whoever remains in me and I in him brings much fruit, for without me you can do nothing, says the Lord, John 15:1-5.

Paul also wrote of this in Romans 15:16-18, where he says of the conversion of the Gentiles: how they become an acceptable sacrifice in his service through the Gospel, sanctified by the Holy Spirit <term>etc.</term> <note>The source uses the abbreviation "tc." for "etcetera."</note> and speaks further: Therefore I can boast in Jesus Christ that I serve God (or can boast of God's dealings), for I would not dare to speak anything if Christ did not work the same through me.

<summary>This page argues that good works are not a human achievement but are performed by Christ dwelling within the believer. It emphasizes that believers are "handiwork" created for these works and uses the metaphor of the vine and branches to show total dependence on God's power.</summary>
<keywords>grace, faith, good works, workmanship, indwelling of Christ, Vine and Branches, Saint Paul, sanctification</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous chapter, asserting that the "new creature" is not the author of their own good deeds; rather, it is the indwelling presence of Christ that performs these actions through the believer. It shifts from the internal transformation of the soul to the external evidence of that transformation: good works.</meta>

And 1 Corinthians 15:10: *I have labored much more than they all.* Whereupon he immediately softens this and says: *Yet not I, but the grace of God which is in me.* As he also says before: *By the grace of God I am what I am, and his grace toward me was not in vain.* Now, the grace of God is fundamentally nothing other than the reigning Lord Christ in the Holy Spirit.

In the same way, Paul writes in 2 Corinthians 18 <note>The source manuscript cites chapter 18, but the Epistle of 2 Corinthians contains only 13 chapters; this likely refers to 2 Corinthians 13:2–3.</note>, 2 and 3, that Christ speaks in him, when he says: *When I come again, I will not spare; since you seek a proof of him who speaks in me, that is Christ.* From this it follows that Christ, through the Spirit of his grace, performs all good works in us; indeed, everything that is to please God, he himself works and fulfills in the Christian believers through his Spirit.

Since Christ himself is the one who speaks, acts, and fulfills all good works in the believers, who would then say that such works of Christ were not truly perfect, good, and pleasing to God? Indeed, who would say that they were not also worthy of all heavenly reward for the sake of Christ and his Spirit, just as he himself, in the Spirit of his grace, makes them worthy of eternal life, which he has earned for us.

To this also belongs what is written in 2 Peter 1:5–8, where Saint Peter exhorts: *That we should apply all our diligence.* First, that we should learn to rightly know Christ, through which knowledge all kinds of power, and whatever serves a godly life and conduct, is given to us, so that we may thereby become partakers of the divine nature.

Secondly, he recounts the works of faith, and both brotherly love and common love <note>original: "gemeinen Liebe"—referring to universal Christian charity or "agape" toward all people, distinguishing it from "Bruderliebe" or love for fellow believers.</note>, and then concludes: *Therefore, dear brothers, be all the more diligent to make your calling and election sure (through good works), for if you do these things, you will not stumble. And thus the entrance into the eternal kingdom of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ will be richly provided for you.*

But this happens when we take hold of the living, reigning Christ in faith, are reborn through him, and let Christ dwell and work in our hearts; yes, when we, as pious children of God, follow the impulse <note>original: "Triebe"—referring to an inner driving force or divine instinct.</note> toward all good works and surrender our members as instruments <note>original: "Waffen"—literally "weapons" or "armor," following the imagery in Romans 6:13 regarding the "weapons of righteousness."</note> of righteousness, then through Christ, and for the sake of our works, we become certain of the entrance into the kingdom of heaven and the inheritance <meta>catchword: schaft (from Erbschaft)</meta> of eternal life.

<summary>This page argues that the "Grace of God" is the living presence of Christ within a believer, who performs all truly good works through them. By cooperating with this inner divine impulse, a person gains assurance of their salvation and their heavenly inheritance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument that human works have no merit of their own. It concludes the previous discussion on the source of good works (Christ acting through the believer) and begins a new chapter categorizing these works into three specific types: those directed toward God, toward others, and toward oneself.</meta>

I say once again: it is not that we may merit such things by our own works—insofar as they are ours—but rather Christ earned it once and for all on the cross. Everything of His which He works in us, He gives and grants to us out of grace, and He desires to reward or crown and endow those works with eternal life.

All faithful lovers and servants of Christ also trustingly hope in this grace-filled reward through faith, according to His gentle promise and gracious word. They do this not for the sake of the reward itself, but out of bound duty and love to God. They hope that after this life, they will dwell eternally in bliss and joy with their Lord and King Christ in the kingdom of heaven, in the magnificence and glory of God, to which they are also sealed by the Holy Spirit. Amen.

# Chapter 20.

### Regarding the Three Kinds of Good Works of Faith and the Christian Life.

But before I write further of this, I first wish to recount the good works of the Christian life or of faith in general. These may all be distinguished or divided into three types of works: namely, good works toward God, good works toward our neighbor, and good works toward ourselves—all three of which Paul calls "sacrifices," <note>Paul uses the language of "living sacrifices" in Romans 12:1 to describe the Christian life.</note> as we shall hear.

### Regarding the Good Works of the Praise of God, and of the Confession and Cross of Christ.

The first good works of the Christian life and of true faith are prayer, invocation, praise, and thanksgiving for all of God's grace, gifts, and benefits. That Paul calls such works "sacrifices of praise" is written in Hebrews 13:15, where he says:

> Through him then let us offer up a sacrifice of praise to God continually, that is, the fruit of lips that acknowledge his name. Concerning this, it is also written in Psalm 50:14 and 15: Offer to God a sacrifice of thanksgiving, and perform your vows to the Most High, and call upon me in the day of trouble; I will deliver you, and you shall glorify me. Likewise, <note>original: "Item"</note> Psalm 119:108: Accept the free-will offerings of my mouth, O Lord, and teach me your laws.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the three categories of good works. Having established that all works are gifts of grace, the author now details the "first order" of such works: those directed toward God through confession and the endurance of suffering.</meta>

To this category also belongs the invocation and the confession <note>The German *Bekenntniß* refers to the public declaration of one's faith, especially in the face of opposition.</note> of the name of the Lord Jesus Christ, as well as the praise and the promotion of His glory, honor, and majesty, along with the knowledge of Him and of all divine truth. To this first order of good works belong all those things for which the Lord Christ has promised eternal reward and joy, provided they are done in faith. John 16, Matthew 10:42. So it is also written in Romans 10:10: "For with the heart one believes unto righteousness, and with the mouth confession is made unto salvation," and consequently, "whoever calls on the name of the Lord shall be saved."

To this also belongs the Cross of Christ—that is, the patient enduring of all kinds of shame, tribulation, persecution, and even death for Christ’s sake. This, Paul also calls a sacrifice; <note>original: "Opfer"</note> and through it, he comforted himself with the crown of righteousness, the joy, and the reward of eternal salvation for himself and for all who love the Lord Christ Jesus and His appearing, as stated in 2 Timothy 4:6–8, where he speaks thus: "For I am already being offered, and the time of my departure is at hand. I have fought a good fight, I have kept the faith; henceforth there is laid up for me a crown of righteousness, which the Lord, the righteous judge, shall give me at that day: and not to me only, but unto all them also that love his appearing."

And 2 Timothy 2:11, 12: "This is a faithful saying: If we be dead with him, we shall also live with him: if we suffer, we shall also reign with him," says Paul. This is not because our sufferings could merit such things by themselves. As it is also written in Romans 8:18: "For I reckon that the sufferings of this present time are not worthy to be compared with the glory which shall be revealed in us." Rather, Christ has perfectly acquired and earned the heavenly kingdom and eternal life for us with His blood. He gives and generously distributes these to us as His gifts (provided we follow Him in the obedience of faith and become partakers of Christ’s sufferings); indeed, He Himself calls this a "perfect reward in heaven," which, as we shall hear, comes out of pure grace.

Furthermore, <note>original Latin: "Item"</note> Paul also writes to the Hebrews—to all Christians who stand under the cross <note>To "stand under the cross" was a common Reformation-era idiom for living through times of religious persecution.</note>—for their eternal comfort and joy regarding the reward for such works of patience, the cross, and suffering that is in Christ. He speaks thus: "But call to remembrance the former days, in which, after ye were illuminated, ye endured a great fight of afflictions; partly, whilst ye were made a gazingstock both by reproaches and afflictions; and partly, whilst ye became companions of them that were so used. For ye had compassion of me in my bonds, and took joyfully the spoiling of your goods, knowing in yourselves that ye have in heaven a better and an enduring substance" (namely, Christ with His heavenly riches).

Here one sees how the holy Paul wonderfully comforted the Christians who are under the cross <note>In this context, "the cross" refers to the specific trials, persecutions, and hardships a believer undergoes for their faith.</note> and persecution for the sake of the Lord Christ, with the eternal reward and everlasting joy and peace that will follow all His confessors after this life. The Lord also clearly promised this in Matthew 10:32, where He speaks: "Whoever confesses me before men, him will I confess before my Father in heaven," etc. If we Christians, who are in danger every hour, hope in Christ in this life only, then we are the most miserable among all men. 1 Corinthians 15:19. But in the resurrection of the dead, our joy and kingdom in Christ shall begin and endure eternally.

May our Lord Jesus Christ work such grace-filled works in us through His Spirit, and blissfully preserve us in all patience under His cross for the full fellowship of glory until the end.

# Chapter 21.

### Concerning the Good Works of Love and the Edification of the Body of Christ.

The other good works of the Christian life are all works of love, faithfulness, benevolence, and mercy, which we practice and show for the sake of God and Christ, not only toward the brothers or fellow believers, but also toward our neighbor, toward widows and orphans, and indeed even toward our enemies.

To this also belong the works of teaching, reproof, and admonition, through which the sinner is converted and the neighbor is improved in Christ; indeed, all gifts of the Spirit given for the edification of the body of Christ (which is the congregation), with which one member serves another for the common benefit, as one person brings another in the grace of God from darkness to light, from sin to righteousness, and from error to truth. Whoever does this, says St. James, has helped a soul from death. James 5:20.

<vocab>Patience, Promise, Cross, Reward, Resurrection, Love, Mercy, Edification, Body of Christ, Righteousness</vocab>

It defines the 'Body of Christ' as the congregation where members use their spiritual gifts to help and improve one another.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 21, expanding on how gifts of the Spirit are used for the service of others and the edification of the church, following the introduction of works directed toward one's neighbor.</meta>

For we do not only serve God with what is His own, but we also serve our brothers and our neighbors with it; just as there are various gifts, there are also various services and powers within the congregation of Christ. But it is one Lord, one God, who works all in all. It is one Spirit, who distributes to each member his own part for the edification of the body, as He wills. 1 Corinthians 12:4-6; Ephesians 4:4-7.

I have also wished to mention this so that we may learn to rightly understand the dignity of these mentioned good works; because they are all gifts of the Holy Spirit, which God also deems worthy and rewards for the sake of His Spirit.

Likewise <note>German: *Item* — a Latin-derived term frequently used in theological and legal writing to mean "furthermore" or "also."</note>, we should pay close attention to works regarding the edification of the body of Christ <note>The "body of Christ" refers to the collective community of Christian believers.</note> (for the sake of which edification these gifts are graciously given, and in which these works do much and bring great benefit). Thus, we will easily be able to judge the future reward—that it will be nothing other than heavenly, everlasting joy and eternal life. Paul also writes of this elsewhere, saying: "Every man shall receive his own reward according to his own labor." 1 Corinthians 3:8 and 1 Timothy 5:18: "A laborer is worthy of his reward," where he speaks primarily of the true apostolic service and the reward for it, to which service the Lord Christ has set him apart and called him.

Concerning this, he also writes in 1 Corinthians 9:16-17, saying: "If I preach the Gospel, I have nothing to boast of, for I am compelled to do it <term>etc.</term> <note>Original: *2c.* — a common 17th-century shorthand for *et cetera*.</note> If I do it willingly, I have a reward."

And 1 Timothy 3:8-9, that the servants <note>The original context refers to deacons or ministers of the church.</note> should be blameless, holding the mystery of the faith in a pure conscience. Those who serve well acquire for themselves a good standing and much boldness in the faith which is in Christ Jesus.

Such works of service, love, and benevolence are also called a "sacrifice" by Saint Paul, as was heard above regarding the conversion of the Gentiles in Romans 15. And in Philippians 4:18, he calls the gift of love and the helping hand a sacrifice of the same kind, when those from Philippi sent him a contribution while he was in prison in Rome, regarding which he writes:

> I am full, having received from Epaphroditus <note>Original: *Epaphroditon*</note> the things which were sent from you, a sweet-smelling aroma, an acceptable sacrifice, well-pleasing to God. Thereupon Paul also considers the reward, saying: "But my God shall supply all your need according to his riches in glory in Christ Jesus."

From all this, it follows irrefutably that the kind and merciful God intends to reward all the aforementioned good works of the newly-born Christian, out of special grace—and as Paul says, with praise, honor, and the immortal nature of his glory—for Christ’s sake. This is also the set hope and strong comfort of true Christians, who in this world are accounted as sheep for the slaughter <note>original: "Schlachtschaafe," a reference to Romans 8:36, describing the suffering and persecution of the faithful</note>, offering thanks that the Lord Christ has made them worthy of His suffering and His heavenly calling, and indeed made them fit for the inheritance of the saints in light. # Chapter 22.

### Regarding the good works of the Christian struggle and the death of the old Adam.

The third kind of good works of the Christian life and faith are those directed toward ourselves. These are the works of true repentance, remorse, and sorrow for sin; the killing of the evil desires of the flesh; and the complete dying away of our old Adam <note>A theological term referring to the sinful, unregenerate nature of a human being that must be "put to death" through spiritual discipline</note> in the following of Christ toward His likeness, in order to enter the Kingdom of God.

To this also belong patience, gentleness, and humility of heart—Christian virtues that can only be rightly learned from the Lord Christ. Matt. 11:29-30.

Likewise, in the number of these works are included the works of practice and victory in the Christian struggle <note>original: "christlicher Streit," referring to the inner battle against temptation and the external battle against worldly pressure</note>, as well as the heartfelt forgiveness of those who offend, revile, or persecute us. It involves the battle ensuring that a Christian shall not seek vain honor or the praise of men, but shall break his own will, overcome himself, tame immoderation, fight manfully against sin, flee from worldly honor, leave behind evil habits, and in short, that a Christian shall hate all vice, find no pleasure in himself, and surrender himself entirely to the Lord Christ.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the third category of good works, focusing on the internal mortification of the "old Adam" and the scriptural mandates for self-denial and following Christ.</meta>

That he henceforth lives for God alone in Christ, does not fulfill the works of the flesh, and walks according to the Spirit in a new life. All these are good works and fruits of true repentance <note>original: "Buße," which in this context implies a transformative turning away from sin toward God</note>, the dying away of our selfhood, and the newness in Christ. God takes a special delight in these, so that He may dwell in our hearts through His Spirit without hindrance.

The Lord Christ also exhorted us toward such good works more than once in the Gospel, such as in Mark 8:34–35, when He called the people to Himself along with His disciples and said to them: "Whoever desires to follow Me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross daily" (that is, he should crucify his flesh along with its lusts and desires, as in Galatians 5:24) "and follow Me; for whoever desires to save his life will lose it, but whoever loses his life for My sake and the Gospel’s will save it," says the Lord.

And in Luke 14 regarding this again, when many people went with the Lord, He turned and said to them: "If anyone comes to Me and does not hate his father, mother, wife, child, brother, sister" (understand this as: when they wish to lead one away from Christ and His truth or disturb one's faith) "and also his own life, he cannot be My disciple. And whoever does not bear his cross and follow Me cannot be My disciple. For who among you, intending to build a tower, does not sit down first and count the cost, etc.? So likewise, whoever of you does not forsake all that he has cannot be My disciple. He who has ears to hear, let him hear."

Look, there we have clear testimony regarding the works of true repentance, forgiveness, and the dying away of our self—just as in Colossians 3:3–5 regarding the putting to death of our members which are on earth—and how the Lord has graciously promised that other life, namely the heavenly eternal life, to all those who lose or risk <note>original: "in die Schanz schlagen," an idiom meaning to hazard or stake something at great risk, like a soldier in the trenches</note> their life here for His sake. Who then, suffering for the sake of Christ and His truth, would allow themselves to be deprived of this comforting promise, and not hope for it with true faith and blessedness in Christ?

Such good works of repentance and the dying away of our old Adam <note>A theological term for the sinful, fallen nature of humanity that must be "put to death" through spiritual discipline</note>, so that the new Adam, Christ, may henceforth live and work in us, are called by Paul—as were the previous two kinds of works—a sacrifice, a sacrifice of our bodies, in Romans 12:1. We shall soon speak further on that passage.

To this Christian order of works also belong moderation, fasting, vigilance, prayer for sin, and the chastening of the body; indeed, everything that serves the struggle and the suppression of our rebellious...

# Chapter 23.

### Concerning the Threefold Sacrifice of Christians in the New Testament.

These then are the threefold good works of faith and the Christian life—indeed, the threefold sacrifice and service of Christians in the New Testament. All of these find their fulfillment in the single sacrifice and offering of Christ on the Cross (which alone is sufficient for our sins); they are also perfected <note>original: "consumieret," from the Latin "consummare," meaning to bring to completion or full realization</note>, finished, prepared, and worked within us through His Spirit.

It is a sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving, the sacrifice of charity <note>original: "Wohlthat," referring to acts of kindness or financial assistance to those in need</note> and gifts, and the sacrifice of our mortal body or our very selves. To these three kinds of sacrifices all Christians are bound through faith in Christ; for this reason, Christianity and all true Christians are called priests of God, as is written in 1 Peter 2:5, namely:

> "And you also" (says Saint Peter to the Christians, having just spoken of Christ as the cornerstone), "as living stones, are being built up into a spiritual house and a holy priesthood, to offer up spiritual sacrifices that are acceptable to God through Jesus Christ."

But what are such spiritual sacrifices other than that Christian believers—with all they have, are, and are capable of—surrender themselves entirely to the service of God and the Lord Christ? They do so with praise and thanks for His sacrifice, redemption, and all His benefits, even unto death for the sake of His name, and themselves

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on self-discipline by citing the examples of Paul and the prophetess Anna, then introduces the three types of Christian sacrifice: praise, charity, and self-offering. It emphasizes that all Christians act as a "holy priesthood" through their faith and devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Apostle Paul, Prophetess Anna, threefold sacrifice, priesthood of all believers, spiritual house, self-discipline, New Testament theology</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the third type of Christian sacrifice: the offering of one's own body and self to God. It transitions from the general concept of good works as sacrifice into a specific biblical exposition of Romans 12.</meta>

...through Him <note>referring to Christ</note>, offer ourselves to God the Heavenly Father; for to sacrifice means to dedicate something to God the Almighty Lord, so that through it He may be recognized, praised, and honored.

Yet, because God desires to dwell and work within human beings, and also to reveal His praise and glory through them, nothing more pleasing or more agreeable can be offered to the Lord by us than that we dedicate or surrender ourselves to Him for His service, proclaim His praise, thank God for His benevolence <note>original: "Wohlthat," referring to God's gracious acts and benefits</note>, remain obedient to His commandments, and demonstrate love and faithfulness to our neighbors—indeed, to all those in need—for God’s sake. We must slaughter and put to death our impure, animalistic desires so that we may be sanctified through the Holy Spirit, allowing the kind and merciful God to work and dwell within us.

# Chapter 24.

### Concerning the Passage Romans 12:1: "I exhort you, etc., that you present your bodies as a sacrifice, etc."

Saint Paul also speaks of this sacrifice in Romans 12:1–2, where he says: "I exhort you, dear brothers, by the mercy of God, that you present your bodies as a sacrifice that is living, holy, and well-pleasing to God, which is your rational <note>original: "vernünftiger," from the Greek "logikēn," often translated as "reasonable" or "spiritual" service</note> worship. And do not be conformed to this world, but be transformed by the renewal of your mind, so that you may test and discern what is the will of God: what is good, well-pleasing, and perfect."

From this we can clearly see what the sacrifices of Christians are, what Saint Peter meant earlier by "spiritual sacrifice," and why he calls those who believe in Christ a "priesthood." Namely, it is because they offer themselves up to God the Lord and are to stand ready at His hand for His service, just as Saint Paul also calls such an offering a "service of God" <term>Gottesdienst: literally "God-service," the standard German term for "worship" or "divine service"</term>. However, he calls it a *rational* service so that he may properly distinguish such worship and sacrifice of the New Testament from the sacrifices and worship of the Old Testament.

And the word *Rational* here does not signify the reason and cleverness of the "Old Adam" <note>A theological term referring to the fallen, sinful human nature</note>—as if "rational" meant "moderate" or "according to natural reason," implying that we should not do "too much" for Him in our worship or that we should not discipline ourselves too strictly (as some have wrongly understood it). Rather, it originates from the Word of God and speaks of the reason of the...

<summary>This page defines Christian sacrifice as the total dedication of the self to God's service, distinguishing it from Old Testament rituals. It focuses on Paul's exhortation in Romans 12 to offer the body as a "rational" or spiritual act of worship driven by the Holy Spirit rather than human logic.</summary>

<keywords>sacrifice, Romans 12, Saint Paul, rational worship, Old Adam, spiritual life, priesthood of believers, New Testament vs Old Testament, sanctification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exposition of Romans 12, focusing on the definition of "rational worship" and the internal transformation required to become a "living sacrifice" through the Holy Spirit.</meta>

new man, signifies here divine wisdom; and "rational worship" is nothing other than a spiritual worship and sacrifice, just as Peter also calls it. Similarly, the "rational milk" <note>original: "vernünftige Milch," referring to 1 Peter 2:2, often translated as the "pure milk of the word"</note> is called a spiritual milk or the milk of the Word of God, by which the new man is nurtured. This word in Greek is exactly the same in both Romans 12:1 and 1 Peter 2:2 <note>original: "logikēn," meaning rational, logical, or spiritual</note>.

Thus Saint Paul, as also Peter, exhorts us that we should offer ourselves as a sacrifice that is living, holy, and well-pleasing. This happens when we put to death our evil desires and animalistic passions through the Spirit of God, do not conform ourselves to this world, sweep out the old leaven of malice, and become in Christ a new dough—an unleavened bread <note>original: "Süßteig," literally "sweet dough," referring to the pure, unleavened bread used in the Passover</note> of sincerity and truth—indeed, serving God and our Lord Christ eternally in the newness of heart.

Whichever person is not swept and refined like silver and gold with the children of Levi <note>A reference to Malachi 3:3, regarding the purification of the priesthood</note>—that is, renewed and changed in his heart—but instead walks according to the will of the flesh and remains subject to it, cannot test the will of God, nor recognize what is good, well-pleasing, and perfect before Him. Therefore, everything must be salted with fire, and every sacrifice will be salted with salt, as the Lord Himself says in Mark 9:49.

And Psalm 51:19 <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 51:17</note>: "The sacrifices that please God are a troubled spirit; a troubled and broken heart, You, O God, will not despise."

Then our body also becomes a living sacrifice, well-pleasing to God, when—after the killing of evil lusts, sin, and desires—God the Lord lets us feel His grace, sanctification, and <term>quickening</term> <note>original: "Lebendigmachung," the spiritual act of being made alive by God</note> sweetly in our hearts. This is otherwise called a "sweet-smelling savor," and in Psalm 51:19-21, a "sacrifice of righteousness." We offer up our old man through faith, in likeness with Christ, upon the cross, so that the death and the shedding of His blood may blot out our sins. In return, He gives us His Spirit, who sanctifies, regenerates, and renews us, so that we become a well-pleasing sacrifice to God, a sacrifice of righteousness, all through Jesus Christ our Lord.

Thus Paul also called the Philippians a sacrifice along with their worship and faith—indeed, even himself, as he writes to them: "And even if I am being offered as a sacrifice upon the sacrifice and service of your faith, I rejoice and rejoice with you all; in the same way, you also should rejoice and rejoice...

...rejoice with me; that is, even if I am physically offered up and killed for this cause—having served you so that through the Gospel you have become a living, holy, and spiritual sacrifice to God in Christ—I shall nevertheless rejoice, and you should rejoice with me. For he looked toward the promised reward, namely the crown of righteousness, which was laid up in heaven through faith for him, and also for the Philippians, in Christ out of grace.

However, that "offering our bodies" (Romans 12:1) means to kill and slay evil desires and to be sanctified through the Holy Spirit is also proven by the vision of Peter in Acts 10:11–13. There he saw a vessel descending from heaven, and inside it all kinds of four-footed animals of the earth, wild beasts, creeping things, and birds of the air. The Spirit of the Lord interpreted this to him, saying: "Rise, Peter; kill and eat." By this, He intended to show him the conversion of the Gentiles and sinners—that is, the sacrifice, purification, and renewal of our hearts—which Peter was to serve through the Gospel.

Just as we all must be "slaughtered" in this way and have our fleshly desires strangled and killed by the Spirit of God if we are to be an acceptable, holy sacrifice to God—and indeed, even be food for Christ (John 4)—so also the Lord, as our Head, first offered Himself up for us to God His Father. This was done so that we, as members of His body, might also become a holy, well-pleasing sacrifice to God in Him and through His Spirit, as a "fragrance of sweetness" <note>original: "Geruch der Süßigkeit," a traditional rendering of the "sweet-smelling savor" of biblical sacrifices</note>. He calls this sacrifice holy when He says: "I sanctify myself for them, that they also may be sanctified in the truth" (John 17:19).

In this way, what stands in the Psalm is also abundantly fulfilled, where the prophet says: "Do good to Zion according to your grace; build the walls of Jerusalem" <note>Psalm 51:18</note>. (The prophet David is praying here for the coming of Christ, for His sacrifice, grace, and redemption, and for the building of the heavenly Jerusalem), which is the Christian community or Church. Following this, he says: "Then you will be pleased with the sacrifices of righteousness, with burnt offerings and whole offerings; then they will offer bulls <note>original: "Farren," meaning young bulls or steers</note> upon your altar." The altar of God is Christ; the bulls are all those who believe in Christ—newly reborn people who give up their bodies as a sacrifice that is living, holy, and well-pleasing to God.

I have wanted to explain this at greater length here regarding the word "sacrifice," because while it is so common in Christ, it is used very little with the correct understanding. And I wished to show that Saint Paul intended to signify all divine service of the three kinds of works (as has been heard) with the common name of "sacrifice," to remind us that we also should apply ourselves in grace, so that we may serve God and the Lord...

...in Christ a pure, holy, and well-pleasing sacrifice, indeed to serve God in the newness of the Spirit. We shall say more about this hereafter regarding true divine service; at which time we shall also consider the "pure sacrifice" that should be offered to God in all places (Malachi 1:11), and likewise the "daily sacrifice" mentioned in the book of Daniel.

From this we can see once again the origin of the three kinds of sacrificial works <note>The author likely refers to a tripartite division of Christian duty common in this era, such as prayer, fasting, and almsgiving, or perhaps a specific internal classification of faith-driven deeds.</note>—indeed, of all righteous good works—and how they are made pleasing to God and esteemed as worthy and good in His eyes. Namely, they are accepted solely through Jesus Christ our Lord, for whose sake, as previously stated, they are rewarded to us and crowned with eternal salvation. Thus, the actions of true Christians—indeed, everything they undertake or touch—depend more upon Christ and the Spirit of His grace than upon their own strength or themselves. This is true just as all good works must proceed through faith in the love of God and Christ, and also in love for one's neighbor in Christ.

How then can believers so presumptuously rely upon their own works and not much more upon their Lord Christ? And even if they hope for the reward and promise of eternal life in Christ through these works, they nevertheless know that it is granted out of pure grace.

# Chapter 25.

## Concerning the Promised Reward of the Three Kinds of Accomplished Works of Faith; the Twelve-fold Proof.

Since I have now spoken in general terms of all kinds of good works of faith, I wish to say more concerning the promised reward for such good works and a godly life to all pious Christians who enter into the vineyard of the Lord <note>A common biblical metaphor (from Matthew 20) for the Church or the sphere of Christian labor.</note>, for their hope and comfort. To confirm what has been said, I will also prove through the words of the Lord Christ Himself and His apostles that the aforementioned good works and sacrifices are rewarded with the crown of eternal salvation—not according to our merit or worth, or for the sake of our own strength (so that no one may boast), but according to the gracious pledge and promise of God in Christ, out of pure love, goodness, and generosity.

Firstly, it is obvious from what has preceded that such good works performed in faith are not our own works by nature; for by nature nothing is our own but sin, through...

51
<sig>G 2</sig>

...which before God makes everything in us unclean, stained, and corrupted; indeed, as Paul says: *We are not sufficient of ourselves to think of anything (good) as coming from ourselves, but our sufficiency is from God.* 2 Corinthians 3:5.

Therefore, above all things, we should note that good and meritorious—that is, works pleasing to God—are actually God’s works in us and the works of the reigning Lord Christ; and furthermore, they are called "ours" out of grace for Christ’s sake.

Good works do not belong to us according to the **old man**, but according to the **new man**, who is born from above out of God, from the flesh and bones of Christ (Ephesians 5:30). <note>The "old man" refers to the unregenerate sinful nature, while the "new man" represents the believer's identity in Christ. This reflects a central Pauline and Lutheran concept of spiritual rebirth.</note> This new man well knows to whom he should attribute the good works he performs, along with their merit and hope—whence they derive their merit and why they shall be rewarded. He does not fix his mind and hope upon the work itself, but upon the promise of Christ the Lord that accompanies these works in grace.

Secondly, the Lord Christ Himself gives testimony to righteous good works that they are "done in God," where He says: *For everyone who does evil hates the light* <term>tc.</term> <note>original: "tc." is an abbreviation for "etcetera," indicating the reader should complete the familiar verse.</note> *But he who does the truth comes to the light, that his works may be revealed, for they have been done in God.* John 3:21.

If, then, good works are done in God and from His work (He who is also the life of all truly good works), then they can in no way be perishable, nor can they remain without their dignity, merit, and reward or recompense before God, nor be performed in vain.

Thirdly, that all righteous good works are actually and primarily God's and from God—and that they should rightly be rewarded with the crown of eternal salvation—is easily understood from when the Lord Christ, in Matthew 19:16–17, answered the rich young man who came to Him and said: *Good Teacher, what good thing shall I do that I may have eternal life?* and He said to him: *Why do you call Me good? No one is good but One, that is, God.* Since then no one is good but the one God, it must certainly follow that no one is able to perform truly good works except God and those to whom He imparts His grace and goodness through Christ in the Holy Spirit. On account of this divine grace and goodness, good works are also called "works rich in grace," and as God's gifts, they are finally crowned and rewarded by God through Christ.

Fourthly, since the Lord Christ Himself has also spoken many times in the Gospel concerning the reward and recompense of good works...

Why then should we not believe the words of the Lord and his Apostles, and comfort ourselves with the hope of such a gracious promise? This should encourage us to apply ourselves all the more to good works, because we know from the kind promise and assurance of Christ that the almighty, good, and merciful God will leave no good thing unrewarded—indeed, that our labor in Christ shall not be in vain.

Regarding this, Paul also writes to the Corinthians, where he says: *Therefore, my beloved brothers, be steadfast, immovable, and always abounding in the work of the Lord, for you know that your labor is not in vain in the Lord.* 1 Corinthians 15:58. From this, one thing follows: that no one can overturn or reject the merit or the reward of the good works of faith with any rightful claim or reason. However, no true Christian will trust in these works or build his salvation upon them, even though he is certain of the reward; indeed, he can trust in them as little as if he were serving God merely for the sake of reward or being a Christian for that reason alone.

# Chapter 26.

# Chapter 27.

## On the promised eternal reward for works of love and mercy.

Sixthly, the Lord Christ also promises to reward the good works of the second order, namely the works of love, benevolence, and mercy, in the Resurrection of the Just. With this, the Lord Christ intends to exhort us all toward works of mercy. He teaches that since we are not masters, but merely servants, managers, or stewards <term>Haushalter: a steward or manager of a household; in this theological context, it refers to the Christian duty to manage God's gifts and wealth according to His will rather than for selfish gain</term> over the temporal goods which the gracious and merciful God kindly grants us for His praise, we should use these according to His will. Matthew 25:21, 23.**

He spoke the aforementioned parable <note>The Parable of the Unjust Steward, which contrasts the shrewdness of worldly people with the behavior of "the children of light."</note> with the following teaching to His disciples, using the example of the "children of this world" and their cleverness to exhort us toward spiritual wisdom. This wisdom is faithful to God the Lord in all things, uses His temporal goods and gifts according to His will, and also kindly distributes them to those in need. Upon this follows the merciful reward in the kingdom of heaven for all believers and faithful servants of Christ on the Last Day <note>The Day of Judgment.</note>.

Of this it is also written in Matthew 25: after the Lord commands the lazy, useless servant to be cast into the outermost darkness, where there will be wailing and gnashing of teeth, He then points to when the Son of Man shall come in His glory, etc. Then the King will say to those on His right: "Come, you blessed of my Father, inherit the kingdom prepared for you from the beginning of the world. I was in prison and you came to me," etc. This clearly proves how the Lord Christ, as a righteous judge, will reward works of mercy on the Last Day with the kingdom of heaven, just as He will punish the unmerciful with eternal hellfire.

# Chapter 28.

### On the Promised Eternal Reward for the Good Works of Godliness, and if we Forsake Anything for Christ's Sake.

Seventhly, we have a similar promise of reward regarding the third category of previously mentioned good works, namely the works of discipline and the dying-off of the old man <term>old man: a theological term (German: *der alte Mensch*) referring to the sinful, unredeemed nature of a human being before spiritual transformation</term>, as well as fasting, prayer, and almsgiving, as in Matthew 6:1, where the Lord Christ says: Take heed that you do not give your alms before people, to be seen by them; otherwise you have no reward from your Father in heaven, etc. But when you give alms, let not your left hand know what the right does, so that your alms may be hidden, and your Father, who sees into what is hidden, will reward you openly.

Likewise, the good works of fasting and prayer also have their promise in that same place. Similarly, when we forgive those who offend us, the Lord says: If you forgive men their failings, your heavenly Father will also forgive you, etc. And, as heard previously, whoever hates his life in this world shall preserve it for eternal life.

All these are comforting promises of God’s grace, through which a person is encouraged and exhorted to practice all good works and thus serve God.

Here also belongs what Paul writes in 1 Corinthians 9:24–25, where he says: Do you not know that all who run in the racecourse <note>original: *Schranken*; literally "barriers" or "bounds," referring to the designated path of a race</note> all run, but only one receives the prize <term>prize: the German *Kleinod* refers to a jewel or a precious treasure, used here to translate the Greek *brabeion* or "award" in a race</term>. Run now in such a way that you may seize it. But everyone who competes abstains from all things. They do it to receive a perishable crown, but we an imperishable one.

See, who would now say that Saint Paul did not hope for such an imperishable crown? He speaks of it himself, just as he comforted all Christian warriors by encouraging them to hope for it following his own example. As he also further says in the same place: I therefore run, not as if toward uncertainty. I fight not as one who beats the air. 2 Corinthians 9:26. <note>The text cites 2 Corinthians, but the verse is actually 1 Corinthians 9:26, continuing the "race" metaphor.</note>

To all this he exhorts the Philippians, Chapter 2:12–13: That we should work out our salvation with fear and trembling, for it is God, says Saint Paul, who works in you, both the

willing and doing, according to his good pleasure. From which we see once again what good works are, and although we hope for their reward according to the promise of our Lord Christ, for that reason all honor should be given and ascribed therein not to our works, but to GOD the Lord.

Eighthly, we should also not forget the comforting promise when Peter spoke to the Lord: "See, we have left everything and followed you; what will we have for it?" But Jesus answered and said: "Truly I say to you, there is no one who leaves house or brothers, or sisters, or father, or mother, or wife, or children, or fields for my sake or for the sake of the Gospel, who does not receive a hundredfold now in this time—houses and brothers, and sisters, and mothers, and children, and fields, with persecution—and in the world to come, eternal life." Mark 10:29, 30; Matthew 19:28, 29.

There, the Lord Christ clearly promised all believers who leave something for his sake that he will repay such things not only here temporally with more than they have left behind, but also there in the future with eternal life. Which indeed is a magnificent comfort in our times to those who stand under the cross of Christ <note>standing under the cross: a metaphor for enduring suffering or persecution for one's faith</note>, provided they believe the words of the Lord Christ.

How else could one have brought the Heathen <term>Heathen: historical term (German: *Heiden*) for non-Christians, specifically referring to the pagans of the Roman Empire</term> to the Christian faith through such heavy labor, misery, and tribulation even unto death, if one had not told them of such comforting promises of eternal life and of something better in Christ. As Tertullian <note>Tertullian (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) was a prolific early Christian author from Carthage</note> also writes: We have committed ourselves to the struggle and the Christian life, so that we hope to attain what GOD has promised in return; so that we also do not have to suffer what he threatens the ungodly with.

Ninthly, the Gospel parable of the workers <note>The transcription reads *Arabern* (Arabs), which is likely a typographical error in the original or OCR for *Arbeitern* (workers)</note> in the vineyard might also belong here, where the householder has them called and gives each his penny <term>penny: the original *Groschen* refers to the denarius, a day's wage in the biblical parable</term> or wage, which however may be understood as the people of the Law, namely the Jews, and the people of Grace, namely the true Christians. For as the work is, so follows the reward; that is, how and with what purpose, intention, or heart the good works are done by people, so they are rewarded, of which was also spoken previously.

Therefore, "he who comes to God must believe that He is, and that He is a rewarder of those who seek Him," says Paul in Hebrews 11:6. Since He is a rewarder, why should believers not hope for it, seeking eternal life through good works alongside Paul?

# Chapter 29.

# Chapter 30.

### How the words Reward and Merit should be correctly judged and understood regarding good works.

From all this, it is now easy to observe that the words **reward**, **recompense**, or even **merit**, as some call it—when the Lord Christ and the Holy Scriptures speak of the rewarding of good works—should not be understood according to the nature of human reward or merit. In human terms, one serves for a specific wage and looks only at the bare work performed. We should not think that the Lord Christ, with the little word "reward," promised salvation to bare works alone, apart from faith and other spiritual connections. It is not like a day laborer who splits wood, or a lazy, wicked servant <note>original: "Schalksknecht" — a biblical term for a deceitful or unmotivated servant</note> who works without joy or willingness, yet is still given his earned wage because he focused his labor on it and hoped for payment solely because of his work.

The words reward and merit must not be understood here in that way, for such a thing would be entirely contrary and opposed to the grace and mercy of God. Indeed, grace would no longer be grace if we could earn salvation and eternal life through our labor or deeds in such a manner, or if we could become pious and righteous before God through works. If that were the case, the grace-filled promises in Christ and the merit of His suffering would lose their meaning. Paul powerfully explained this in Romans 4 and 11 to the praise of Christ and His grace, where he rebukes the Jews and all those for their error who wish to be made righteous before God through the works of the law, and who—through their own human righteousness—seek to abolish or suppress faith and the promised grace in Christ. Titus 3: "Not because of works of righteousness which we have done, but according to His mercy He saves us."

Instead, the word should be understood such that the reward in heaven, or the crown and joy of eternal salvation, is promised to those who have accepted Christ and have already been made righteous through faith in Christ. Indeed, as soon as a person accepts Christ with true faith and is converted, then...

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 30, which argues that "reward" in a Christian context is a gift of grace for the faithful, rather than a transactional wage earned through works alone.</summary>

This is how it is with the children of God and their reward. For although they know that the inheritance of the Kingdom of Heaven is given to them out of pure grace for the sake of Christ, they also know that they could never earn such a great and unspeakable good with all their works. But the true knowledge of Christ decides it all. # Chapter 31.

### Regarding the understanding of the saying in Luke 17: "When you have done everything, then say, we are unprofitable servants."

That the saying in Luke 17:10, where the Lord says to His disciples: "When you have done everything that was commanded of you, then say, we are unprofitable servants," etc., is not at all (as some imagine) directed against the reward of good works, but solely against confidence and self-satisfaction—against spiritual **pride** <note>original: "Hoffart" — an archaic term for high-handed pride or haughtiness.</note>, puffing up, boasting, and the presumption of the **fleshly mind** <note>original: "fleischlichen Sinnes" — referring to human nature or intellect when it is not guided by the Holy Spirit.</note>—is clearly proven by the words of the Lord. For He does not say, "When you have done all that you were commanded, then you *are* unprofitable servants"; rather, He says: "Say: we are unprofitable servants, for we have done what we were duty-bound to do."

It is as if the Lord wanted to say to them: Although much reward is promised to good works and the upright service of God, and I have also spoken before of great reward in heaven, yet you should not exalt yourselves over this, nor regard it as if such promised reward came from your own doing and because of your own worthiness; or as if your works were sufficient to merit the kingdom of heaven. Much less should you want to defy God with your works, as if He were indebted to give you something for them. Rather, you should know that you are duty-bound by right to serve God, as your Lord and Creator, in gratitude and praise for His benevolence, and can never do enough for Him—even in that which you do by nature and through grace—but would be duty-bound to do a thousand times more.

Thus the Lord Christ intended to make known the richness of His great grace to His disciples and to all of us, and to keep us in humility and lowliness during divine service and all good works—indeed, in all divine gifts. For this reason, He also said to the seventy disciples: "Do not rejoice in this, that the spirits are subject to you; but rejoice that your names are written in heaven" (Luke 10:20); that is, that you are called and chosen for the kingdom of God by grace.

With the aforementioned saying, the Lord Christ intends to dampen Pharisaic boasting, presumption, and the pride and self-assurance of our fleshly mind, to strip us of our **ostrich feathers** <note>original: "Strausfedern" — a German idiom referring to vanity or showing off, much like the English "fine feathers."</note>, and to give clearly to understand how a true Christian [should conduct himself] before

<summary>This page explains Luke 17:10, arguing that being "unprofitable servants" does not mean good works are worthless, but rather warns against human pride and the false belief that one can "earn" salvation or put God in one's debt.</summary>
<keywords>Luke 17, unprofitable servants, reward, good works, pride, humility, grace, Pharisaic boasting, merit</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous chapter regarding the nature of Christian service and the avoidance of spiritual pride. It transitions from a warning against self-satisfaction to a reconciliation of two seemingly contradictory biblical concepts: our status as "unprofitable servants" and the promise of "great reward."</meta>

One should acknowledge God in all his good works, gifts, and Christian life, and thus humble oneself so that he knows that our **righteousness** and **salvation** <note>original: "Seligwerdung" — literally "the process of becoming blessed," referring here to the attainment of salvation.</note> have not been established upon works or upon ourselves, nor upon any created external thing; just as God will not find satisfaction in our virtuous works—let alone ceremonies—any more than eternal life is promised or given to us for the sake of our own worthiness.

In short, although a rich reward has been promised for faith and works out of grace, the will of God regarding the salvation of mankind is placed solely upon **Christ** and upon his **merit**, where he says: *See, I come to do Your will, O God;* in which will we are sanctified, through the offering of the body of Jesus Christ once for all. Hebrews 10:7 and 10.

Christ is the only-begotten, beloved Son, who has earned and acquired everything for us; he also enables us to perform good works, in which alone God the Father has pleasure. Through him, we are also accepted as servants, made fit for the service of God in His grace, and become perfect and righteous.

# Chapter 32.

### That the Lord Christ does not consider His faithful servants and confessors of the truth as unprofitable servants.

Therefore, these two sentences or sayings—where Christ says here: *When you have done everything, etc., say: we are unprofitable servants;* and where He previously spoke in Luke 6:23, as well as Matthew 5:12 and 6:6, 18, regarding the reward for the **works of the cross** <note>original: "Werke des Kreuzes" — referring to the hardships, self-denial, or "crosses" that a believer bears in service to God.</note>: *Rejoice then and be glad, for behold your reward is great in heaven*—may be well reconciled with one another. For even if true believers, in all their works, gifts, and Christian life, consider themselves **unprofitable servants** <note>original: "unnütze Knechte" — a reference to Luke 17:10, meaning servants who have only done their duty and have no extra claim to merit.</note> and acknowledge before God that they do far, far too little—indeed, as those who are not sufficient in themselves to even think anything good—the Lord nevertheless does not consider them unprofitable servants when they serve Him according to His will. Rather, He considers them **pious, faithful servants** and laborers (Matthew 25:21, 23), and even His **friends**. *You are my friends, if you do what I command you,* says the Lord. This shall be a strong and heartfelt comfort to all who strive for obedience or the will of Christ and confess His truth before the world.

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous section regarding the "unprofitable servant." It transitions from the idea that human effort is never enough to satisfy God's law on its own, to a definition of "merit" and "reward" as gifts of grace rather than wages earned through labor.</meta>

...and were duty-bound to do much more; we give praise, honor, and glory to Your mercy, compassion, gifts, and grace. As it is written so comfortingly in the Apocalypse <note>original: "Apoc." — the Book of Revelation</note> regarding this.

Therefore, "merit" or "reward" in this context does not refer to the servile manner of a hireling <term>hireling (Miethling): one who works strictly for pay without personal devotion</term>, who serves only for wages and directs his service, works, or labor toward that end, or deems himself worthy of it as if he had earned the reward by his own right. Rather, the reward and merit of which the Scripture speaks regarding good works refers to God’s gracious benevolence, the gift and the increase of His bounties. It is called the crown of righteousness and the gift of eternal life, which God has promised to give generously to all His servants, Christian combatants, and spiritual knights, for the sake of Christ and His merit. As it is common to say in a parable: "This man has deserved well of his master," when he is pleasing and highly commended to him, and thus has a gracious king or lord who gives him more than he is obligated to give and more than the servant could ever earn.

In this way, we "earn" favor with God the heavenly Father when we believe in Him and His Son Jesus Christ, love one another, and keep God’s commandments (1 John 3:23). And whoever keeps His commandments has great reward, says the prophet David in Psalm 19:11 <note>The German text cites Psalm 19:12, following a different verse numbering common in some older translations.</note>. If one keeps them according to the letter—after the fashion of the legalistic people, without heart, spirit, or grace—then he receives a temporal <note>temporary or worldly</note> reward for it, as previously mentioned. But if he keeps them also in the spirit and obedience of the heart through faith in Christ, then because of the spirit of faith—indeed, because of the workings of Christ—he will bring forth righteousness, sanctification, and finally the reward of the joy of eternal life. Of this, Paul says in Romans 2:10: "Glory, honor, and peace to everyone who does good." From this, the following passage is easily understood: that it is not those who hear the law who are righteous before God, but those who *do* the law; and what it means to be a "doer of the law" in that context. Doers of the law are those who fulfill or accomplish it from the heart according to the will of God—which can in no way happen without the Spirit of Christ.

But St. Paul further indicates that such a promised reward is nothing other than a gracious present and gift from God. He exhorts Christians to surrender their members to the service of righteousness, saying: "But now that you have been set free from sin and have become slaves of God <note>original: "GOttes Knechte"</note> (namely, through faith in Jesus Christ), you have your fruit in sanctification, and its end is eternal life. For the wages of sin is death, but the gift (of the merciful God) is eternal life in Christ Jesus our Lord."

<summary>This page redefines religious "merit" not as earned wages, but as a gracious gift from God given to those who act out of faith and love. It distinguishes between following the "letter of the law" for worldly rewards and following the "spirit of the law" through Christ for eternal life.</summary>
<keywords>merit, reward, grace, faith, law vs spirit, sanctification, eternal life, St. Paul, King David</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of "merit" and "reward." It bridges the gap between the previous page's warning against legalistic "hireling" service and the upcoming explanation of how a believer should view their own good deeds: as a response to grace rather than a way to earn salvation.</meta>

See, such a gracious gift and endowment of eternal life is the reward of all the chosen, pious hearts who fear God and constantly serve Him as their Lord, Creator, and Savior out of love and faithfulness. Indeed, God Himself is their reward, in whom they hope; as He also says to Abraham: "Do not fear, Abraham; I am your shield and your exceedingly great reward" <note>Genesis 15:1</note>. God, I say, is the exceedingly abundant and rich reward for Abraham and all his people—namely, the believers—here in hidden truth and there, in the future, in eternal heavenly clarity. As it is also written in the Book of Wisdom: "The righteous shall live forever, and the Lord is their reward, and the Most High cares for them; therefore they shall receive a heavenly kingdom and a beautiful crown from the hand of the Lord" <note>Wisdom of Solomon 5:15–16</note>.

# The 33rd Chapter.

### A summary of the reward of the righteous, and how and why Christian believers should perform their good works.

Thus we have now heard concerning the reward of works and the merit of the saints of God, and that one should not understand the words "merit," "reward," or "recompense" in this context so literally in a human fashion—as if it were an obligatory debt based on our fleshly labor, honor, or glory. This is so that the benefits of Christ, His merit, and His satisfaction <term>satisfaction (Genugthuung): the theological concept that Christ’s death fully paid the debt of human sin to God</term> are not nullified; and so that nothing is taken away from the grace of God, nor the merit of the kingdom of heaven ascribed to our own works or actions.

On the other hand, these mentioned words should not be so avoided, suppressed, or explained away <note>original: "gloßirt," meaning to interpret or gloss over a text</note> that we consequently abandon our hope and faith in Christ's promise regarding good works and all true service to God. Nor should we cast aside or think lightly of the divine comfort amidst the tribulations that are in Christ, as if what we do or suffer for the sake of God and Christ were useless and in vain.

Not at all; rather, we should maintain the right middle ground between the two, seeking everything in and through the Lord Christ. We should believe not only the first promise of redemption and Christ's satisfaction, but also firmly believe the second promise—that is, the reward for the good works of Christians and for all sufferings endured for the sake of the truth—with the hope of a better life. We should also, in all needs, tribulations, an<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "reward" of the faithful as God Himself and clarifies that while works do not earn salvation (which is Christ's work), they are still promised a gracious reward by God. It warns against both legalism and the dismissal of the value of good deeds.</summary>
<keywords>eternal life, reward of the righteous, Abraham, Book of Wisdom, merit, satisfaction of Christ, good works, promise</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 66 regarding the nature of "merit" and "reward." It concludes a sentence regarding how believers should view the trials they face in light of God's grace, emphasizing that good works are a fruit of faith rather than a means to earn salvation.</meta>

[and] adversities of faith find total comfort in such a grace-filled reward; yet all this in Christ Jesus our Lord and solely for His sake, who makes us worthy of all good things without merit, out of pure grace.

As also St. Paul, when he exhorts the Corinthians to become rich in the work of Christ, and comforts them that their labor is not in vain, immediately adds: *in the Lord*. "In the Lord," he says, your labor shall not be in vain—that is, in Christ Jesus, who died and rose for us, for whom we should henceforth live and not for ourselves. 2 Cor. 5:15.

Therefore, true believers do not perform good works for their own personal gain in a fleshly manner—seeking to collect many crowns and rewards from God, or seeking to obtain forgiveness of sins apart from Christ—much less do they wish to be honored and praised by men for their works. Rather, they do it to serve God and Christ their Lord, thereby proving their faith and that they are children of God, heirs and citizens of the kingdom of heaven. They do all their works freely out of love, out of bounden duty and gratitude; indeed, they do them in Christ the Lord, who gives them the doing, the willing, and the accomplishing <note>A reference to Philippians 2:13</note>, into whom they are incorporated through faith, in whom alone they have salvation and the forgiveness of sins.

In short, the elect and Christian believers perform their good works so that God the Almighty, the giver of all good things, and our Lord Jesus Christ may be lauded and praised, and that the neighbor may also be improved thereby. Regarding this, the Lord, after the promise of the great reward in heaven, says to His disciples: "So let your light shine before men, that they may see your good works and praise your Father in heaven" <note>Matthew 5:16</note>. Not, says the Lord, so that one praises *you*, or that you yourselves are praised thereby, but so that God, the "Father of lights, from whom comes every good and every perfect gift from above" (James 1:17 <note>original: "Jacob. 1, 17"</note>), may be praised and loved through it.

# The 34th Chapter.

### On the True and Upright Worship of the New Testament.

From what has been said regarding good works and the promise of their reward—which is also why such works are called "good"—it may now be easily understood what true, genuine worship <term>Gottesdienst: literally "God-service," referring both to religious liturgy and the internal devotion of a believer's life</term> is. One can see what it primarily consists of, where it originates, and what a true servant of God is. Likewise, one can see how and in what manner one serves God the Lord rightly and pleasingly. I am not speaking here of the Old Testament worship, which was figurative <note>original: "figürlichen," referring to the symbolic rituals and animal sacrifices of the Mosaic Law</note> and Jewish, but of the true Christian worship—the worship of the New Testament—which the Lord Christ establishes through the Spirit of Grace within His community and in all believing hearts.

This point or article of faith is one of the most important, which all people who desire to be saved should understand. This is because nearly everyone, despite a lack of true repentance, still presumes to serve God in some way and insists on having a form of worship, whatever it may be. Hypocrites <term>Gleißner: a traditional term for those whose piety is only for outward show</term>, and even the heathens and Turks <note>In the 17th century, "Turks" was frequently used as a general term for Muslims</note>, as much as true believers and Christians, care a great deal about their worship; therefore, one should rightfully be all the more concerned with what the true, right worship actually is.

Furthermore, in our own times, many boast that they are servants of God, servants of Christ, and even servants of the Holy Spirit and the New Testament. However, few of them wish to truly seek after the right worship and everything that belongs to it. Instead, just as everyone would like to have a God and a Christ to their own liking, they also fashion a form of worship for themselves to suit their own pleasure. Thus, many often mistake their own errors for God and worship, just as some immediately assume that anything possessing a grand appearance or outward show before men must be worship. Yet the Almighty Lord God has caused it to be described in many ways how and in what form one should serve Him rightly, and how one should rightly recognize Him. For just as God is true and certain in all His words, works, and promises, so too must everything be true and certain by which one wishes to serve Him, by which one recognizes Him, and which shall count as valid before Him in eternity.

<summary>This chapter introduces the concept of true Christian worship, distinguishing it from the symbolic rituals of the Old Testament and the self-made traditions of hypocrites or other religions. The author emphasizes that true service to God must be established by Christ in the heart and grounded in the certainty of God's Word.</summary>

<keywords>true worship, New Testament, divine service, hypocrisy, repentance, Spirit of Grace, religious error, Christian living</keywords>

This page continues that theme into Chapter 35, explaining that true worship is actually God working through the believer.</meta>

# The 35th Chapter.

### What Belongs to True Worship, and Wherein It Chiefly Consists.

Briefly, however, it is important to know that all true worship—as well as the true knowledge of God and of the Lord Christ—flows out and proceeds from God Himself. This is so that we can only serve God rightly using that which is His own; indeed, that God the Almighty Lord (so to speak) serves Himself from His own property within us. Yet, He nonetheless gifts us His own service or work and graciously rewards that which is His as if it were our very own, all of which He does by grace through Jesus Christ our Lord.

For the sake of such true worship, so that we might serve Him rightly, the Almighty God promised through the Prophet <note>referring to the prophecy of the New Covenant</note> that He would put His law in our hearts and write it in our minds, and we shall be His people, and He would be our God. <margin>Jeremiah 31:33</margin>

Likewise, He promised that He would make an everlasting covenant with us and put His fear into our hearts. Yes, He would build up Israel and cleanse them from all iniquity and sins; that He would also pour out the Spirit of grace and of prayer, take the stony heart out of our body, and give us a heart of flesh, a new heart, and a new spirit. All of this belongs to true worship, as it has been abundantly fulfilled through Christ in the establishment of the new everlasting testament <term>Testament: in this context, it refers to the "New Covenant" or the new relationship between God and humanity</term> and true worship, and is still fulfilled through Him today in all true believers to the praise of God.

For what is the law that God promises to give into our hearts other than His life-giving, living Word that became flesh, namely Jesus Christ? It is the law of which it is written in <margin>Psalm 19:8 and 9</margin>, which refreshes the soul, the sure testimony that makes the simple <term>Albern: in 17th-century German, this refers to the "simple-hearted" or "uncomplicated," rather than the modern sense of "silly"</term> wise, that gladdens the heart and enlightens the eyes, etc. Now, where such a law is written into the heart—yes, where a new heart and a new spirit, the Spirit of grace and of prayer is given—there follows without doubt the right, true service of Almighty God. We then perform and manifest this service to Him pleasingly out of His own property and riches; that is, in and with those who are His. How could such a service, which God Himself performs and works within us, remain without a gift or a reward?

<vocab>Worship/Service, Knowledge of God, Jesus Christ, Grace, Prophet, Jeremiah, Covenant, Israel, Testament, Law, Psalm</vocab>

<summary>This chapter explains that true worship is not a human effort but a divine work where God "serves Himself" through the believer by gifting them a new heart and the Holy Spirit. It identifies Jesus Christ as the "living Law" written on the heart, fulfilling the Old Testament prophecies of a New Covenant.</summary>
<keywords>internal worship, New Covenant, grace, indwelling of Christ, spiritual transformation, Jeremiah 31:33, Psalm 19, divine reward</keywords>
