...cannot well happen [purely] by the [human] spirit, it is a comforting hope to be drawn from this that the Lord will yet demonstrate himself with more gifts, light, spirit, and power for the building of his church, and that the kingdom of his grace will gloriously arise to destroy the kingdom of the <term>Antichrist<note>In early modern Protestant thought, the "Antichrist" was often identified with the corrupt institutional structures of the Roman Church that were seen as opposing the true Gospel.</note></term> before the Last Day. However, as previously mentioned, no one should wait for this with their repentance. Instead, because it is "today," we should obey the Lord’s voice, turn to him, cease from sin, and enter with Christ into his eternal rest. Amen.

I have wished to recall these points regarding the <term>Reformation<note>original: "Reformirung." The author argues that a true "re-forming" of the church requires an internal spiritual change, not just a change in external laws or leaders.</note></term> of the church here for the further consideration of all sensible Christians. One must fundamentally look to Christ as the head of the church and to the knowledge of him through sound, pure doctrine and a penitent life. Each person must begin this work within themselves, faithfully asking God for his gracious help and visitation. For just as the foundation of the Christian church—the new heavenly Jerusalem—is above in heaven (whereas the foundation, strength, and power of the Antichristian kingdom is here on earth and ultimately in hell), so all right building and reforming of the church must come from the grace of God in heaven, just as the creation of its members must come from above through <term>rebirth<note>original: "Wiedergeburt." A central concept in spiritual renewal movements, referring to a radical internal transformation by the Holy Spirit.</note></term> in the Holy Spirit. A man can receive nothing, says <note>original: "Johannis 3, 27"</note> John the Baptist, unless it has been given to him from heaven. And in <note>original: "Jacobi 1, 16-17"</note> James 1:16–17: Do not err, dear brothers; every good gift and every perfect gift comes down from above, from the Father of Lights.

In summary, Christ himself must reform the church in his Spirit; he must build and gather it himself, unite and regenerate hearts, and restore the true <term>worship of God<note>original: "Gottesdienst." While often translated as "church service," here it refers to the broader concept of serving and honoring God "in spirit and in truth."</note></term> that exists in spirit and truth (for which we should also pray daily). Otherwise, without Christ and his Spirit, the church may well remain unreformed, unmultiplied—even ungathered and unbuilt—before God for a long time, no matter what external changes humans may attempt.

This is not to say that we wish to despise or reject the <term>Councils<note>original: "Concilia." Formal assemblies of church leaders or scholars meant to decide on matters of faith and doctrine.</note></term> or the assemblies of pious, learned, and godly men, nor their diligence and efforts in the reformation of the church. Rather, we wish to look at the heart of the matter and remember what is primarily required if the church is to be rightly reformed and a Christian life is to be established.

And since the pure truth of Christ is now so mingled and burdened with many human inventions and added doctrines and articles, it is impossible for many to...

...to learn and to grasp; it is also often difficult and vexing to the conscience when things that have come through <term>human regulations<note>original: "Menschen-Satzung." This refers to laws and traditions created by church authorities rather than those found directly in the Bible.</note></term> are added to the simplicity of faith. Therefore, in the building or <term>reforming of the Church<note>original: "Reformirung der Kirch." The author uses this term to describe a return to original Christian purity.</note></term>, such things should nonetheless be distinguished for the better, considered alongside many other points, and the conscience—which should be founded solely upon Christ and His word—ought to be fairly helped in this.

Just as it was sufficient in the first <term>Apostolic Church<note>The early church established by the original apostles of Jesus, which many reformers viewed as the ideal model of faith before it was corrupted by later institutional power.</note></term> when a Christian confessed the twelve articles of faith <note>This refers to the Apostles' Creed, the traditional summary of Christian belief.</note>, properly understood them, and lived a Christian and godly life accordingly. Beyond this, the dear apostles and the shepherds and ministers appointed by the Holy Spirit demanded and desired nothing more. Now, however, people are by no means willing to let it rest there. It is as if twelve more articles have been added as being necessary for salvation. Indeed, so many have been added that the original twelve, in their living understanding and knowledge of faith, are now almost entirely obscured. Consequently, people tend to enforce and urge such human additions (which only create erroneous and <term>seared consciences<note>original: "brandmålige Gewissen." A reference to 1 Timothy 4:2, describing a conscience that has become insensitive or "branded" by false teachings.</note></term>) with sword, fire, and water, more than they focus on the true foundation in which our salvation stands. This truly belongs to the spirit of the Antichrist, rather than the spirit of the gentle, kind Lord Christ. But You, Lord Jesus, have mercy on this.

# The 45th Chapter.

### What should be understood by the name "Antichrist" in the Holy Scriptures.

Since the Antichrist has been mentioned here, and a brief indication has been given as to how his kingdom and the destruction of the Church of Christ—which is to occur through him—should be recognized: One should know that such a name does not refer to a single person, but to a whole body, a whole kingdom of deception, error, and <term>apostasy from Christ<note>original: "Abfall von Christo." A falling away or rejection of the true faith.</note></term>, or to discord and idolatry within Christianity. Thus, the Holy Scripture counts all those as part of the kingdom of the Antichrist who, under the name of Christ and the Christian Church, act against the reigning King Christ and against His simple divine truth in words or deeds—especially those who teach such things and, moreover, fight for them and want them to be held as right.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the corruption of the Church, specifically focusing on how the "Antichrist" operates within the Christian institution by mimicking its forms while undermining its spiritual essence through human regulations.</meta>

-> ❧ ❧ ❧ ❧ ❧ <-

The Antichrist is such a seductive, harmful, and blasphemous thing that the Holy Spirit has fought against him from the beginning through all the prophets, and Christ and His apostles faithfully warned of him, clearly depicting and indicating his wickedness. His kingdom and office are such a disguised, hidden, and <term>shining deceit<note>original: "gleissende Betriegerey." This suggests a deceptive outward appearance of holiness or "glistering" beauty that hides inward corruption.</note></term> that Christ Himself says in <term>Matthew 24<note>Specifically referring to Matthew 24:24: "For false messiahs and false prophets will appear and perform great signs and wonders to deceive, if possible, even the elect."</note></term>: "If it were possible, even the elect would be deceived, etc." He conducts such an impressive-looking existence that one is less concerned that he is the Antichrist, until everything prophesied about him has been fulfilled.

All those are understood under the name of the Antichrist who, within Christianity, boast of the name of Christ or of Christian offices, yet deal in erroneous, seductive teaching, as well as an evil, offensive life or false <term>worship<note>original: "Gottesdienst," literally "God-service." This includes rituals, prayer, and the formal practice of religion.</note></term>. Indeed, all false prophets who prophesy lies for the truth concerning Christ; all unrighteous teachers who introduce damnable error and idolatry under the name of God’s Word; all self-appointed apostles or shepherds who do not gather with Christ but only scatter; who pass themselves off as servants of God and leaders of the Christian church, and yet destroy the true worship that stands in spirit and in truth; who persecute the members of Christ’s church, scatter His sheep, and make the believers fall away from Christ Jesus by placing their trust in external things, even seeking Christ in or through such things. Yes, all those who point the core of our <term>justification<note>original: "Gerechtwerdung." This is the theological concept of being declared righteous or "right with God" through faith.</note></term> and <term>salvation<note>original: "Seligkeit." This refers to eternal happiness or the state of being saved.</note></term> anywhere else than to the reigning King Christ above in heaven, and who do not enter the sheepfold through the right door—whom the Lord calls thieves and murderers.

Scripture intends for all such to be understood under the name and kingdom of the Antichrist, just as there were already many antichrists in the time of the apostles, about which John writes in his epistle: "Children, it is the last hour; and as you have heard that the Antichrist is coming, even now many antichrists have come, by which we know that it is the last hour. They went out from **Us**, but they were not of us; for if they had been of **Us**, they would have continued with **Us**, etc." One should well consider who these "**Us**" were: namely, the sent apostles of the Lord Christ, and pious, reborn, holy Christians. With the words "They went out from us," John intended to indicate the <term>apostasy<note>original: "Abfall." A formal turning away from or renunciation of a religious or political belief.</note></term> from the pure apostolic teaching of the truth—indeed, from Christ to Satan, and from a Christian life into a damned, sinful life. All those who have so departed, he calls antichrists, and states that they belong to the kingdom of the Antichrist.

And John writes more in that same place concerning the Antichrist, where he says: That he is a liar who denies that Jesus is the Christ. Following this, he further says: This is the Antichrist, who denies the Father and the Son. Whoever denies the Son does not have the Father either. (1 John 2:22, 23). Here again, it should be well considered what it means for Jesus to be the Son of God, and that such faith, confession, and knowledge cannot be a <term>simple, ordinary thing<note>original: "schlecht gemein Ding." In this historical context, "schlecht" often meant "plain" or "simple" rather than "bad." And that no one overcomes the world except the one who believes that Jesus is the Son of God. Because it also says earlier in the 4th chapter: Whoever shall confess that Jesus is the Son of God, God remains in him and he in God.

There we see what a great and glorious thing true knowledge and confession of Jesus Christ is. But whoever denies this—that is, whoever does not hold the man Jesus Christ to be the true, natural Son of God, nor the promised and anointed King of Heaven, indeed, not even his Lord and God, and yet wishes to be a Christian—he is an Antichrist, says John, who denies the Father and the Son. We should (I say once more) take all of this rightly and earnestly to heart in our own times, so that in the fear of God we may take care that we are not found among the Antichrists, but are finally found by God as rightly believing and true Christians in all things.

Regarding the Antichrist and his power, we have also heard previously that he is <term>prefigured<note>original: "figuriret." This refers to the theological concept of "typology," where Old Testament figures or events are seen as symbolic "shadows" of New Testament realities.</note></term> by the little horn in the Prophet Daniel, and how this was at first of little significance, but afterwards became a great and powerful kingdom. Of which Paul also writes in 2 Thessalonians 2:7, that such a mystery of wickedness was already stirring secretly then, although it was not yet manifest, nor was the Antichrist yet <term>in power<note>original: "im Regiment," referring to the exercise of government or rule.</note></term>. The Holy Spirit with His power then held it back through the service of the true apostles and the shepherds and prophets appointed by God, who suppressed such things with the healthy, pure teaching of the Gospel of Christ, and fought earnestly with the sword of the Spirit against Satan and his secret designs and malice, so that he could not bring his powerful errors and deceptions into power during their times.

Thus, as the Antichrist was already on his way during the time of the Apostles, and had already gradually begun even then to scatter the little sheep of Christ and to destroy the Christian church or community of God, how much more should we now [guard ourselves] against him and his kingdom (whose king and head [is] Satan...

This refers to a specific reading of 1 John 4:3 found in some Latin traditions ("solvit Jesum"), interpreted here as "deconstructing" or "breaking apart" the unity of Christ’s divine and human natures.</note></term> (that is, who breaks apart his personal wholeness, separates one nature from the other, or denies either his divinity or his humanity) is not of God. What Saint Paul also writes to the Thessalonians in Chapter 2, Verses 3 and 4, regarding the Antichrist, is easily found there. He goes so far as to sit in the temple of God as God, and presents himself as if he were God, etc. Whom the Lord Jesus will slay with the breath of his mouth, and destroy—or finish off—with the brightness of his coming.

God’s temple is the Christian Church, as well as a Christian heart and conscience (1 Corinthians 6:19).

The prophet Isaiah calls the Antichrist—under the figure of Nebuchadnezzar—a despiser of Christ and a destroyer of God’s people. The prophet Nahum calls him the "scatterer" who marches out against Christ and besieges his strongholds, which is to say, the Christian Church.

Zechariah calls him the "foolish, useless shepherd" who will not look after those who are forsaken, nor seek the scattered, nor heal the sick, nor nourish the healthy. Instead, he will eat the flesh of the fat ones and <term>tear off their claws<note>original: "ihre Klauen auflosen." This vivid biblical imagery from Zechariah 11:16 suggests a leader who is not just neglectful but actively predatory, consuming the very resources of his flock.</note></term>, says Zechariah in Chapter 11, Verse 16.

This is so that one might learn to recognize such a destroyer and his kingdom well, and know how to guard oneself against them; and so that one might also depart from the great city of Babylon, according to the Lord’s warning, and not become a partaker in her sins, Revelation 18 <note>original: "Apoc. 18." # The 46th Chapter.

### On the Benefit, Fruit, and Riches of the Knowledge of Christ, through which not only Eternal Life, but also all kinds of deviations, deception, and error are seen and recognized.

Now we return again to our initial purpose, regarding <term>divine service<note>original: "Gottesdienst." While often translated as "church service," here it refers more broadly to the internal and external ways a believer serves and honors God.</note></term> and good works, with which we then wish to conclude. Since, however, all of this—as well as Babylon and the Antichristian kingdom—can only be rightly understood and perceived through Jesus Christ in his heavenly light, we should take the greatest care that we first learn to thoroughly know the Lord Christ, which is both the greatest art on earth and the central theme of the entire Holy Scripture. We should speak often of Christ, gladly hear or read of him, diligently inquire after Christ, delight ourselves in him, love him from the heart, and daily take note of his benevolence and his <term>intercession<note>original: "Einsprechens." This refers to Christ speaking on behalf of the believer or the internal "speaking" of the Word in the soul.</note></term> <term>etc.<note>original: "2c." An archaic shorthand for "et cetera."</note></term> Because the knowledge of Christ serves not only so that we may rightly recognize the mysteries of God, our redemption, righteousness, and salvation, but also that we might judge, see, and rightly understand all deviations, all improper worship, all false teachings, indeed all sorts of error, sin, and deception, and where they fall short—just as all the treasures of divine wisdom and knowledge lie hidden in Christ, and as Christ and the knowledge of him are the light, wisdom, and truth of God himself.

In such light of the knowledge of Christ, the Lord commands all Christians to walk, John 12:35–36, as well as to believe in it, so that we may be children of light; for as long as we walk therein, darkness cannot overtake us. It goes before us and shines during the day, so that we perceive the deviations that lead from eternal life to death, through which we are preserved from error, strengthened in our right course, exhorted to it, and advanced in it, so that we follow the light, look upon it, hold it up against the darkness, and always strengthen our weakness by it, lighting and illuminating our darkness from it, and not moving any further from it than it shines for us or enlightens us; and if we pervert such light, or if it wishes to hide from us, then we should devout-

...continually pray, sigh, and plead, so that it <note>Referring to the "heavenly light" mentioned on the previous page.</note> might graciously appear to us again, so that we may return to the right path that leads from death to life, from hell to heaven.

Such is the fruit, benefit, and wealth of the knowledge of Christ and His light, in which we can also see wherein the <term>divine service<note>original: "Gottesdienst." While often meaning a church liturgy, here it refers to the broader way a believer serves and honors God in spirit and truth.</note></term> of the New Testament primarily consists, and that one can by no means serve God blessedly and rightly without Christ, but only in Christ and through Christ. Indeed, that Christ, reigning in the Holy Spirit, is Himself the worker of all good in and through us, as it is written in Galatians 3:5: namely, that Christ provides the Spirit and works powerful deeds within the believers. We should faithfully serve Him in this with the application of all our strength to His praise, just as we then also cooperate in His grace. Christ is the faithful treasure that Christians hold in their thin, weak vessels, so that the exceeding greatness of the power may be of God and not from us, 2 Corinthians 4:7.

Thus, through the knowledge of Christ, we can learn to ever more rightly understand the great riches of God's grace, as well as everything said previously regarding the Christian life, good works, and their promised reward. We can truly recognize in Christ that with all our own righteousness, works, piety, and with all that we otherwise consider to be service to God without Christ, we would utterly perish. He is our righteousness, our wisdom, sanctification, and redemption, so that as it is written: "He who boasts, let him boast in the Lord," 1 Corinthians 1:31.

Then the Lord Jesus Christ, the dear and precious guest and inhabitant of all Christian hearts (if He is indeed rightly known), first becomes truly beloved and desirable to us. His redemption becomes sweet, pleasant, lovely, and comforting in all the <term>temptations<note>original: "Anfechtung." A significant term in German theology referring to spiritual trials, deep inner struggles, or the feeling of being overwhelmed by sin and doubt.</note></term> of sin. From this we also rightly learn to recognize the kingdom of Christ and the office of His grace within us—indeed, how He daily refreshes, feeds, waters, and nourishes our souls with Himself in the Holy Spirit, and how we are ever more <term>incorporated<note>original: "eingeleibet," literally "in-bodied." A mystical term for the believer becoming a living part of the Body of Christ.</note></term> into Him.

Also, apart from the knowledge of Christ and what was stated at the beginning, all God-fearing people may also more thoroughly understand the matter of <term>justification<note>original: "Justification." The theological process or act by which a person is made or declared righteous before God.</note></term>—when or how we become righteous, holy, and pious before God. This does not happen if we merely throw ourselves upon Christ with a <term>historical faith<note>original: "historischer Glaube." In this context, it refers to an intellectual belief in the facts of the Bible or history without an accompanying spiritual transformation or personal relationship with God.</note></term>...

These three passages I wish to leave for further consideration by those who have received grace and rightly know the Lord Christ.

Briefly and finally, the <term>simple ones<note>original: "Einfältigen." This refers to ordinary, humble believers who may lack formal education but possess sincere faith.</note></term> should note and know, as a summary of all this, that everything that comes to us from God or is given to us is not from our merit, work, or strength as a matter of obligatory duty; rather, it is all mercy, all pure grace, compassion, and the gift of God. For this reason, we should praise and thank God much more than our flesh should boast of itself in pride...

...praise God or exalt [Him] in any other way. What do you have, says Paul, that you did not receive? Now if you did receive it, why do you boast as if you had not received it? <note>This is a reference to 1 Corinthians 4:7.</note> Therefore, no one should boast of their works, nor of their merit, wealth, wisdom, or strength; instead, whoever wishes to boast should boast only in the Lord Christ, His grace, and His merit. Furthermore, through Christ and in Christ, one should boast in God and all His goodness, love, mercy, and benevolence (to Him alone for eternal praise) in all humility and <term>equanimity<note>original: "Gelassenheit." In the context of German mysticism and Pietism, this refers to a state of surrendered calmness and letting go of self-will to rely entirely on God.</note></term>, and in the fact that they know Christ and God in Christ. This alone pleases God the Lord. Jeremiah 9:24.

# Chapter 47.

### Conclusion regarding various errors concerning good works. Also concerning free will.

Since it has now been stated and demonstrated with the testimony of Holy Scripture what a Christian, godly life is, what genuine good works and true <term>divine service<note>original: "Gottesdienst." While often used for a church service, the author uses it here to mean the internal and external ways a believer serves and honors God.</note></term> are, and that without the Lord Christ and His grace, nothing in all flesh is good before God, nor pleasing or meritorious for salvation—from which the opposite and the false teachings of those who err regarding divine service and good works may easily be recognized—I will nevertheless recount some errors regarding works, repeating several previous points, in a summary for further explanation, and then conclude with them.

### The difference between human laws and the commandments of God.

The first error is when one does not only fail to distinguish between the good works commanded by God and the traditions or ordinances of men, but holds human law, ceremony, or the works of church ordinances higher than God’s commandments; insisting on them more forcefully and thereby entangling and burdening consciences against God’s Word, often creating sin where there is no sin before God. And when the transgression of human laws is punished much more severely than the transgression of God’s commandments, it is truly a grave error and a corruption of consciences, as well as an obscuring of God’s commandments and the genuine good works of <term>repentance<note>original: "Buße." This term refers to both the internal "change of mind" and the outward acts that demonstrate a return to God.</note></term>. Against this, the Lord Christ Himself taught in the Gospel from the prophet Isaiah, in Matthew 15.

# Against Trusting in Works and One’s Own Merit.

The second error regarding good works is when one seeks to make <term>satisfaction<note>original: "genug thun." In a theological context, this refers to the idea of performing penance or deeds to "satisfy" or pay off the debt of sin before God's justice.</note></term> for sin before God’s justice through works of righteousness. This leads a person to believe they can become righteous before God through their own deeds. Alternatively, it occurs when one builds upon works and places their trust for salvation in ceremonies or rituals, as if God were required to forgive our sins and grant us the kingdom of heaven for the sake of our efforts or merit. This error is contrary to the mercy and grace of God, and contrary to Christ and the merit of His suffering. Paul refutes this in his letters to the Romans and Galatians, and also to the Ephesians, where he says: **"For by grace you have been saved through faith, and that not of yourselves; it is the gift of God, not of works, lest anyone should boast," etc. Ephesians 2:8, 9. And Romans 11:6: "If it is by grace, then it is no longer of works."** Likewise: **"Not by works of righteousness which we have done, but according to His mercy He saved us," Titus 3:5.** In all these passages, St. Paul is not rejecting the good works that God has commanded, but rather the *trust* in those works—relying on our own merit, righteousness, and personal worthiness.

Such an error arises because God, in Holy Scripture, promises reward, life, and eternal salvation to the works of faith performed by justified people. Because of this, some imagine that He has promised such grace to the mere works themselves, regardless of the spirit in which they are done. Such people also falsely believe that hope arises from mere works, whereas it actually springs from promised grace and from faith in Jesus Christ. Thus, they devote themselves to outward deeds or ceremonies to wipe away sin and acquire salvation, while in the meantime forgetting Christ, our only Savior, and His grace. Consequently, they exalt themselves because of their works, as if they were accumulating great merit and no longer needed God's mercy. In this way, some monks have even sold not only their own "surplus" merits but the merits of all the saints to dying people. <note>This refers to the medieval "Treasury of Merit," a belief that the extra good deeds of saints could be distributed to others, often via the sale of indulgences.</note> These are the true <term>Pharisees<note>A reference to the biblical group often criticized for focusing on the "letter of the law" and outward displays of piety rather than the heart.</note></term>, who perform all their works out of their own strength and in the pride of their minds, desiring only to be praised, honored, and seen by men. They also look down upon, exclude, and condemn others as if they were better than them, an example of which we see in the Pharisee in the temple (Luke 18), which the Lord also rejected in Matthew 6.

Therefore, this distinction regarding the truth should be well noted: that it is not good works themselves, but rather the trust in the

A key term in German mystical and Pietist thought, referring to a state of total surrender to God’s will and the letting go of selfish desires.</note></term>, and in the fact that they know Christ and God in Christ. Such things alone please God the Lord. Jeremiah 9:24.

# Chapter 47.

### Conclusion regarding various errors concerning good works. Also concerning free will.

Now that it has been stated, and demonstrated with the testimony of Holy Scripture, what a Christian godly life is, what righteous good works and true <term>divine service<note>original: "Gottesdienst." While often translated as "church service" or "worship," here it refers to the broader concept of serving God through one's life and actions.</note></term> are, and that without the Lord Christ and His grace, no flesh is good before God, nor is anything pleasant or meritorious for salvation. From this, the opposite—the false teaching of those who err regarding divine service and good works—can easily be recognized. Therefore, I will briefly recount several errors regarding works, repeating certain previous points for greater clarity, and then conclude.

### The difference between human laws and God's commandments.

The first error is when one not only fails to distinguish between the good works that God has commanded and the traditions or ordinances of men, but even holds human law, ceremony, or the works of church order higher than God’s commandments. Such a person insists upon these more urgently, entangling and burdening consciences against God's Word, often creating a "sin" where there is no sin before God. And when one punishes the transgression of human laws much more severely than the transgression of God’s commandments, it is a grave error and a corruption of the conscience, just as it is an obscuring of God’s commandments and the righteous good works of <term>repentance<note>original: "Buße." This term carries the weight of both internal change of heart and the outward fruits of that change.</note></term>. Against this, the Lord Christ Himself taught in the Gospel, citing the prophet Isaiah in Matthew 15.

## Against Relying on Works and Personal Merit.

The second error regarding good works is when one seeks to <term>satisfy<note>original: "genug thun." This refers to the theological concept of "satisfaction"—the idea of making amends or paying a debt for sin.</note></term> God’s justice for sin through works of righteousness. This happens when a person believes they can be made righteous before God by their own deeds, or when they build upon their works and place their trust for salvation in ceremonies or deeds, as if God were required to forgive our sins and grant us the kingdom of heaven for the sake of our works or merit. This error stands against the mercy and grace of God, against Christ, and against the merit of His suffering. Indeed, Paul refutes this in his letters to the Romans and the Galatians, and also to the Ephesians, where he says: “For by grace you have been saved through faith, and that not of yourselves; it is the gift of God, not of works, lest anyone should boast.” <note>Ephesians 2:8–9</note> And in Romans 11:6: “If salvation is from works, then it is no longer grace.” Likewise: “Not by works of righteousness which we have done, but according to His mercy He saved us.” <note>Titus 3:5</note> In all these passages, St. Paul is not rejecting the good works that God has commanded, but rather the *reliance* on those works—the trust in our own merit, righteousness, and personal worthiness.

Such an error stems from the fact that in Holy Scripture, God promises rewards, life, and eternal salvation to the works of faith and to the person who has been <term>justified<note>original: "gerechtfertigten." To be declared righteous or "in the right" before God, a central theme of the Reformation.</note></term>. Because of this, some believe that He promised such grace to the outward works themselves, regardless of the spirit in which they are done. Such people imagine that hope arises from mere works, even though it actually comes from promised grace and from faith in Jesus Christ. Thus, they devote themselves to outward works or ceremonies to wipe away sin and acquire salvation; in the process, they forget Christ, our only Savior, and His grace. Consequently, they puff themselves up because of their works, as if they were accumulating great merit and no longer needed God’s mercy.

This is how certain monks, out of their supposed "abundance," sold not only their own merits but the merits of all the saints to dying people. <note>This refers to the medieval practice of selling indulgences or "works of supererogation," where the extra good deeds of saints were thought to be transferable to others.</note> These are the true Pharisees, who perform all their works—whatever they may be—out of their own strength and in the pride of their minds, desiring to be praised, honored, and seen by men. They also look down upon, exclude, and condemn others as if they were better than them, an example of which we see in the Pharisee in the temple in Luke 18, which the Lord also rejected in Matthew 6.

Therefore, for the sake of the truth, this distinction should be well noted: that it is not the good works themselves, but the reliance on the...

May Almighty, merciful God grant that it may also come to pass for us poor people, that we may truly boast in Christ Jesus of a good, joyful, satisfied conscience and an irreproachable walk before God in His grace. Even if the world should scold us for it, or whoever wishes to—for in these times people are very reluctant to hear someone boast of Christ, the grace of God, and a good conscience therein. It is quickly said that such a person wants to be holier than other people, or wants to be a sinner no longer, etc. Indeed, it is now almost considered the greatest sin if one does not condemn themselves along with the world, and throw themselves in with the godless crowd, as if one could do nothing but sin.

## On the Free Will of Man.

The third error concerns free will in relation to good works, where one can once again stray to the right or to the left. This happens when one attributes too much to the will and ability of the "natural man" <note>The human condition as it exists by nature, apart from the influence of the Holy Spirit</note> and says: even now, after Adam's Fall, man possesses by nature a free will to do good works that please God for salvation <term>Seligkeit: the state of eternal blessedness or salvation</term>, such that he might merit the highest grace of justification <term>Gerechtwerdung: the process of being declared righteous before God</term> through morally good works by his own natural strength, and likewise abstain from sins.

On the other hand, error occurs if one believes the opposite, namely: that man—even a Christian believer—has no free will at all, neither toward good nor evil works, neither to be obedient to God nor to abstain from sins, but rather that all things happen out of forced, unavoidable necessity, according to the providence of God, who "works all in all" <note>original: 1 Corinthians 12:6</note>.

Now, however, this point regarding free will is to be distinguished according to the testimony of Holy Scripture as follows: namely, that the natural man indeed has a free will, but only in the doing or refraining from *external works*, and in those works which belong to this life, whether they be good or evil. Such works every human being can do or leave undone by the power of his own will, freely and without compulsion.

<note>Key terms from this section: Grace, Jesus Christ, free will, Adam's fall, salvation, justification, providence of God, Holy Scripture</note>

<summary>This page discusses the balance between divine grace and human action, concluding the section on works before defining free will. It argues that while humans have free will in worldly, external matters, spiritual goodness and salvation depend entirely on the grace of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>free will, grace, justification, Adam's Fall, synergism, natural man, external works, Jesus Christ</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "third error" concerning free will. It transitions from defining the limitations of the "natural man" to describing the restored freedom of the "reborn man" through divine grace, maintaining the distinction between earthly morality and spiritual salvation.</meta>

The will of the natural man <note>The human condition as it exists by nature, corrupted by sin, before spiritual rebirth</note>, with its freedom, power, and ability, extends no further than to an external human righteousness or piety. This is a level of conduct that God nonetheless demands from all people, and as heard previously, He rewards it here in this life according to His goodness, just as He punishes unrighteousness and all evil here and eternally according to His justice. 

However, because the human heart—indeed, his entire nature, soul, and conscience with all its powers—has been so corrupted, permeated, and brought to nothing through sin in Adam’s Fall, his will has also become entirely incapable of not only completing but even beginning truly good works that serve eternal life. Thus, in this spiritual sense, man has no free will toward good works, just as he is unable by his own natural strength, without grace, to change, lay aside, or dampen his sinful inclinations <term>Affecte: powerful internal emotions or impulses that drive a person toward sin</term> or internal evil desires before God, or even to recognize God and His kingdom. As it is written in John 15:5: *Without me you can do nothing.* And as stated in 1 Corinthians 2:14: *The natural man perceives nothing of the Spirit of God; it is all foolishness to him.* And Romans 8:7: *To be carnally minded is enmity against God, for it is not subject to the law of God, nor indeed can it be,* says Paul. Furthermore, Philippians 2:13: *For it is God who works in you, both to will and to do according to His good pleasure.*

However, after a person is brought back to grace through Christ, reborn, becomes a believer and a Christian, he then receives and possesses a new free will to do all kinds of good works that please God. This all flows from the merit, grace, and benefit of Christ our Lord, of whom the Lord Himself says in the Gospel (John 8:36): *If the Son makes you free, you shall be free indeed.* And Paul (2 Corinthians 3:17): *Where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty.* Likewise (Romans 8:2): *The law of the Spirit of life in Christ Jesus has made me free from the law of sin and death.*

Some speak of it in this way: that the will of the natural man is indeed fit for moral virtues; but it is powerless for eternal salvation <term>Seligkeit: the state of eternal blessedness or salvation</term> if grace through faith is not added to it.

Therefore, in the article concerning free will, we must also pay attention to the proper middle ground and rightly distinguish between nature and grace, as well as the old and new man with their two different types of will and ability, namely: although we have no free will according to the old man and by nature to keep God's commandments according to His pleasure, nor to do good meritorious works or to effect salvation, we nevertheless have

We should rightly hold to this, as long as we are true Christians and wish to take comfort in the redemption of Christ.

St. Paul also maintained such a distinction concerning free will quite elegantly and correctly, according to spiritual judgment, throughout all his teaching, but especially in 2 Corinthians 3:5. There, he speaks of the ability of the New and Old Man <note>Terms used by St. Paul to describe the two natures of a Christian: the "Old Man" is the sinful, worldly nature, while the "New Man" is the nature reborn through faith</note>, and says: *Such trust we have through the Lord Christ toward the Almighty God, not that we are sufficient of ourselves to think anything as being from ourselves, but all our sufficiency or ability is from the Almighty God.* Here we see how St. Paul does not discard or deny free will or human ability regarding good works as indiscreetly as some do today. As it is written in 2 Peter 1:3, through the knowledge of Him who has called us to His kingdom, all things of His divine power that serve for eternal life and a godly walk have been given to us.

From all of this, it is now clearly understood that it is a significant and harmful error when people teach or hold that Christians have no free will to do good, and that they cannot abstain from sin through the grace of God. What has followed from such harmful, evil teaching in times past is, unfortunately, only too evident today.

Luther himself also complains in his Postil <note>A collection of sermons or commentaries on the Gospels and Epistles, intended to be read in church or at home</note> that many people become much worse than they were before.

But although Christians, or new reborn people, possess a renewed free will, strength, and ability for all good and godly works out of the riches of God's grace, there nevertheless remains a strong innate inclination toward evil. ### That good works should be distinguished from sin.

The fourth error regarding good works is when one believes that all good works—even the work done in the very best way—are sin. Or that even the righteous man sins in all good works. Likewise, that all people sin at all times and in truth. This error is also caused when one does not distinguish between the works of the **Old Man** and the **New Man** <note>In Lutheran theology, the "Old Man" refers to the sinful human nature inherited from Adam, while the "New Man" is the nature reborn through faith in Christ</note>; just as when one takes little notice of the distinct roles of sin and grace within a person. This mentioned error—that the good works of the righteous are sins—can be quickly overturned if one considers that such works are nothing but gifts of God, indeed, the fruits of the Holy Spirit and the works of Christ Himself, who works all good and is in all things. How can the spiritual gifts of God, and what He works within us, be sin?

If even the good works of a righteous or new reborn person—such as praying, giving alms, praising God, believing in Christ, and suffering affliction for His name's sake—were to be sin and damnable, how could they please God? Indeed, how would the Lord be able to promise them the reward of eternal life in return? Or how could the believing New Man have a good conscience in his works? Or walk blamelessly before God, as the Scripture testifies?

The Lord Christ says of Mary Magdalene, when she anointed him: *She has done a good work for me.* <note>Matthew 26:10</note> Where was the sin there?

Saint John writes in his epistle and teaches: *Whoever is born of God does not sin, for his seed remains*... <note>1 John 3:9</note>

<summary>This page argues against the idea that even the good works of a believer are inherently sinful. It emphasizes that while Christians still struggle with their sinful nature, their actions performed through faith and the Holy Spirit are pleasing to God.</summary>
<keywords>Paul, sin, condemnation, righteousness, good works, error, Holy Spirit, Mary Magdalene, John, New Man, Old Man</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument against the fourth "error" regarding good works. The previous page ended while explaining that good works are the fruits of the Holy Spirit and the works of Christ Himself, who dwells in the believer.</meta>

...remains in him, and he cannot sin, because he is born of God <note>This is a reference to 1 John 3:9: "Whosoever is born of God doth not commit sin; for his seed remaineth in him: and he cannot sin, because he is born of God."</note>. Therefore, a doctrine that teaches the opposite—namely, that such a person born of God can do nothing but sin, and even that a person sins in the very act of loving God because they do not love Him with their whole heart—is entirely wrong. For although the **Old Man** <note>The "Old Man" refers to the fallen human nature prone to selfishness and sin.</note> cannot love God with his whole heart, the **New Man** <note>The "New Man" is the spiritual nature of a person redeemed by Christ.</note> is able to do so, in as much as he is renewed and remains in Christ, according to the measure of his faith. How else could Saint John have dared to say that we keep God’s commandments, and that His commandments are not burdensome? <note>1 John 5:3</note>

Since Saint Paul also writes that the love of God is poured into our hearts by the **Holy Spirit** (for the blotting out of sins), and since love, faith, and hope are themselves gifts of the Holy Spirit, it can be concluded that when the saints use such gifts for their salvation and offer them back to the Lord with gratitude, they are by no means sinning. The Holy Spirit is no more capable of pouring sin into a believing heart than these gifts are capable of being sinful.

Even though a person's heart, soul, and strength in this life—while we still walk in the flesh—are not so perfectly pure and upright before God as His judgment might strictly demand (for such perfection was found in only one man on earth, the Lord Jesus Christ), nevertheless, all believing Christians now enjoy full perfection. Through Him, the works they perform in His grace are good—indeed, perfect—and are praiseworthy and pleasing to God. In these works, sin can have no place or room, because they are performed in God.

->A completely new heart grows in newness before God toward full perfection.<-

It is indeed written that there is no one righteous on earth who does good and does not sin <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 7:20.</note>. However, it does not follow from this that the righteous person sins *within* those good works for which God cleanses and prepares them, so that they should walk in them. Titus 2:12; Ephesians 2:8.

Therefore, the aforementioned doctrine—that a Christian or a righteous person sins in all good works—is contrary to all Scripture and historical example. It is an evil and offensive doctrine that fundamentally allows no good work to remain "good," because sin and grace, evil and good, Belial <note>A term from the Hebrew Bible used to personify wickedness or the devil.</note> and Christ (who works good works in us through His Spirit just as Belial works sin) can in no way stand together. 2 Corinthians 6:15.

<term>Gott gebohren</term> (Born of God): A theological term for the spiritual rebirth of a person through faith.
<term>Heiliger Geist</term> (Holy Spirit): In this context, viewed as the source of all truly good actions in a believer.
<term>Vollkommenheit</term> (Perfection): Here, it refers to a state of grace granted through Christ rather than a person's own flawless performance.

<summary>This page argues against the idea that even the good deeds of a believer are sinful, asserting that works done through the Holy Spirit are truly good and pleasing to God. It emphasizes the distinction between the flawed "Old Man" and the redeemed "New Man" who acts through Christ's perfection.</summary>

<keywords>perfection, good works, Holy Spirit, New Man, Saint Paul, Saint John, righteousness, Belial, faith, love</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of good works, moving from the definition of the "New Man" to a specific defense against the idea that God's laws are an impossible burden for the believer.</meta>

### That God Has Not Commanded Impossible Things of the Believer.

The fifth error concerning good works is that, in times past, it has been taught and written without any distinction that God has commanded us impossible things, and that it is also impossible for the believer or the saint to keep God's commandment. This is, once again, a very unreasonable <note>The original German *unbescheidene* suggests a doctrine that is immoderate or lacks proper theological judgment.</note> doctrine, which takes away the office of grace and the diligence in fulfilling God's commandments, as well as all zeal for good works. For this reason, Saint Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD) was a Church Father who famously argued that calling God's commands impossible was a slight against God’s character.</note> writes, not without cause, that such a person is *anathema* <note>original: "Anathema" — a formal curse or decree of excommunication.</note> or banished who says that God has commanded us impossible things. Such a judgment is also contrary to Holy Scripture, as Paul writes to the Romans: that God sent His Son in the likeness of sinful flesh and condemned sin in the flesh through sin, so that the righteousness required by the law might be fulfilled in us, who walk not after the flesh but after the Spirit. Romans 8:1–4. And John in his epistle: If we ask, we shall receive from Him, for we keep His commandments and do what is pleasing before Him. 1 John 3:22 <note>The OCR cites 1 John 2:3–5, but the specific quote regarding receiving what we ask is found in 1 John 3:22.</note>.

Therefore (if one does not wish to err), this point should also be correctly distinguished as follows: Although God's commandments are entirely difficult and impossible for the **Old Man** <note>The "Old Man" refers to human nature in its fallen, unredeemed state.</note> to keep, they are nevertheless easy, possible, and lovely for the **New**, born-again, believing person. Mark 9:23 <note>The OCR lists Mark 9:22, but the quote "all things are possible to him that believeth" is verse 23.</note>—as the Lord Himself says: All things are possible for the believer. Matthew 11:30: And my yoke is easy, my burden is tolerable. 1 John 5:3: Likewise, John says His commandments are not burdensome. And Paul in Philippians 4:13: I can do all things through Him who strengthens me; that is, in Christ, who gave Himself for us, that He might redeem us from all unrighteousness and purify for Himself a people as His own possession, who would be zealous for good works. Titus 2:14.

The sixth error concerning good works is when no distinction of works is maintained, but instead the works of human necessity are compared to—or even placed above—the works of repentance; when it is taught and believed that natural household works, such as managing a home, governing, serving, and the like, are just as purely noble and good works as praying, fasting, and giving alms.

But when Saint Paul and the other apostles, as well as the Lord Christ Himself in the Gospel, speak of the reward for works, they do not teach of such household works, which even the heathens...

<summary>This page refutes the idea that God's commandments are impossible to follow, arguing that through the "New Man" and the strength of Christ, obedience is possible. It also begins to distinguish between common "household works" and spiritual "works of repentance."</summary>
<keywords>Good works, commandments, Saint Jerome, Romans 8, New Man, Old Man, repentance, household works, grace, zeal</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of good works, specifically distinguishing between mundane physical tasks and the spiritual "fruits" produced by the Holy Spirit. It then pivots to refute the idea that God is the author of sin or that human choice is irrelevant due to predestination.</meta>

man <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "...impossible for the Old Man."</note> are common, but rather concern the works of repentance and godliness <term>Godliness (Gottseligkeit): A state of living in a way that is pleasing to God, often used in this era to describe active piety.</term> which the Spirit of God works in us, which Saint Paul in Galatians 5:16 and Ephesians 5:9 calls the fruits of the Spirit. By these a Christian is recognized and distinguished from other people, as the Lord speaks to His disciples: **"So let your light shine before the people, that they may see your good works and praise your Father in heaven."** And regarding the eight Beatitudes <note>The "Eight Beatitudes" are the blessings listed by Jesus in the Sermon on the Mount (Matthew 5).</note> there in Matthew 5, verses 3–10. It is clear that He and His apostles are concerned with something other than washing dishes and sweeping the house. <note>This is a common Reformation-era argument: that "good works" are not just external, trivial chores or monastic rituals, but internal spiritual transformations.</note> Likewise, Paul in Romans 2:6–7 means many other works than such natural works when he says: **"That God will give to everyone according to his works, namely praise and honor and the imperishable being, to those who with patience in good works strive for eternal life, etc."**

### That God is not an author of sins, but of everything good.

The seventh error regarding good works has entered from pagan philosophy, where it is taught and held that God works both the good and the evil deeds in man; as if Judas's betrayal was no less God's own work than the calling of Paul.

Item, <note>Latin "Item" used here as "Likewise" or "Furthermore."</note> That man is moved, driven, indeed forced by God even toward evil, sinful works.

Item, it is not in man's power to commit sin; that man has no free will even in evil, but that all things must happen out of unavoidable necessity. All of these are grave, powerful errors against the honor, nature, and glory of the blessed, righteous God, as well as against the public, clear testimonies of Holy Scripture. Through this, not only are good works and their reward obscured and true godliness abolished, but also all godlessness is introduced.

### Error regarding Providence.

Regarding Providence (which has been treated entirely apart from Christ), people have also taught quite incautiously and destructively, saying that a person is destined to be either saved or damned regardless of whether they do or omit evil or good in their life. If he is destined for salvation, no sin can harm him. But if he is destined for damnation, no good work can help him.

<summary>This page argues that good works are spiritual fruits rather than mundane tasks and strongly refutes the "pagan" idea that God causes evil or that human will is entirely forced by necessity. It warns against a view of predestination that makes moral behavior irrelevant to salvation.</summary>
<keywords>good works, fruits of the Spirit, free will, providence, predestination, godliness, Saint Paul, Beatitudes</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against the "seventh error"—the philosophical idea that God is the cause of both good and evil actions. It completes the thought that if all actions are divinely ordained, then human effort becomes meaningless.</meta>

...let him do exactly as he will, it will still go as God has determined and ordered, all of which is meant to be called "God's will" without distinction.

The cause of these grave errors is human presumption and philosophy, by which one is led away from the **Apostolic simplicity** <term>Apostolic simplicity: A reference to a straightforward, Christ-centered faith, contrasted with the complex "scholastic" or "pagan" philosophy that the author believes complicates the Gospel.</term> that is in Christ. Consequently, one pays far too little attention to or exerts too little effort for Christ and His kingdom, or for **repentance** <term>Repentance (Buße): The process of turning away from sin and seeking reconciliation with God.</term> and rebirth in Christ. One also confuses the revealed will of God with His secret, unsearchable will, and maintains no spiritual discernment in such matters.

However, it is certain among all God-fearing people who know the Lord that God neither produces sin nor drives or compels one toward it. Rather, the devil drives and works sin and all evil in the children of unbelief, as Paul says in Ephesians 2:2. Concerning this, it is also written in Psalm 5:5: **"For You are not a God who is pleased with godless behavior; he who is evil does not remain before You."** And in 1 John 2:1–2: **"Christ appeared so that He might take away our sin"** (not so that He would first work or cause it). **"And in Him is no sin,"** says John. Therefore, it is false, erroneous, and wrong when one teaches or says that God performs both the good and the bad works (that is, sin as well) in man—yes, even that Judas's betrayal, as well as David's adultery <note>The author uses the biblical examples of Judas (who betrayed Jesus) and King David (who committed adultery with Bathsheba) to argue that these sins must be blamed on the individual and the devil, not on God's divine plan.</note>, is God's own work, and that all things must happen out of unavoidable necessity. But this is actually to blaspheme God; indeed, it is to link Him with Satan and to mix the fruits of the Holy Spirit with the works of the evil spirit—the very things Christ came to dissolve, and for which reason He became man. They also write that no work is so evil that it could condemn a man.

The **common man** <note>The "common man" (*der gemeine Mann*) refers to the general public or laypeople, who the author fears are being led into moral laziness by these complex theological debates.</note> quickly grasped such harmful teaching, which immediately caused him to doubt whether he should turn to repentance, refrain from evil, do good works, and live differently, or even fear God's judgment. As a result, he has become strayed, as is still seen today. May Almighty God graciously have mercy on us and improve everything that is corrupted in works or faith; may He make all crooked things straight and all erroneous things right through the appearance of the Spirit of Christ our Lord.

However, what has been taught, preached, and written about good works in past times in many places—quite indiscreetly and without spirit or judgment—has unfortunately been clearly seen in the consequences and fruits, and where such teaching has led. Would it not be better if we asked God the Lord for forgiveness, humbled ourselves under Him, and recanted such and similar erro-

<summary>The author argues that attributing human sin to God's will is a blasphemous error derived from philosophy. This teaching is condemned for causing moral apathy among the public and confusing God's character with that of the devil.</summary>
<keywords>predestination, free will, blasphemy, repentance, Judas Iscariot, King David, common man, spiritual discernment</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues a critique of contemporary theological teachings that, in the author's view, discourage believers from performing good works. The previous page ended with a discussion of biblical figures like Judas and David, likely leading into a discussion of human frailty or the consequences of sin.</meta>

...misery? <note>The text begins with the fragment "mer?", likely the end of the word "Jammer" (misery/woe) or a similar term continuing from the previous page.</note> Even **Saint Augustine** was not ashamed of this before the whole world; he did not offer excuses, gloss over the matter, or claim that he had neither done nor taught anything wrong or blameworthy.

But no matter how one tries to excuse it, it remains undeniable that regarding **merit** <term>Merit (Verdienst): The theological concept of whether human actions have value in God's eyes or deserve reward.</term> and the **reward of works**, as the Holy Scripture points out, this Gospel <note>The author is likely referring to the specific Lutheran or Reformist preaching of his time rather than the four Gospels of the Bible.</note> has not only failed to teach these things or use them to stir up a zeal for works, but has instead frequently and violently stormed against them. Indeed, it has even denied the **free will** to do good without any distinction. Because of this, people have become unenthusiastic, sluggish, cold, lazy, and negligent toward all good works.

And what wonder would it be, when it is taught that even a righteous person sins in all good works? Some might then say: we wish to remain uninvolved with good works, so that we do not sin or provoke God to anger.

Recently, it has been taught that faith cannot tolerate works or merit alongside it. Yet true faith should take hold not only of the promise of redemption through Christ, but also of the promise regarding the works, merit, or reward of the redeemed Christian. Of this promise **Paul** also writes (as mentioned before) that **godliness** <term>Godliness (Gottseligkeit): A lifestyle of Christian piety and the fear of God.</term>—which is the fear of God and a Christian, godly life, or as **Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 339–397 AD), an influential Bishop of Milan.</note> interprets it, "to practice works of mercy and to be kind and gentle toward one's brother"—is useful for all things and holds the promise of both this life and the life to come. If we are to believe the one—namely, that God has redeemed us purely for nothing out of grace through Christ—why should we not also believe the other: that for His sake, out of pure grace, He wishes to reward our good works (God grant that one might do many of them) both here and in the hereafter?

**Saint Augustine** says in one place that grace produces merit in us, and to this merit eternal life is given as a gift. And when God crowns or rewards our merit, He crowns and rewards nothing other than His own gifts, as it stands in the Psalm: <note>original: "Deine Güte oder Barmherzigkeit wird mir zuvorkommen, und deine Barmherzigkeit wird mir nachfolgen." A reference to Psalm 23:6 and Psalm 59:10.</note> **"Your goodness or mercy shall go before me, and Your mercy shall follow me."** This is what Augustine says, which may also serve us here.

Therefore, it is an indiscreet and offensive doctrine to teach that faith cannot tolerate any good work or merit alongside it, just as it is to reject the promise of reward or merit for Christians. Instead, one should teach that Christians receive everything that God gives them—both the...

<summary>The author argues that contemporary teaching has wrongly suppressed the importance of good works and divine rewards, leading to spiritual lethargy. Citing Augustine and Ambrose, he maintains that while salvation is by grace, God graciously chooses to reward the good deeds he works within the believer.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Augustine, Saint Ambrose, merit, reward of works, free will, godliness, grace, faith vs works, piety</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's treatise on good works. It follows a discussion on the relationship between faith and the promise of reward for the actions of a believer.</meta>

...that which God <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of what Paul writes regarding the promise of reward.</note> has promised for the sake of redemption and good works; they should believe firmly, and also hope comfortingly and without wavering for such an eternal reward.

In short, I say that we must constantly believe that the kind and merciful God will leave no good work unrewarded; if we do so, we will apply ourselves all the more to good works, and yet, in doing so, give praise and honor only to God the Lord, from whom all good things come.

Thus, the Christian reader now has instruction not only on how he may conduct himself in a Christian manner regarding true worship, the Christian life, and good works, but also what he should flee and avoid therein, and how he may walk in Christ and through His grace upon the right **royal road** <term>Royal Road (Königliche Straße): A metaphorical path of moderation or the "middle way" that avoids theological extremes—in this case, avoiding both the rejection of works and the prideful reliance upon them.</term> to the kingdom of heaven. With this, I wish to conclude this little book for the praise of God and for the service of those who have gone astray regarding good works. This was completed and written during a sorrowful, grievous time of heavy warfare <note>Likely referring to the Schmalkaldic War (1546–1547) or the ongoing religious conflicts in Germany during the 16th century.</note> under the manifold punishment of God. It is my hope that someone might hereafter be moved by it toward **repentance** <term>Repentance (Buße): In this context, it implies a turning away from sin and a restructuring of one's life toward God.</term> and the improvement of their life, as well as toward the right understanding and practice of all good works, to escape the future wrath of God and attain eternal life through Christ. I also know, praise God, that it shall not be found inconsistent with the sound, pure doctrine of faith and the apostolic Christian church.

May the Lord Jesus Christ, in whose name it is written, make all of this clear and understandable to the kind-hearted reader through the preceding light of His illumination, and also instill it in their hearts to stimulate love, zeal, and practice; may He bless it and allow it to serve everyone who reads it well for the salvation of their soul. Amen, Amen.

### Caspar Schwenckfeldt.

<meta>This page serves as an errata list (a record of printing errors and their corrections) for the second part of the volume, titled "Of the Threefold Life of Man." It follows the index provided on the previous page, ensuring that the reader has the most accurate version of the theological text before them.</meta>

## Printing Errors in the Second Part,
### Or
## Of the Threefold Life of Man.

Page 18 in the 4th Paragraph, 4th line, read **him**—instead of **her**.
Page 22 in the 1st Paragraph, 14th line, **our** [unser]—instead of **our** [unserm]. <note>The correction adjusts the grammatical case of the possessive pronoun "our."</note>
Page 24 in the 2nd Paragraph, 5th line, **as a child**.
Page 25 in the 2nd Paragraph, 1st line, **to the Galatians**.
Page 26 in the 3rd Par. 4th line, **impenitent**—instead of **penitent**.
Page 27 in the 2nd Paragraph, 1st line, **heavenly Father thus**.
Page 29 in the 2nd Paragraph, 2nd line, **from** [aus]—instead of **outside of** [außer]. <note>In spiritual texts of this era, the distinction between "from" within and "outside" of a person was a vital theological point regarding where grace originates.</note>
Page 35 in the 1st Paragraph, 8th line, **before him**—instead of **for him**.
Page 40 in the 1st Paragraph, 2nd line, **as also the**—instead of **as the**.
Page 48 in the 4th Paragraph, 2nd line, **thou wilt**—instead of **so thou wilt**.
Page 50 in the heading of the 25th Chap., **recounted** [erzählten]—instead of **aimed for** [erzielten].
Page 52 in the 3rd Paragraph, 2nd line, **previously recounted**—instead of **previously aimed for**.
Page 52 in the 4th Paragraph, 1st line, **recounts**—instead of **aims for**.
Page 53 in the 5th Paragraph, 2nd line, **unrighteous**—instead of **righteous**.
Page 55 in the 1st Paragraph, 2nd line, **previously recounted**—instead of **previously aimed for**.
Page 61 in the 3rd Par. 11th line, **good works is**—instead of **reason is**. <note>This correction highlights the author's focus on active piety over mere intellectual "reason."</note>
Page 69 in the 4th Paragraph, 11th line, **the** [dem]—instead of **the** [den].
Page 71 in the 1st Par. 11th line, **pleasant** [angenehme]—instead of **accepted** [angenommene].
Page 81, 10th line, read: **are**—instead of **enemy**.
Page 83 in the 2nd Par. 2nd line, **Samaritan**—instead of **Canaanite**.
Page 87 in the 2nd Par. 9th line, **dangerous**—instead of **solemn**.
Page 88 in the 2nd Paragraph, 9th line, **precious** [theurer]—instead of **faithful** [treuer].
Page 91 in the 3rd Paragraph, 1st line, **turn** [kehre]—instead of **know** [kenne].
Page 94 in the 2nd Paragraph, 12th line, **the**—instead of **also the**.
Page 102 in the 2nd Par. 2nd line, **Apocalypse**—instead of **Acts of the Apostles**. <note>The "Apocalypse" is another name for the Book of Revelation.</note>
Page 104 in the 3rd Paragraph, 3rd line, read: **Hand has**.
Page 108 in the 2nd Paragraph, 13th line, actually more the <term>etc.</term> <note>original: "ic." — likely an abbreviation for "etcetera."</note>
Page 114 in the 2nd Paragraph, 11th line, **precious**—instead of **faithful**.
Page 114 in the 2nd Paragraph, 12th line, **earthly**—instead of **thin**.
Page 114 in the 5th Par. 1st line, read twice: **from**—instead of **outside of**.
Page 115 in the 3rd Paragraph, 4th line, **Lord** [Herren]—instead of **Heart** [Herzen]. <note>In the Fraktur typeface used in these manuscripts, the words for "Lord" and "Heart" look very similar, leading to frequent errors.</note>
Page 115 in the 3rd Paragraph, 7th line, **would be**—instead of **will be**.
Page 122 in the 2nd Par. 14th line, **what Christ in and through us**.
Page 123 in the 3rd Paragraph, 3rd line, **throughout**—instead of **from it**.
Page 123 in the 3rd Paragraph, 6th line, **of Christ** [Christi]—instead of **Christians**.
Page 125 in the 1st Paragraph, 5th line, **your**—instead of **our**.
Page 125 in the 1st Paragraph, 1st line, **to**—instead of **from**.

<summary>This page lists several dozen corrections for the second part of the book, focusing on grammatical errors, word substitutions, and scriptural citations. These corrections ensure the reader correctly understands the author's points on topics like the difference between "Lord" and "Heart" or "Samaritan" and "Canaanite."</summary>

<meta>This is the title page of a collection of letters by the 16th-century reformer Caspar Schwenkfeld, reprinted in America in the early 19th century.</meta>

### Several
### Christian and instructive
# Epistles.
<note>The original term "Send-Briefe" refers to formal, open letters intended for circulation among a wider audience of believers.</note>

### Written
### during the so-called Reformation period,
<term>Reformation period: A religious movement in 16th-century Europe that sought to reform the Roman Catholic Church and led to the creation of Protestantism.</term>
### around the year
### 1531
### by
## Caspar Schwenkfeld
## of Ossig.
<note>Caspar Schwenkfeld von Ossig (1489–1561) was a German nobleman and theologian who founded a spiritual movement distinct from both Lutheranism and Catholicism; his followers eventually settled in Pennsylvania.</note>

---

->Now once again<-
->on account of their instructive content,<-
->for the honor of God and the service of one’s neighbor<-
->committed to print<-
->by several admirers of the same.<-
<note>The "admirers" likely refers to the Schwenkfelder community in Pennsylvania who wished to preserve their founder's teachings.</note>

~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

### Allentown,
->printed by Heinrich Ebner.<-
<note>Heinrich Ebner was a prominent German-language printer in Allentown, Pennsylvania, serving the "Pennsylvania Dutch" community.</note>
### 1819.

<summary>This is the title page for a collection of religious letters written by the reformer Caspar Schwenkfeld in 1531, reprinted in 1819 by the Pennsylvania German community in Allentown.</summary>
